# Overview

When we study functions in mathematics, physics, and engineering, we quickly notice that many of the most critical tools occur naturally as function–inverse pairs. Just as subtraction undoes addition and division undoes multiplication, inverse functions allow us to reverse a mathematical process and solve for the input that produced a known output. Among the most fundamental of these pairs are the natural logarithm $\ln x$ and the exponential function $e^x$. Other essential families include the trigonometric functions (when restricted to appropriate intervals) and their inverses, as well as the hyperbolic functions and their inverses.

Why do we call these functions *transcendental*? Unlike algebraic functions, which can be built from polynomials and roots using basic arithmetic operations, transcendental functions "transcend" algebra. Yet they are indispensable for describing the physical world. They provide the exact mathematical models for phenomena as diverse as the mechanics of hanging cables (catenaries), the diffusion of heat flow through a solid, radioactive decay, and the atmospheric drag experienced by objects falling through fluids.

In our systematic calculus development, how do we build these functions from the ground up on a rock-solid foundation? Rather than relying on hand-wavy definitions of irrational exponents, we begin by examining the general theory of inverse functions and their derivatives. We then define the natural logarithm $\ln x$ directly via integration, and from that single integral definition, we systematically construct $e^x$ and all remaining transcendental functions.

---

# 6.1 Inverse Functions and Their Derivatives

## One-to-One Functions

A function $f$ is a rule that assigns a single output from its range to each input in its domain. Some functions assign the same output to several different inputs—for instance, $f(x) = x^2$ sends both $-2$ and $2$ to $4$, while $f(x) = \sin x$ sends infinitely many angles to $0$. Other functions, however, never repeat an output: different inputs always produce different outputs. When a function has this non-repeating property, we say it is **one-to-one** (or *injective*).

Why do we care whether a function repeats its outputs? Suppose you know that $f(x) = 4$ and you want to recover the exact input $x$ that produced it. If $f(x) = x^2$, you are stuck with an ambiguity: was the input $2$ or $-2$? Without a unique input corresponding to each output, we cannot uniquely reverse the function. Having a one-to-one function eliminates this ambiguity completely.

> [!abstract] Definition: One-to-One Function
> A function $f(x)$ is **one-to-one** on a domain $D$ if
> $$
> f(x_1) \neq f(x_2) \quad \text{whenever } x_1 \neq x_2
> $$
> Equivalently, in contrapositive form:
> $$
> f(x_1) = f(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2
> $$

In plain English, the direct definition says that distinct inputs are guaranteed to land on distinct outputs. The contrapositive form gives us our go-to algebraic strategy for proving a function is one-to-one: if two inputs $x_1$ and $x_2$ happen to yield the exact same output $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$, then those two inputs must have been the exact same number ($x_1 = x_2$) all along.

### The Horizontal Line Test

How can we tell at a glance from a function's Cartesian graph whether it is one-to-one? Since outputs are plotted as vertical heights $y$, a repeated output means the graph reaches the same height $y = c$ at two or more different horizontal positions $x$. This geometric observation gives us a visual counterpart to the vertical line test.

> [!theorem] The Horizontal Line Test
> A function $y = f(x)$ is one-to-one if and only if its graph intersects each horizontal line at most once.

To see why this works, imagine drawing a horizontal line $y = c$ across the coordinate plane. If that line intersects the graph of $f$ at two or more distinct points, say $(x_1, c)$ and $(x_2, c)$, then both $x_1$ and $x_2$ share the exact same output height: $f(x_1) = f(x_2) = c$ despite $x_1 \neq x_2$. That immediately violates the definition of a one-to-one function. For example, $y = x^3$ and $y = \sqrt{x}$ pass the horizontal line test across their entire domains because no horizontal line cuts their graphs more than once, whereas $y = x^2$ and $y = \sin x$ fail because horizontal lines slice through them multiple times.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927105517.webp)

## Inverses

Because each output of a one-to-one function originates from one—and only one—input, we can reverse the assignment rule entirely. Instead of feeding in $a$ to get $b$, we can build a reverse machine that takes the output $b$ and sends it right back to the unique input $a$ that produced it. This reversed function is called the **inverse** of $f$.

### Formal Definition and Domain–Range Relations

> [!abstract] Definition: Inverse Function
> Let $f$ be a one-to-one function with domain $\operatorname{Dom}(f)$ and range $\operatorname{Ran}(f)$. The **inverse function** $f^{-1}$, read "$f$ inverse," is defined by the rule
> $$
> f^{-1}(b) = a \iff f(a) = b
> $$
> for every $b \in \operatorname{Ran}(f)$.
> 
> Because $f^{-1}$ swaps the roles of inputs and outputs, the domain and range of $f$ and $f^{-1}$ are interchanged:
> - $\operatorname{Dom}(f^{-1}) = \operatorname{Ran}(f)$
> - $\operatorname{Ran}(f^{-1}) = \operatorname{Dom}(f)$

In other words, whatever set of numbers comes *out* of $f$ becomes the valid set of inputs you can feed *into* $f^{-1}$, and the values that come *out* of $f^{-1}$ are the original inputs from the domain of $f$.

> [!warning] Notational Caution: Functional Inversion vs. Reciprocal
> Be careful with the superscript "$-1$" in $f^{-1}(x)$! In function notation, this symbol denotes **functional inversion** (reversing the mapping), **not** an algebraic exponent of $-1$. That is, $f^{-1}(x)$ is never the reciprocal $\frac{1}{f(x)}$:
> $$
> f^{-1}(x) \neq \frac{1}{f(x)} = (f(x))^{-1}
> $$
> If we want to write the reciprocal of $f(x)$ using an exponent, we wrap the entire expression in parentheses as $(f(x))^{-1}$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927105632.webp)

### Composition and Identity Characterization

What happens if we apply $f$ to a number $x$, and then immediately apply $f^{-1}$ to the result? Since $f$ sends $x$ to $f(x)$, and $f^{-1}$ sends $f(x)$ right back to the input that produced it, we end up back where we started at $x$. The same thing happens if we apply $f^{-1}$ first and $f$ second. A function whose output is always identical to its input is called the **identity function**. Thus, composing a function and its inverse in either order cancels both operations out, leaving the identity function on their respective domains.

> [!theorem] Inverse Composition Rule
> Functions $f$ and $g$ form an inverse pair ($g = f^{-1}$ and $f = g^{-1}$) if and only if both of the following composition identities hold:
> $$
> f(g(x)) = x \quad \text{for all } x \in \operatorname{Dom}(g)
> $$
> and
> $$
> g(f(x)) = x \quad \text{for all } x \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)
> $$

This theorem gives us a powerful algebraic test: to verify that two given formulas $f$ and $g$ are inverses of each other, we simply compute both composite functions $(f \circ g)(x)$ and $(g \circ f)(x)$ and check that both simplify cleanly to $x$.

### Existence Criteria (Connection to Monotonicity and the MVT)

We know that a function possesses an inverse if and only if it is one-to-one. In practice, however, checking $f(x_1) = f(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$ algebraically can be difficult for complicated functions. How can calculus help us guarantee that a function is one-to-one without solving equations?

Think about how a function moves as $x$ increases from left to right. If a function is always climbing (**strictly increasing**), it can never loop back down to hit a previous height. Specifically, a strictly increasing function satisfies $x_1 < x_2 \implies f(x_1) < f(x_2)$, so two distinct inputs can never share the same output. Similarly, a **strictly decreasing** function satisfies $x_1 < x_2 \implies f(x_1) > f(x_2)$, so it also never repeats an output. In short, any strictly monotonic function on an interval is automatically one-to-one!

By combining this observation with **Corollary 3 of the Mean Value Theorem**, we obtain a simple derivative test for invertibility:
1. If $f'(x) > 0$ for all $x$ in an interval $I$, then $f$ is strictly increasing on $I$, and therefore $f^{-1}$ exists on $f(I)$.
2. If $f'(x) < 0$ for all $x$ in an interval $I$, then $f$ is strictly decreasing on $I$, and therefore $f^{-1}$ exists on $f(I)$.

## Finding and Graphing Inverses

### Geometric Symmetry Across the Line $y = x$

Suppose we have already plotted the graph of a one-to-one function $y = f(x)$. How does the graph of its inverse $y = f^{-1}(x)$ relate to it geometrically?

Think about what happens to individual coordinates. If a point $(a, b)$ lies on the graph of $f$, that means $b = f(a)$. By the definition of the inverse, this is equivalent to saying $a = f^{-1}(b)$. When we plot $f^{-1}$ using $x$ as the horizontal input axis and $y$ as the vertical output axis, the input $b$ produces the output $a$, which means the point $(b, a)$ lies on the graph of $y = f^{-1}(x)$.

Geometrically, swapping the first and second coordinates of a point—sending $(a, b)$ to $(b, a)$—is a reflection across the $45^\circ$ diagonal line $y = x$. Consequently, the graph of $f^{-1}$ is the exact mirror reflection of the graph of $f$ across the line $y = x$, and the line $y = x$ acts as an axis of symmetry between the two curves.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927105839.webp)

### Algebraic Procedure to Express $f^{-1}$ as a Function of $x$

When we write functions in calculus, we customarily use $x$ for the independent input variable and $y$ for the dependent output variable. To find an explicit formula for $f^{-1}(x)$ and graph it on the same coordinate axes as $f(x)$, we follow a standard two-step algebraic procedure:
1. **Solve for $x$ in terms of $y$:** Start with the equation $y = f(x)$ and manipulate it algebraically to isolate $x$ on one side, giving $x = f^{-1}(y)$. This step actually finds the inverse rule, expressing the original input $x$ as a function of the output $y$.
2. **Interchange $x$ and $y$:** Swap the symbols $x$ and $y$ throughout the equation to obtain $y = f^{-1}(x)$. This relabeling puts the inverse function back into conventional notation with $x$ as the independent variable, which also corresponds geometrically to reflecting the graph across the line $y = x$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927105935.webp)![](Pasted%20image%2020260927105947.webp)

### Domain Restrictions

What if a function we care about is *not* one-to-one across its entire natural domain? We don't have to give up on inverting it! Instead, we can restrict its domain to a smaller sub-interval on which the function *is* strictly monotonic (and therefore one-to-one).

For example, the squaring function $y = x^2$ fails the horizontal line test on $(-\infty, \infty)$ because every positive height $y$ is hit twice (at $\pm\sqrt{y}$). However, if we restrict the domain to nonnegative numbers $x \ge 0$, the restricted function $f(x) = x^2$ for $x \ge 0$ is strictly increasing and passes the horizontal line test. Solving $y = x^2$ for $x \ge 0$ gives $x = \sqrt{y}$, and swapping variables yields the inverse function $f^{-1}(x) = \sqrt{x}$ on the domain $x \ge 0$. (As we will see later in this chapter, this exact technique of domain restriction is how we define the inverse trigonometric functions $\sin^{-1} x$, $\cos^{-1} x$, and $\tan^{-1} x$.)

## Derivatives of Inverses of Differentiable Functions

Now let's bring calculus into the picture. If a one-to-one function $f$ is continuous and differentiable, is its inverse $f^{-1}$ also differentiable? And if so, how is the derivative of $f^{-1}$ related to the derivative of $f$?

### Reciprocal Slopes of Reflected Tangents

To build geometric intuition before stating the formal theorem, let's look at what reflection across the line $y = x$ does to the slope of a straight line. Consider a nonvertical, nonhorizontal line $L$ given by the equation $y = mx + b$ with slope $m \neq 0$. To reflect this line across $y = x$, we follow our two-step inversion procedure. First, we solve for $x$:
$$
x = \frac{1}{m}y - \frac{b}{m}
$$
Second, we interchange $x$ and $y$ to get the equation of the reflected line $L'$:
$$
y = \frac{1}{m}x - \frac{b}{m}
$$
Look at the coefficient of $x$ in the reflected line: its slope is $\frac{1}{m}$, the exact **reciprocal** of the original slope $m$!

Now extend this idea to smooth curves. The tangent line to the graph of $f^{-1}$ at the point $(f(a), a)$ is simply the mirror reflection across $y = x$ of the tangent line to the graph of $f$ at the point $(a, f(a))$. Because reflecting a line across $y = x$ takes the reciprocal of its slope, the slope of $f^{-1}$ at $x = f(a)$ must be the reciprocal of the slope of $f$ at $x = a$:
$$
\left. \frac{df^{-1}}{dx} \right|_{x = f(a)} = \frac{1}{\left. \frac{df}{dx} \right|_{x = a}}
$$
Notice carefully *where* each derivative is evaluated! We do not evaluate both derivatives at the same $x$-value. The derivative of $f^{-1}$ is evaluated at the output value $f(a)$, while the derivative of $f$ in the denominator is evaluated at the corresponding input value $a$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927110136.webp)

### The Derivative Rule for Inverses

Let's state this fundamental relationship as a rigorous theorem and see why the condition $f'(a) \neq 0$ is essential. Geometrically, if $f'(a) = 0$, the graph of $f$ has a horizontal tangent line at $(a, f(a))$. When you reflect a horizontal line across $y = x$, it becomes a *vertical* tangent line at $(f(a), a)$, where the slope is undefined and $f^{-1}$ fails to be differentiable. Avoiding zero derivatives prevents vertical tangents on the inverse graph.

> [!theorem] Theorem 1: The Derivative Rule for Inverses
> If $f$ is differentiable at every point of an interval $I$ and $\frac{df}{dx}$ is never zero on $I$, then $f$ is one-to-one and its inverse $f^{-1}$ is differentiable at every point of the interval $f(I)$. Furthermore, the value of $\frac{df^{-1}}{dx}$ at any particular point $f(a)$ is the reciprocal of the value of $\frac{df}{dx}$ at $a$:
> $$
> \left( \frac{df^{-1}}{dx} \right)_{x = f(a)} = \frac{1}{\left( \frac{df}{dx} \right)_{x = a}}
> $$
> In prime notation, this rule reads:
> $$
> (f^{-1})'(f(a)) = \frac{1}{f'(a)}
> $$
> Or, in concise operator notation:
> $$
> (f^{-1})' = \frac{1}{f'}
> $$

**Proof:**
The proof consists of two parts: first establishing that the inverse function $f^{-1}$ exists and is continuous on $f(I)$, and second, proving from the limit definition of the derivative that $f^{-1}$ is differentiable with the stated reciprocal formula.

**Step 1: Existence and Continuity of $f^{-1}$**
1. **Strict Monotonicity:** Since $f$ is differentiable on the interval $I$, its derivative $f'(x)$ satisfies the Intermediate Value Property (by Darboux's Theorem)—meaning $f'$ cannot jump from positive to negative values without passing through zero. Because $f'(x) \neq 0$ anywhere on $I$, $f'(x)$ must maintain a single strict sign across the entire interval: either $f'(x) > 0$ everywhere on $I$, or $f'(x) < 0$ everywhere on $I$. By Corollary 3 of the Mean Value Theorem, $f$ is either strictly increasing on $I$ or strictly decreasing on $I$.
2. **Invertibility:** Because $f$ is strictly monotonic on $I$, it is one-to-one (injective). Therefore, the inverse function $f^{-1}$ is guaranteed to exist on the range $f(I)$.
3. **Continuity of the Inverse:** Since $f$ is differentiable on the interval $I$, it is continuous on $I$. By a standard theorem of real analysis, the inverse of a continuous, strictly monotonic function on an interval $I$ is itself continuous on the image interval $f(I)$.

**Step 2: Differentiability via the Limit Definition**
Let $a \in I$ and set $b = f(a)$, so that $a = f^{-1}(b)$. To show that $f^{-1}$ is differentiable at $b$, we examine the difference quotient for $f^{-1}$ at any point $y \in f(I)$ with $y \neq b$. Because $f: I \to f(I)$ is a bijection, there exists a unique $x \in I$ such that $y = f(x)$, which is equivalent to $x = f^{-1}(y)$. Moreover, since $y \neq b$ and $f$ is one-to-one, we must have $x \neq a$.

Using the substitutions $f^{-1}(y) = x$, $f^{-1}(b) = a$, $y = f(x)$, and $b = f(a)$, we can rewrite the difference quotient for $f^{-1}$ at $b$ as:
$$
\frac{f^{-1}(y) - f^{-1}(b)}{y - b} = \frac{x - a}{f(x) - f(a)}
$$
Since $x \neq a$, we can divide the numerator and denominator on the right-hand side by $x - a$ to express this ratio as the reciprocal of the difference quotient for $f$:
$$
\frac{f^{-1}(y) - f^{-1}(b)}{y - b} = \frac{1}{\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}}
$$
Now let's take the limit as $y \to b$. What happens to $x$ as $y$ approaches $b$? Because we proved in Step 1 that $f^{-1}$ is continuous at $b$, taking $y \to b$ forces $x = f^{-1}(y) \to f^{-1}(b) = a$. Since $y \neq b$ implies $x \neq a$, the limit as $y \to b$ is completely equivalent to the limit as $x \to a$. By the quotient law for limits, the denominator approaches $f'(a)$:
$$
\lim_{y \to b} \frac{f^{-1}(y) - f^{-1}(b)}{y - b} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{1}{\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}} = \frac{1}{\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}} = \frac{1}{f'(a)}
$$
Because $f$ is differentiable at $a$ and $f'(a) \neq 0$ by hypothesis, this limit exists and is finite. We conclude that $f^{-1}$ is differentiable at $b = f(a)$ and its derivative is given by:
$$
\left(\frac{df^{-1}}{dx}\right)_{x=f(a)} = \frac{1}{\left(\frac{df}{dx}\right)_{x=a}} \quad \text{or} \quad (f^{-1})'(f(a)) = \frac{1}{f'(a)} \quad \blacksquare
$$

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927110352.webp)

### Differential and Rate-of-Change Interpretation

There is also a very natural way to understand Theorem 1 through differentials and rates of change. Suppose $y = f(x)$ is differentiable at $x = a$. If we nudge $x$ by a tiny increment $dx$, the corresponding change in $y$ is approximated by the differential relation:
$$
dy \approx f'(a) \, dx
$$
In plain language, near $x = a$, the output $y$ changes approximately $f'(a)$ times as fast as the input $x$. For example, if $f'(a) = 3$, then $y$ grows three times as fast as $x$.

Now flip your perspective and view $x$ as a function of $y$ via the inverse $x = f^{-1}(y)$. If $y$ changes three times as fast as $x$, then $x$ must change one-third as fast as $y$! Algebraically, dividing the differential relation by $f'(a)$ gives:
$$
dx \approx \frac{1}{f'(a)} \, dy
$$
This confirms that $x$ changes approximately $\frac{1}{f'(a)}$ times as fast as $y$, which is why the Leibniz notation $\frac{dx}{dy} = \frac{1}{dy/dx}$ behaves just like an ordinary fraction.

### Chain Rule Connection

Once you already know that both $f$ and $f^{-1}$ are differentiable, you never have to memorize the formula in Theorem 1—you can re-derive it in two lines using the Chain Rule! Starting from the defining identity $f^{-1}(f(x)) = x$, we differentiate both sides with respect to $x$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx} \left[ f^{-1}(f(x)) \right] &= \frac{d}{dx}[x] \\
(f^{-1})'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) &= 1
\end{aligned}
$$
Dividing both sides by $f'(x)$ (which is valid whenever $f'(x) \neq 0$) immediately produces:
$$
(f^{-1})'(f(x)) = \frac{1}{f'(x)}
$$

## 6.1 Exercises: Theory and Applications (Selected Problems 45–52)

### Problem 45: Invertibility of Negated Functions

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> If $f(x)$ is one-to-one, can anything be said about $g(x) = -f(x)$? Give reasons for your answer.

**Solution:**
Yes! If $f(x)$ is one-to-one, then its negation $g(x) = -f(x)$ is also guaranteed to be one-to-one. We can verify this both algebraically and geometrically.

For the algebraic proof, let $x_1$ and $x_2$ be any two points in $\operatorname{Dom}(g) = \operatorname{Dom}(f)$ and suppose that $g(x_1) = g(x_2)$. By the definition of $g$, this means:
$$
-f(x_1) = -f(x_2)
$$
Multiplying both sides of the equation by $-1$ cancels the negative signs:
$$
f(x_1) = f(x_2)
$$
Since $f$ is given to be one-to-one, having equal outputs $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ forces the inputs to be equal: $x_1 = x_2$. Thus, $g(x_1) = g(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$, which proves that $g$ is one-to-one.

From a geometric perspective, the graph of $g(x) = -f(x)$ is obtained by reflecting the graph of $f(x)$ vertically across the $x$-axis. Any horizontal line $y = c$ intersects the reflected graph $y = g(x)$ at an $x$-value if and only if the mirrored horizontal line $y = -c$ intersects the original graph $y = f(x)$ at that exact same $x$-value. Because $f$ passes the horizontal line test (meaning $y = -c$ hits $f$ at most once), the line $y = c$ can hit $g$ at most once. Therefore, $g$ also passes the horizontal line test. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 46: Invertibility of Reciprocal Functions

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> If $f(x)$ is one-to-one and $f(x)$ is never $0$, can anything be said about $h(x) = 1/f(x)$? Give reasons for your answer.

**Solution:**
Yes, the reciprocal function $h(x) = \frac{1}{f(x)}$ is guaranteed to be one-to-one on $\operatorname{Dom}(h) = \operatorname{Dom}(f)$.

To prove this, suppose that $h(x_1) = h(x_2)$ for some $x_1, x_2 \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)$. Writing out the definition of $h$ gives:
$$
\frac{1}{f(x_1)} = \frac{1}{f(x_2)}
$$
Because $f(x)$ is never zero on its domain, both denominators are nonzero real numbers, allowing us to take the reciprocal of both sides:
$$
f(x_1) = f(x_2)
$$
Since $f$ is one-to-one, $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ implies $x_1 = x_2$. This shows that $h(x_1) = h(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$, proving that $h$ is one-to-one. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 47: Composition of One-to-One Functions

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose that the range of $g$ lies in the domain of $f$ so that the composite $f \circ g$ is defined. If $f$ and $g$ are one-to-one, can anything be said about $f \circ g$? Give reasons for your answer.

**Solution:**
Yes, the composite function $f \circ g$ is guaranteed to be one-to-one. Intuitively, if the inner machine $g$ never merges distinct inputs, and the outer machine $f$ never merges distinct intermediate outputs, then chaining the two machines together will never merge distinct inputs either.

To prove this formally, let $x_1, x_2 \in \operatorname{Dom}(g)$ and assume that $(f \circ g)(x_1) = (f \circ g)(x_2)$. By the definition of function composition, we have:
$$
f(g(x_1)) = f(g(x_2))
$$
Here, the numbers $g(x_1)$ and $g(x_2)$ act as inputs to the outer function $f$. Since $f$ is one-to-one, two inputs that produce the same output under $f$ must be equal:
$$
g(x_1) = g(x_2)
$$
Now we peel back the second layer: since the inner function $g$ is also one-to-one, the equality $g(x_1) = g(x_2)$ forces the original inputs to be equal:
$$
x_1 = x_2
$$
Therefore, $(f \circ g)(x_1) = (f \circ g)(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$, so $f \circ g$ is one-to-one. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 48: One-to-One Property of Inner Functions in a Composite

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> If a composite $f \circ g$ is one-to-one, must $g$ be one-to-one? Give reasons for your answer.

**Solution:**
Yes, the inner function $g$ **must** be one-to-one. Why? Imagine what would happen if $g$ were *not* one-to-one: two different inputs $x_1 \neq x_2$ would get collapsed by $g$ into the exact same intermediate number $g(x_1) = g(x_2)$. Once those two inputs are merged into a single number, the outer function $f$ receives only that single value and must output $f(g(x_1)) = f(g(x_2))$, which would destroy the one-to-one property of $f \circ g$.

We can turn this intuition into a direct proof. Suppose $g(x_1) = g(x_2)$ for some $x_1, x_2 \in \operatorname{Dom}(g)$. Because $f$ is a well-defined function, applying $f$ to the equal numbers $g(x_1)$ and $g(x_2)$ must yield equal outputs:
$$
f(g(x_1)) = f(g(x_2))
$$
By the definition of composition, this equation is simply:
$$
(f \circ g)(x_1) = (f \circ g)(x_2)
$$
Since the composite function $f \circ g$ is given to be one-to-one, equal outputs under $f \circ g$ imply that the starting inputs must be equal:
$$
x_1 = x_2
$$
Hence, $g(x_1) = g(x_2) \implies x_1 = x_2$, which proves that $g$ must be one-to-one. (Notice, by the way, that the outer function $f$ only needs to be one-to-one on the subset $\operatorname{Ran}(g)$, not necessarily on all of $\operatorname{Dom}(f)$!) $\blacksquare$

### Problem 49: Geometric Area Integral Identity for Inverses

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Suppose $f(x)$ is positive, continuous, and increasing over the interval $[a, b]$. By interpreting the graph of $f$ show that:
> $$
> \int_a^b f(x) \, dx + \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy = b f(b) - a f(a)
> $$

**Solution:**
We can establish this beautiful identity both geometrically—by slicing up rectangles in the plane—and analytically via integration by parts.

**Method 1: Geometric Interpretation (Decomposition of Bounding Rectangles)**
Assuming $a \ge 0$ so that the curve lies entirely in the first quadrant ($x \ge 0$ and $y \ge 0$), let's look at the rectangles formed by the endpoints $(a, f(a))$ and $(b, f(b))$ on the graph of $y = f(x)$:
1. First, construct the large outer rectangle with vertices at $(0,0)$, $(b,0)$, $(b, f(b))$, and $(0, f(b))$. Its base is $b$ and its height is $f(b)$, so its area is:
   $$
   \text{Area}_{\text{large}} = b \cdot f(b)
   $$
2. Next, look at the smaller inner rectangle tucked into the lower-left corner with vertices at $(0,0)$, $(a,0)$, $(a, f(a))$, and $(0, f(a))$. Its base is $a$ and its height is $f(a)$, so its area is:
   $$
   \text{Area}_{\text{corner}} = a \cdot f(a)
   $$
3. Because $f$ is increasing on $[a, b]$, we have $a < b$ and $f(a) < f(b)$, so the corner rectangle lies completely inside the large rectangle. Removing the smaller corner rectangle from the large outer rectangle leaves an $L$-shaped region whose total area is the difference:
   $$
   \text{Area}_L = b f(b) - a f(a)
   $$
4. Now look at how the continuous, strictly increasing curve $y = f(x)$ travels from the inner corner $(a, f(a))$ to the outer corner $(b, f(b))$. This curve slices the $L$-shaped region cleanly into two non-overlapping pieces:
   - **Region 1 (Below the curve, along the $x$-axis):** The vertical strip bounded below by the $x$-axis, above by $y = f(x)$, and horizontally from $x = a$ to $x = b$. By the definition of the definite integral with respect to $x$, its area is:
     $$
     \text{Area}_1 = \int_a^b f(x) \, dx
     $$
   - **Region 2 (Left of the curve, along the $y$-axis):** The horizontal strip bounded on the left by the $y$-axis, on the right by the curve $x = f^{-1}(y)$, and vertically from $y = f(a)$ to $y = f(b)$. Integrating horizontally with respect to $y$, its area is:
     $$
     \text{Area}_2 = \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy
     $$

Because Region 1 and Region 2 fit together like puzzle pieces to form the entire $L$-shaped region ($\text{Area}_1 + \text{Area}_2 = \text{Area}_L$), summing the two integrals gives the desired identity:
$$
\int_a^b f(x) \, dx + \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy = b f(b) - a f(a)
$$

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927150017.webp)

**Method 2: Analytic Verification (Assuming Differentiability, via Integration by Parts)**
If $f$ is also continuously differentiable, we can verify this identity purely by calculus. In the second integral $\int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy$, make the substitution $y = f(x)$, so that $dy = f'(x) \, dx$. When $y = f(a)$, $x = a$; when $y = f(b)$, $x = b$. Since $f^{-1}(f(x)) = x$, the integral transforms into:
$$
\int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy = \int_a^b f^{-1}(f(x)) f'(x) \, dx = \int_a^b x f'(x) \, dx
$$
Now apply integration by parts ($\int u \, dv = uv - \int v \, du$) with $u = x$ and $dv = f'(x) \, dx$, so that $du = dx$ and $v = f(x)$:
$$
\int_a^b x f'(x) \, dx = \left[ x f(x) \right]_a^b - \int_a^b f(x) \, dx = b f(b) - a f(a) - \int_a^b f(x) \, dx
$$
Adding $\int_a^b f(x) \, dx$ to both sides immediately confirms the identity:
$$
\int_a^b f(x) \, dx + \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy = b f(b) - a f(a) \quad \blacksquare
$$

### Problem 50: Invertibility of Linear Fractional (Möbius) Transformations

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Determine conditions on the constants $a, b, c,$ and $d$ so that the rational function
> $$
> f(x) = \frac{ax + b}{cx + d}
> $$
> has an inverse.

**Solution:**
First, for $f(x)$ to be a well-defined function at all, the denominator $cx + d$ cannot be identically zero, meaning $c$ and $d$ cannot both be $0$. On its natural domain $\{x \in \mathbb{R} \mid cx + d \neq 0\}$, the rational function $f(x)$ has an inverse if and only if it is one-to-one.

Let's test when $f$ is one-to-one by setting $f(x_1) = f(x_2)$ for two inputs $x_1, x_2 \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)$ and seeing what condition forces $x_1 = x_2$:
$$
\frac{ax_1 + b}{cx_1 + d} = \frac{ax_2 + b}{cx_2 + d}
$$
Cross-multiplying to clear the nonzero denominators gives:
$$
(ax_1 + b)(cx_2 + d) = (ax_2 + b)(cx_1 + d)
$$
Expanding the products on both sides yields:
$$
ac x_1 x_2 + ad x_1 + bc x_2 + bd = ac x_1 x_2 + ad x_2 + bc x_1 + bd
$$
Notice that the terms $ac x_1 x_2$ and $bd$ appear identically on both sides! Subtracting them away simplifies the equation to:
$$
ad x_1 + bc x_2 = ad x_2 + bc x_1
$$
Now let's bring all terms involving $x_1$ to the left side and all terms involving $x_2$ to the right side, and factor:
$$
\begin{aligned}
ad x_1 - bc x_1 &= ad x_2 - bc x_2 \\
(ad - bc) x_1 &= (ad - bc) x_2 \\
(ad - bc)(x_1 - x_2) &= 0
\end{aligned}
$$
Look closely at the factor $ad - bc$ (which you may recognize as the determinant of the coefficient matrix $\begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{pmatrix}$). This equation splits into two distinct cases:
1. **Case 1 ($ad - bc \neq 0$):** Because the constant $ad - bc$ is nonzero, we can divide both sides of $(ad - bc)(x_1 - x_2) = 0$ by $ad - bc$ to conclude that:
   $$
   x_1 - x_2 = 0 \implies x_1 = x_2
   $$
   Thus, whenever $ad - bc \neq 0$, the function $f$ is strictly one-to-one and has a valid inverse! In fact, solving $y = \frac{ax+b}{cx+d}$ for $x$ via $y(cx+d) = ax+b \implies (cy - a)x = -dy + b$ gives the explicit inverse formula:
   $$
   f^{-1}(y) = \frac{-dy + b}{cy - a}
   $$
2. **Case 2 ($ad - bc = 0$):** If $ad - bc = 0$, the equation $(ad - bc)(x_1 - x_2) = 0$ becomes $0 \cdot (x_1 - x_2) = 0$, which is satisfied for *all* pairs $x_1$ and $x_2$ even when $x_1 \neq x_2$. Why does this happen? We can also see this via calculus by computing the derivative of $f(x)$ with the Quotient Rule:
   $$
   f'(x) = \frac{a(cx + d) - c(ax + b)}{(cx + d)^2} = \frac{ad - bc}{(cx + d)^2}
   $$
   When $ad - bc = 0$, the derivative $f'(x)$ is identically $0$ everywhere on its domain, meaning $f(x)$ collapses into a constant function, which is never one-to-one and has no inverse.

Therefore, the rational function $f(x) = \frac{ax+b}{cx+d}$ has an inverse if and only if:
$$
ad - bc \neq 0 \quad \blacksquare
$$

### Problem 51: Chain Rule Connection and Differentiability of Inverses

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> If we write $g(x)$ for $f^{-1}(x)$, the derivative formula can be written as:
> $$
> g'(f(a)) = \frac{1}{f'(a)}, \quad \text{or} \quad g'(f(a)) \cdot f'(a) = 1
> $$
> Replacing $a$ with $x$ yields:
> $$
> g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) = 1
> $$
> Assume that $f$ and $g$ are differentiable functions that are inverses of one another, so that $(g \circ f)(x) = x$. Differentiate both sides of this equation with respect to $x$, using the Chain Rule to express $(g \circ f)'(x)$ as a product of derivatives of $g$ and $f$. What do you find? Explain why this is not a proof of Theorem 1.

**Solution:**
Let's perform the differentiation first and then examine the logical assumptions behind it.

**Part 1: Chain Rule Derivation**
Assuming that $f$ and $g = f^{-1}$ are both differentiable inverse functions, they satisfy the identity $(g \circ f)(x) = g(f(x)) = x$ for all $x \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)$. Differentiating both sides of this equation with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}\left[ g(f(x)) \right] = \frac{d}{dx}[x]
$$
Applying the Chain Rule to the composite function on the left-hand side and noting that $\frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1$ on the right-hand side, we find:
$$
g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) = 1
$$
Provided $f'(x) \neq 0$, dividing both sides by $f'(x)$ yields the exact derivative formula from Theorem 1:
$$
g'(f(x)) = \frac{1}{f'(x)}
$$
(Or, setting $y = f(x)$ so that $x = g(y) = f^{-1}(y)$, this reads $(f^{-1})'(y) = \frac{1}{f'(f^{-1}(y))}$.)

**Part 2: Why This Is Not a Proof of Theorem 1**
Why couldn't we just use this short two-line calculation to prove Theorem 1 instead of going through the two-step limit proof? Look at the hypotheses required to invoke the Chain Rule: in order to write $\frac{d}{dx}[g(f(x))] = g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x)$, you must **already know in advance** that the outer function $g = f^{-1}$ is differentiable at $f(x)$!

In Theorem 1, however, we are *only* given that $f$ is differentiable with $f' \neq 0$; we are not told whether $f^{-1}$ is differentiable. The hardest and most important part of Theorem 1 is proving that the derivative $g'(f(x))$ *exists in the first place*. If you use the Chain Rule to prove Theorem 1, you are assuming at the very first step that $g$ is differentiable in order to prove that $g$ is differentiable—a classic logical fallacy known as **circular reasoning** (or begging the question). Thus, while the Chain Rule gives a great shortcut for finding the *formula* once differentiability is known, proving *existence* of the derivative requires the rigorous difference-quotient argument of Theorem 1. $\blacksquare$

### Problem 52: Equivalence of the Washer and Shell Methods for Inverses

> [!abstract] Problem Statement
> Let $f$ be differentiable on the interval $a \le x \le b$, with $a > 0$, and suppose that $f$ has a differentiable inverse, $f^{-1}$. Revolve about the $y$-axis the region bounded by the graph of $f$ and the lines $x = a$ and $y = f(b)$ to generate a solid. Then the values of the integrals given by the washer and shell methods for the volume have identical values:
> $$
> \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy = \int_a^b 2\pi x (f(b) - f(x)) \, dx
> $$
> To prove this equality, define:
> $$
> W(t) = \int_{f(a)}^{f(t)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy
> $$
> $$
> S(t) = \int_a^t 2\pi x (f(t) - f(x)) \, dx
> $$
> Show that the functions $W$ and $S$ agree at a point of $[a, b]$ and have identical derivatives on $[a, b]$, thereby establishing $W(t) = S(t)$ for all $t \in [a, b]$, and in particular $W(b) = S(b)$.

**Solution:**
In earlier chapters on volumes of solids of revolution, we took on faith that slicing a solid horizontally into washers ($W$) and slicing it vertically into cylindrical shells ($S$) always produce the exact same volume. Here, by letting the upper bound $b$ vary as a variable $t \in [a, b]$, we can prove that the two integral formulas are mathematically identical!

**Step 1: Verify Agreement at the Initial Point $t = a$**
Let's evaluate both volume functions $W(t)$ and $S(t)$ at the left endpoint $t = a$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
W(a) &= \int_{f(a)}^{f(a)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy = 0 \\
S(a) &= \int_a^a 2\pi x (f(a) - f(x)) \, dx = 0
\end{aligned}
$$
Because both definite integrals have matching upper and lower limits of integration at $t = a$, both evaluate to zero, confirming that $W(a) = S(a) = 0$.

**Step 2: Differentiate $W(t)$ Using the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus and the Chain Rule**
In $W(t)$, the variable $t$ appears only in the upper limit of integration $u(t) = f(t)$, while the integrand $\pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right)$ is a continuous function of the dummy variable $y$. By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (Part 1) combined with the Chain Rule, we differentiate $W(t)$ by plugging the upper limit $y = f(t)$ into the integrand and multiplying by the derivative of the upper limit, $f'(t)$:
$$
W'(t) = \frac{d}{dt} \left[ \int_{f(a)}^{f(t)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy \right] = \pi \left( (f^{-1}(f(t)))^2 - a^2 \right) \cdot f'(t)
$$
Since $f^{-1}(f(t)) = t$ by the inverse composition rule, this simplifies cleanly to:
$$
W'(t) = \pi (t^2 - a^2) f'(t)
$$

**Step 3: Differentiate $S(t)$ Using Algebra, the Product Rule, and the FTC**
Be careful when differentiating $S(t)$! Notice that the variable $t$ appears in *two* places: as the upper limit of integration $\int_a^t$, and inside the integrand via the factor $f(t)$. Before we can apply the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, we must expand the integrand and factor $f(t)$ outside the $x$-integral (since $f(t)$ is independent of the integration variable $x$):
$$
\begin{aligned}
S(t) &= \int_a^t 2\pi x (f(t) - f(x)) \, dx \\
&= \int_a^t 2\pi x f(t) \, dx - \int_a^t 2\pi x f(x) \, dx \\
&= f(t) \int_a^t 2\pi x \, dx - \int_a^t 2\pi x f(x) \, dx
\end{aligned}
$$
We can evaluate the first integral explicitly right now:
$$
\int_a^t 2\pi x \, dx = \left[ \pi x^2 \right]_a^t = \pi(t^2 - a^2)
$$
Substituting this back into our expression for $S(t)$ gives:
$$
S(t) = \pi(t^2 - a^2) f(t) - \int_a^t 2\pi x f(x) \, dx
$$
Now we can differentiate $S(t)$ with respect to $t$. We apply the Product Rule to the first term $\pi(t^2 - a^2) f(t)$ and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus to the integral term $\int_a^t 2\pi x f(x) \, dx$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
S'(t) &= \frac{d}{dt}\left[ \pi(t^2 - a^2) f(t) \right] - \frac{d}{dt}\left[ \int_a^t 2\pi x f(x) \, dx \right] \\
&= \left( \frac{d}{dt}[\pi(t^2 - a^2)] \cdot f(t) + \pi(t^2 - a^2) \cdot f'(t) \right) - 2\pi t f(t) \\
&= 2\pi t f(t) + \pi(t^2 - a^2) f'(t) - 2\pi t f(t) \\
&= \pi(t^2 - a^2) f'(t)
\end{aligned}
$$
Look how nicely the $2\pi t f(t)$ terms cancel out!

**Step 4: Conclude That $W(b) = S(b)$**
Comparing our results from Step 2 and Step 3, we see that $W(t)$ and $S(t)$ have the exact same derivative at every point of $[a, b]$:
$$
W'(t) = S'(t) = \pi(t^2 - a^2) f'(t) \quad \text{for all } t \in [a, b]
$$
By Corollary 2 of the Mean Value Theorem, two functions with identical derivatives on an interval $[a, b]$ can differ only by a constant $C$:
$$
W(t) - S(t) = C \quad \text{for all } t \in [a, b]
$$
Evaluating this equation at $t = a$ and using $W(a) = S(a) = 0$ from Step 1 gives:
$$
C = W(a) - S(a) = 0 - 0 = 0
$$
Therefore, $W(t) = S(t)$ for all $t \in [a, b]$. Setting $t = b$ establishes the exact equality of the washer and shell volume integrals:
$$
W(b) = S(b) \iff \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy = \int_a^b 2\pi x (f(b) - f(x)) \, dx \quad \blacksquare
$$

---

# 6.2 Natural Logarithms

## Historical Context and Development

Before the age of electronic calculators, multiplying and dividing large multi-digit numbers by hand was a grueling bottleneck for astronomers, navigators, and engineers. In the late 1500s, the Scottish baron **John Napier** (1550–1617) asked a brilliant question: *What if we could transform multiplication into simple addition?* This led him to invent a revolutionary computational method called the **logarithm**, built around the fundamental algebraic identity:
$$
\ln(ax) = \ln a + \ln x
$$
Instead of multiplying two large numbers $a$ and $x$ directly, a calculator could simply look up their logarithms in a precomputed table, add those two numbers together, and then look up the corresponding "anti-logarithm" to read off the product.

Napier devoted the final 20 years of his life to calculating these extensive logarithmic tables by hand, while the Danish astronomer Tycho Brahe waited anxiously for them to accelerate his celestial mechanics calculations. When Napier passed away before finishing, the tables were completed posthumously by his friend **Henry Briggs** in London. Because Briggs adapted the tables to base $10$ for practical decimal arithmetic, base-$10$ logarithms became known historically as *Briggsian logarithms*.

*(Historical note: Napier was also a prolific inventor of military technology and designed a devastatingly accurate artillery piece capable of hitting a cow a mile away; appalled by the destructive capability of the weapon, he stopped manufacturing it and suppressed the blueprints.)*

In modern calculus, however, we reverse Napier's historical path. Rather than starting with computational bases and fractional exponents—which are tricky to define rigorously for irrational numbers—we define the **natural logarithm** directly and cleanly as a definite integral. From this single geometric foundation, all the classic algebraic laws of logarithms emerge naturally through calculus.

## Definition of the Natural Logarithm Function

Why do we define the natural logarithm as an integral? Recall the power rule for integration, $\int t^n \, dt = \frac{t^{n+1}}{n+1} + C$, which works for every rational power except $n = -1$ (where dividing by $n + 1$ causes division by zero). Defining a function that accumulates the area under the hyperbola $y = \frac{1}{t}$ fills this exact gap in calculus.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Natural Logarithm Function
> The **natural logarithm function**, denoted $\ln x$, is defined for all positive real numbers $x > 0$ by:
> $$
> \ln x = \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt, \quad x > 0
> $$

In plain language, $\ln x$ measures the signed area between the curve $y = \frac{1}{t}$ and the horizontal $t$-axis from the fixed starting point $t = 1$ to the variable endpoint $t = x$.

### Geometric Interpretation as an Accumulation Function

Because $\ln x$ is defined as a definite integral starting at $t = 1$, its value and algebraic sign depend on where $x$ lies relative to $1$ along the horizontal axis:

1. **For $x > 1$:** We integrate from left to right ($1$ to $x$) beneath a strictly positive curve, so $\ln x$ represents the positive geometric area under $y = \frac{1}{t}$ from $t = 1$ to $t = x$:
   $$
   \ln x = \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt > 0
   $$
2. **For $x = 1$:** The upper and lower integration limits coincide, collapsing the region of integration to a single line segment of zero width:
   $$
   \ln 1 = \int_1^1 \frac{1}{t} \, dt = 0
   $$
3. **For $0 < x < 1$:** Here the upper limit $x$ lies to the left of the lower limit $1$. Reversing the integration limits shows that $\ln x$ is the negative of the geometric area under $y = \frac{1}{t}$ from $t = x$ to $t = 1$:
   $$
   \ln x = \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt = -\int_x^1 \frac{1}{t} \, dt < 0
   $$
4. **For $x \le 0$:** The function $\ln x$ is **undefined** because integrating from $1$ to a non-positive number $x$ would require crossing $t = 0$, where the integrand $\frac{1}{t}$ has an infinite, non-integrable singularity.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927110724.webp)

### The Dummy Variable of Integration

Why do we write $\int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt$ using the letter $t$ instead of $\int_1^x \frac{1}{x} \, dx$? In the integral definition $\ln x = \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt$, the symbol $t$ serves as a **dummy variable of integration**. Using a distinct variable $t$ to sweep along the horizontal axis of the curve $y = \frac{1}{t}$ prevents us from confusing the running integration variable with the fixed upper limit $x$ at which we are evaluating the logarithm.

## The Derivative of $y = \ln x$

### Direct Derivative from the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus

Because we defined $\ln x$ as an accumulation integral with a variable upper limit $x$, finding its derivative requires zero algebraic guesswork—we can read it directly off Part 1 of the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, which states that $\frac{d}{dx} \int_a^x f(t)\,dt = f(x)$ for any continuous integrand $f(t)$. Applying this theorem to $f(t) = \frac{1}{t}$ (which is continuous for all $t > 0$) immediately yields:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{d}{dx} \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt = \frac{1}{x}
$$

> [!theorem] Derivative of the Natural Logarithm
> For every $x > 0$:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{1}{x}
> $$

Notice what this says geometrically: at any positive input $x$, the slope of the tangent line to $y = \ln x$ is simply the reciprocal $\frac{1}{x}$. As $x$ grows very large, the slope $\frac{1}{x}$ flattens out toward $0$; as $x$ approaches $0$ from the right, the slope shoots up toward $+\infty$.

### Chain Rule Extension

What if the argument inside the natural logarithm is not simply $x$, but a composite function $u = u(x)$? Provided $u(x)$ is positive and differentiable, we can combine our basic derivative formula with the Chain Rule:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln u = \frac{d}{du}[\ln u] \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{1}{u} \frac{du}{dx}
$$

> [!theorem] Chain Rule for Natural Logarithms
> If $u > 0$ is a differentiable function of $x$:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \ln u = \frac{1}{u} \frac{du}{dx}
> $$

In words, differentiating the natural logarithm of a positive function produces a fraction: place the inside function $u$ in the denominator, and place its derivative $\frac{du}{dx}$ in the numerator.

### Scaling Invariance of the Derivative

Let's test the Chain Rule on a simple scaled input, $\ln(ax)$, where $a > 0$ is any positive constant. Differentiating with respect to $x$ gives a surprising result:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln(ax) = \frac{1}{ax} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(ax) = \frac{1}{ax} \cdot a = \frac{1}{x}
$$
The constant factor $a$ cancels out completely! Consequently, the horizontally scaled function $\ln(ax)$ has the **exact same derivative** as $\ln x$ at every point $x > 0$. Geometrically, this means the graph of $y = \ln(ax)$ has the exact same slope as $y = \ln x$ at every $x$, so one graph must simply be a vertical shift of the other—an observation that unlocks all the algebraic laws of logarithms.

## Algebraic Properties of Logarithms and Their Rigorous Proofs

In precalculus, you likely memorized the logarithm laws as consequences of exponent rules. Now that we have defined $\ln x$ as an integral, how do we know it actually obeys Napier's algebraic rules? Let's summarize these four classic identities and then prove each one rigorously using calculus.

> [!summary] Table 6.1: Properties of Natural Logarithms
> For any positive numbers $a > 0$ and $x > 0$:
> 1. **Product Rule:** $\ln(ax) = \ln a + \ln x$
> 2. **Quotient Rule:** $\ln \left(\frac{a}{x}\right) = \ln a - \ln x$
> 3. **Reciprocal Rule:** $\ln \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = -\ln x$ (special case of Quotient Rule with $a = 1$)
> 4. **Power Rule:** $\ln(x^n) = n \ln x$ (for rational $n$, and all real $n$)

### Proof of the Product Rule: $\ln(ax) = \ln a + \ln x$

**Proof:** Why should the logarithm of a product equal the sum of the individual logarithms? Recall from our scaling invariance calculation above that when we treat $a > 0$ as a fixed constant and differentiate both $\ln(ax)$ and $\ln x$ with respect to $x$, we obtain the exact same derivative:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln(ax) = \frac{1}{ax} \cdot a = \frac{1}{x} \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{1}{x}
$$
Because both functions share the identical derivative across the entire connected open interval $(0, \infty)$, **Corollary 1 of the Mean Value Theorem** guarantees that they can differ only by a constant $C$ on $(0, \infty)$:
$$
\ln(ax) = \ln x + C
$$
To pinpoint the value of $C$, we can plug in any convenient positive value of $x$ since this equation holds for all $x > 0$. Choosing $x = 1$ (where we already know $\ln 1 = 0$) makes the evaluation effortless:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\ln(a \cdot 1) &= \ln(1) + C \\
\ln a &= 0 + C \\
\implies C &= \ln a
\end{aligned}
$$
Substituting $C = \ln a$ back into our relation gives the desired identity for all $a, x > 0$:
$$
\ln(ax) = \ln a + \ln x \quad \blacksquare
$$

### Proof of the Reciprocal Rule: $\ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = -\ln x$

**Proof:** Once the Product Rule is established, we don't even need derivatives to prove the Reciprocal Rule! Since $\frac{1}{x}$ and $x$ multiply to $1$, we can substitute $a = \frac{1}{x}$ directly into the Product Rule $\ln(ax) = \ln a + \ln x$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\ln\left(\frac{1}{x} \cdot x\right) &= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) + \ln x \\
\ln(1) &= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) + \ln x \\
0 &= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) + \ln x \\
\implies \ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) &= -\ln x \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

### Proof of the Quotient Rule: $\ln\left(\frac{a}{x}\right) = \ln a - \ln x$

**Proof:** We can view division by $x$ as multiplication by the reciprocal $\frac{1}{x}$. Rewriting $\frac{a}{x}$ as the product $a \cdot \frac{1}{x}$ and applying the Product Rule followed by the Reciprocal Rule yields:
$$
\ln\left(\frac{a}{x}\right) = \ln\left(a \cdot \frac{1}{x}\right) = \ln a + \ln\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \ln a - \ln x \quad \blacksquare
$$

### Proof of the Power Rule: $\ln(x^n) = n \ln x$ (for Rational $n$)

**Proof:** Let $n$ be any rational number, and let $x > 0$. We use the same Mean Value Theorem strategy that worked for the Product Rule: compare the derivatives of the left-hand side and the right-hand side. First, differentiating $\ln(x^n)$ with respect to $x$ via the Chain Rule and the Power Rule for rational exponents gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln(x^n) = \frac{1}{x^n} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = \frac{1}{x^n} \cdot (n x^{n-1}) = \frac{n}{x}
$$
Next, differentiating $n \ln x$ with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(n \ln x) = n \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \frac{n}{x}
$$
Because $\ln(x^n)$ and $n \ln x$ have identical derivatives on the open interval $(0, \infty)$, Corollary 1 of the Mean Value Theorem ensures that they differ by a constant $C$:
$$
\ln(x^n) = n \ln x + C
$$
Evaluating both sides at $x = 1$ (where $1^n = 1$ and $\ln 1 = 0$) determines $C$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\ln(1^n) &= n \ln(1) + C \\
\ln(1) &= 0 + C \\
0 &= 0 + C \implies C = 0
\end{aligned}
$$
Substituting $C = 0$ completes the proof:
$$
\ln(x^n) = n \ln x \quad \blacksquare
$$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on Irrational Exponents
> Notice that the proof above relies on the differentiation formula $\frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = n x^{n-1}$, which has only been established for rational exponents $n \in \mathbb{Q}$ at this stage of calculus. While the identity $\ln(x^n) = n \ln x$ does hold for all real exponents, proving it for irrational powers requires first defining what an irrational power $x^r$ even means via the exponential definition $x^r = e^{r \ln x}$.

## Analysis of the Graph and Range of $\ln x$

What does the curve $y = \ln x$ look like globally, and how much of the vertical $y$-axis does its range cover? By combining derivative tests for shape with our newly proven Power Rule for boundary limits, we can build a complete picture of the graph.

### Monotonicity and Concavity

We can determine the direction and bending of $y = \ln x$ directly from its first and second derivatives:
- **First Derivative (Monotonicity):** Because $x$ is restricted to the domain $(0, \infty)$, the first derivative is always strictly positive:
  $$
  \frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{1}{x} > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in (0, \infty)
  $$
  Therefore, $\ln x$ is **strictly increasing** on its entire domain $(0, \infty)$ (and hence is a one-to-one function).
- **Second Derivative (Concavity):** Differentiating $\frac{1}{x} = x^{-1}$ a second time shows that the second derivative is always strictly negative:
  $$
  \frac{d^2}{dx^2} \ln x = \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = -\frac{1}{x^2} < 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in (0, \infty)
  $$
  Therefore, the graph of $\ln x$ is **strictly concave down** everywhere on its domain.

### Asymptotic Behavior at Boundaries

Because the slope $\frac{1}{x}$ approaches $0$ as $x$ grows large, a natural question arises: *Does $\ln x$ level off below some horizontal asymptote, or does it keep climbing to $\infty$?*

To answer this rigorously, we first observe by numerical area approximation (for instance, comparing the area under $y = \frac{1}{t}$ on $[1, 2]$ to a simple inscribed rectangle or trapezoid) that $\ln 2 \approx 0.693 > \frac{1}{2}$. Now we can use the Power Rule on powers of $2$:
- **Behavior as $x \to \infty$:** For any positive integer $n$, evaluating $\ln x$ at $x = 2^n$ yields:
  $$
  \ln(2^n) = n \ln 2 > n \left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{n}{2}
  $$
  As $n \to \infty$, the lower bound $\frac{n}{2} \to \infty$, which forces $\ln(2^n) \to \infty$. Since $\ln x$ is strictly increasing for all $x > 0$, it cannot be bounded above, proving that:
  $$
  \lim_{x \to \infty} \ln x = \infty
  $$
- **Behavior as $x \to 0^+$:** Similarly, evaluating $\ln x$ at $x = 2^{-n} = \frac{1}{2^n}$ for any positive integer $n$ gives:
  $$
  \ln(2^{-n}) = -n \ln 2 < -n \left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\frac{n}{2}
  $$
  As $n \to \infty$, $2^{-n} \to 0^+$ while the upper bound $-\frac{n}{2} \to -\infty$. Because $\ln x$ is strictly increasing, this proves that the graph plunges downward without bound near the $y$-axis:
  $$
  \lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln x = -\infty
  $$

### Summary of Function Characteristics

Because $\ln x$ is continuous on $(0, \infty)$ and stretches between $-\infty$ and $\infty$, the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantees that it attains every real number as an output. We can summarize its fundamental characteristics as follows:
- **Domain:** $(0, \infty)$
- **Range:** $(-\infty, \infty)$
- **Intercept:** $(1, 0)$ (since $\ln 1 = 0$)
- **Asymptote:** Vertical asymptote at the line $x = 0$ (the $y$-axis).

## Technique of Logarithmic Differentiation

Suppose you need to differentiate a daunting function built from multiple products, quotients, and fractional powers—such as $y = \frac{(x^2 + 1)(x + 3)^{1/2}}{x - 1}$. Attacking this directly with the Product, Quotient, and Chain Rules all at once is an algebraic minefield.

Instead, we can use **logarithmic differentiation**: by taking the natural logarithm of both sides *before* differentiating, we use Napier's logarithm laws to break complicated products into simple sums, quotients into differences, and exponents into scalar multipliers.

### Procedural Algorithm (for $y = f(x) > 0$)

Whenever you want to differentiate a positive function $y = f(x) > 0$ involving messy products, quotients, or powers, follow this five-step algorithm:

1. **Take logarithms:** Take the natural logarithm of both sides of the equation:
   $$
   \ln y = \ln f(x)
   $$
2. **Expand:** Apply the algebraic properties of logarithms (Product, Quotient, and Power rules) to expand $\ln f(x)$ into a sum and difference of simpler logarithmic terms.
3. **Differentiate implicitly:** Differentiate both sides with respect to $x$, using the Chain Rule $\frac{d}{dx}[\ln y] = \frac{1}{y}\frac{dy}{dx}$ on the left:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}[\ln y] &= \frac{d}{dx}[\ln f(x)] \\
   \frac{1}{y} \frac{dy}{dx} &= \frac{d}{dx}[\ln f(x)]
   \end{aligned}
   $$
4. **Isolate $\frac{dy}{dx}$:** Multiply both sides by $y$ to solve for the derivative:
   $$
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\ln f(x)]
   $$
5. **Substitute the original formula:** Replace $y$ with its explicit expression $f(x)$ so that the final derivative is written purely in terms of $x$:
   $$
   \frac{dy}{dx} = f(x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\ln f(x)]
   $$

> [!note] Theoretical Nuance: Domain Restrictions and Validity of Logarithmic Differentiation
> A natural theoretical question arises: *Does taking the logarithm in Step 1 artificially restrict our final derivative formula only to points where $f(x) > 0$?* 
> 
> Remarkably, the resulting derivative formula remains valid everywhere the original function $f$ is differentiable—including points where $f(x) < 0$ and even roots where $f(x) = 0$! This holds because of three theoretical principles:
> 
> 1. **Negative Values ($f(x) < 0$):** Where $f(x)$ is negative (and nonzero), we can apply logarithms to the absolute value: $\ln |y| = \ln |f(x)|$. Because $\frac{d}{dx}\ln |u| = \frac{1}{u}\frac{du}{dx}$ for any nonzero differentiable function $u$, the negative signs cancel identically during differentiation:
>    - For $u > 0$: $\frac{d}{dx}\ln(u) = \frac{1}{u}\frac{du}{dx}$
>    - For $u < 0$: $\frac{d}{dx}\ln(-u) = \frac{1}{-u}\left(-\frac{du}{dx}\right) = \frac{1}{u}\frac{du}{dx}$
>    
>    Thus, sign information does not alter the resulting derivative at all; the intermediate logarithmic step requires only $f(x) \neq 0$.
> 
> 2. **Zeroes ($f(x) = 0$):** At roots where $f(x) = 0$, $\ln |f(x)|$ is temporarily undefined. However, in Step 5:
>    $$
>    \frac{dy}{dx} = f(x) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\ln |f(x)|]
>    $$
>    multiplying back through by the original factor $f(x)$ algebraically cancels the vanishing factors in the denominators, healing those removable singularities. By the continuity of $f'(x)$, the simplified derivative formula evaluated at the root $x = a$ automatically matches the true derivative limit:
>    $$
>    f'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} f'(x)
>    $$
> 
> 3. **Equivalence to the Product and Quotient Rules:** At its core, logarithmic differentiation is simply an algebraic bookkeeping shortcut for repeated applications of the Leibniz Product Rule and Quotient Rule. For any product $y = uv$, the standard product rule $y' = u'v + uv'$ (when divided through by $y = uv$) is algebraically equivalent to:
>    $$
>    \frac{y'}{y} = \frac{u'}{u} + \frac{v'}{v}
>    $$
>    The logarithm serves solely as a temporary scaffolding to decompose products into sums. Because the underlying product and quotient rules hold globally across the entire domain of $f$, the final simplified formula is valid everywhere $f$ is differentiable.

## Integration Involving Logarithms

Every differentiation formula in calculus can be read in reverse as an integration formula. Let's see how reversing the derivative of the natural logarithm allows us to integrate reciprocal functions and quotients across both positive and negative domains.

### The Integral $\int \frac{1}{u} \, du$

Since $\frac{d}{du}\ln u = \frac{1}{u}$ for $u > 0$, we immediately know that $\int \frac{1}{u} \, du = \ln u + C$ whenever $u$ is positive.

Wait—what if $u < 0$? The reciprocal function $\frac{1}{u}$ is perfectly continuous and integrable on negative intervals, even though $\ln u$ itself is undefined for $u < 0$. How do we write an antiderivative when $u < 0$?
- If $u < 0$, then its opposite $-u$ is positive ($-u > 0$), which means $\ln(-u)$ is well-defined!
- Differentiating $\ln(-u)$ with respect to $u$ via the Chain Rule (with inner function $w = -u$) reveals that the two minus signs cancel out:
  $$
  \frac{d}{du} \ln(-u) = \frac{1}{-u} \cdot \frac{d}{du}(-u) = \frac{1}{-u} (-1) = \frac{1}{u}
  $$
- Therefore, on any interval where $u < 0$, the antiderivative of $\frac{1}{u}$ is:
  $$
  \int \frac{1}{u} \, du = \ln(-u) + C
  $$

We can unify both the positive case ($u > 0$) and the negative case ($u < 0$) into a single formula using the **absolute value** notation $|u|$:
- When $u > 0$, $|u| = u \implies \ln |u| = \ln u$.
- When $u < 0$, $|u| = -u \implies \ln |u| = \ln(-u)$.

> [!theorem] Integral of $1/u$
> If $u$ is a nonzero differentiable function:
> $$
> \int \frac{1}{u} \, du = \ln |u| + C
> $$

Including the absolute value bars inside $\ln |u|$ is crucial whenever you compute an indefinite integral or a definite integral over an interval where $u$ takes negative values.

### Resolution of the Power Rule Exception

Look back at the standard power rule for integration:
$$
\int u^n \, du = \frac{u^{n+1}}{n+1} + C, \quad n \neq -1
$$
For every power $n \neq -1$, integrating a power function yields another power function. The mysterious exception at $n = -1$ (where $\frac{u^0}{0}$ breaks down) is now completely resolved by the natural logarithm:
$$
\int u^{-1} \, du = \int \frac{1}{u} \, du = \ln |u| + C
$$

### The Form $\int \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} \, dx$

In practice, the integral rule $\int \frac{du}{u} = \ln |u| + C$ appears most frequently in disguise as a fraction whose numerator is the derivative of its denominator. Whenever you spot an integrand of the form $\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}$, substituting $u = f(x)$ and $du = f'(x) \, dx$ immediately reduces the integral to a natural logarithm:
$$
\int \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} \, dx = \int \frac{du}{u} = \ln |u| + C = \ln |f(x)| + C
$$
(provided $f(x)$ maintains a constant sign—i.e., does not cross zero—on the domain of integration).

## Theoretical Derivations of the Integrals of $\tan x$ and $\cot x$

Prior to introducing the logarithmic integral rule $\int \frac{du}{u} = \ln |u| + C$, we knew how to integrate $\sin x$ and $\cos x$, as well as $\sec^2 x$ and $\csc^2 x$, but the basic trigonometric integrals $\int \tan x \, dx$ and $\int \cot x \, dx$ could not be evaluated in closed form! By rewriting tangent and cotangent as ratios of sine and cosine, we can recognize both integrals as instances of the logarithmic pattern $\int \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} \, dx$.

### Integral of the Tangent Function

To integrate $\tan x$, we first rewrite it in terms of sine and cosine as $\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}$:
$$
\int \tan x \, dx = \int \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \, dx
$$
Notice that the derivative of the denominator $\cos x$ is $-\sin x$, which matches the numerator up to a minus sign! Let $u = \cos x$, so that $du = -\sin x \, dx$, or equivalently $\sin x \, dx = -du$. Substituting these into the integral gives:
$$
\int \tan x \, dx = \int \frac{-du}{u} = -\int \frac{du}{u} = -\ln |u| + C = -\ln |\cos x| + C
$$
We can also express this answer without the leading minus sign by applying the Reciprocal Rule of logarithms: since $-\ln |\cos x| = \ln |\cos x|^{-1} = \ln \left|\frac{1}{\cos x}\right| = \ln |\sec x|$, we obtain two equally useful forms for the integral of the tangent function:
$$
\int \tan u \, du = -\ln |\cos u| + C = \ln |\sec u| + C
$$

### Integral of the Cotangent Function

Similarly, to integrate $\cot x$, we express it as the quotient $\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}$:
$$
\int \cot x \, dx = \int \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} \, dx
$$
Here the numerator $\cos x$ is the exact derivative of the denominator $\sin x$. Setting $u = \sin x$ and $du = \cos x \, dx$ transforms the integral directly into:
$$
\int \cot x \, dx = \int \frac{du}{u} = \ln |u| + C = \ln |\sin x| + C
$$
Just as before, if we wish to write this in terms of cosecant, applying the Reciprocal Rule $\ln |\sin x| = -\ln \left|\frac{1}{\sin x}\right| = -\ln |\csc x|$ gives:
$$
\int \cot u \, du = \ln |\sin u| + C = -\ln |\csc u| + C
$$

> [!summary] Summary of Boxed Integrals
> $$
> \int \tan u \, du = -\ln |\cos u| + C = \ln |\sec u| + C
> $$
> $$
> \int \cot u \, du = \ln |\sin u| + C = -\ln |\csc u| + C
> $$

---

# 6.3 The Exponential Function

## The Inverse of the Natural Logarithm and the Number $e$

### Invertibility of the Natural Logarithm

In Section 6.2, we defined the natural logarithm for all $x > 0$ via the definite integral:
$$
\ln x = \int_1^x \frac{1}{t} \, dt
$$
Why are we guaranteed that $\ln x$ has an inverse function? By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, the derivative of $\ln x$ is:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} \ln x = \frac{1}{x}
$$
For every $x$ in the domain $(0, \infty)$, the derivative $\frac{1}{x}$ is strictly positive. Because its derivative is strictly positive across its entire domain, $\ln x$ is a strictly increasing function. By Theorem 1 of Section 6.1, any strictly increasing function is one-to-one and therefore possesses a unique inverse function, which we initially denote by $\ln^{-1}$.

Because an inverse function swaps inputs and outputs, the domain and range of $\ln^{-1}$ are the exact reverses of the domain and range of $\ln$:
- $\operatorname{Dom}(\ln^{-1}) = \operatorname{Ran}(\ln) = (-\infty, \infty)$
- $\operatorname{Ran}(\ln^{-1}) = \operatorname{Dom}(\ln) = (0, \infty)$

In other words, $\ln^{-1} x$ accepts any real number $x$ as an input and always produces a strictly positive output. We can also determine the end behavior of $\ln^{-1} x$ directly from the asymptotic limits of $\ln x$:
- Since $\lim_{x \to \infty} \ln x = \infty$, the inverse function grows without bound as $x$ increases:
  $$
  \lim_{x \to \infty} \ln^{-1} x = \infty
  $$
- Since $\lim_{x \to 0^+} \ln x = -\infty$, the inverse function approaches zero as $x$ retreats toward $-\infty$:
  $$
  \lim_{x \to -\infty} \ln^{-1} x = 0
  $$

Geometrically, this second limit tells us that the line $y = 0$ (the negative $x$-axis) serves as a horizontal asymptote for the graph of $y = \ln^{-1} x$, which is the reflection of $y = \ln x$ across the line $y = x$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927161504.webp)

### Definition of the Number $e$

Since $\ln x$ is continuous, strictly increasing, and ranges from $-\infty$ to $\infty$, the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantees that it passes through every real height—including $y = 1$—at a single, unique point. We define the fundamental constant $e$ through this exact crossing point.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Number $e$
> The number $e$ is the unique real number whose natural logarithm equals $1$:
> $$
> e = \ln^{-1}(1)
> $$
> Equivalently, $e$ satisfies the integral condition:
> $$
> \ln e = 1 \iff \int_1^e \frac{1}{t} \, dt = 1
> $$

What does $e$ represent geometrically? Look at the integral above: $e$ is the unique $x$-coordinate along the positive horizontal axis such that the area under the hyperbola $y = 1/t$ between $t = 1$ and $t = e$ is equal to $1$ square unit.

Analytically, $e$ is an irrational, transcendental number. As we will see when studying infinite series, its numerical value can be computed as the limit of Taylor polynomials:
$$
e = \lim_{n \to \infty} \left( 1 + 1 + \frac{1}{2!} + \frac{1}{3!} + \dots + \frac{1}{n!} \right) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{1}{k!}
$$
Evaluated to fifteen decimal places, this series converges to:
$$
e \approx 2.718281828459045
$$

### The Function $y = e^x$

How does the inverse function $\ln^{-1} x$ connect to raising the number $e$ to a power? For any rational number $x$, elementary algebra already defines powers of the positive base $e$ through multiplication, reciprocals, and roots:
$$
e^2 = e \cdot e, \quad e^{-2} = \frac{1}{e^2}, \quad e^{1/2} = \sqrt{e}, \quad e^{p/q} = \sqrt[q]{e^p}
$$
Because $e > 0$, every rational power $e^x$ is strictly positive, so we can take its natural logarithm. Using the power rule for rational exponents from Section 6.2 together with the fact that $\ln e = 1$, we find:
$$
\ln(e^x) = x \ln e = x \cdot 1 = x
$$
Now, apply the inverse function $\ln^{-1}$ to both sides of $\ln(e^x) = x$. Since $\ln^{-1}(\ln u) = u$, we obtain:
$$
e^x = \ln^{-1} x \quad \text{for all rational } x
$$

Here is the crucial question: what does $e^x$ mean when $x$ is an *irrational* number, such as $e^{\sqrt{2}}$ or $e^\pi$? Elementary algebra gives us no way to multiply $e$ by itself $\sqrt{2}$ times. However, the function $\ln^{-1} x$ is already well-defined, smooth, and continuous for *every* real number $x \in (-\infty, \infty)$, and it agrees with algebraic exponentiation at every rational $x$. We therefore use $\ln^{-1} x$ to define $e^x$ across the entire real line.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Natural Exponential Function
> For every real number $x$, the **natural exponential function** $e^x$ is defined as the inverse of the natural logarithm:
> $$
> e^x = \ln^{-1} x
> $$
> 
> **Domain and Range:**
> - $\operatorname{Dom}(e^x) = (-\infty, \infty)$
> - $\operatorname{Ran}(e^x) = (0, \infty)$

### Inverse Equations Involving $\ln x$ and $e^x$

Because $f(x) = e^x$ and $g(x) = \ln x$ are mutually inverse functions ($e^x = \ln^{-1} x$ and $\ln x = (e^x)^{-1}$), composing them in either order cancels both operations and returns the original input.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Inverse Identities
> For all $x > 0$:
> $$
> e^{\ln x} = x
> $$
> and for all $x \in (-\infty, \infty)$:
> $$
> \ln(e^x) = x
> $$

These two identities give us a reliable pair of operating rules whenever we need to isolate a variable trapped inside a logarithm or an exponent:
1. **Removing logarithms:** To eliminate a natural logarithm from an equation, exponentiate both sides with base $e$:
   $$
   \ln y = u(x) \implies e^{\ln y} = e^{u(x)} \implies y = e^{u(x)}
   $$
2. **Removing exponentials:** To eliminate an exponential from an equation, take the natural logarithm of both sides (provided $v(x) > 0$):
   $$
   e^y = v(x) \implies \ln(e^y) = \ln(v(x)) \implies y = \ln(v(x))
   $$

## Laws of Exponents

Even though we defined $e^x$ for arbitrary real exponents as the inverse function $\ln^{-1} x$, does it still obey all the familiar algebraic laws of exponents? Remarkably, yes—and each law follows directly from the corresponding property of logarithms established in Section 6.2.

> [!summary] Summary: Laws of Exponents for $e^x$
> For all real numbers $x, x_1, x_2$:
> 1. **Product Rule:**
>    $$
>    e^{x_1} \cdot e^{x_2} = e^{x_1 + x_2}
>    $$
> 2. **Reciprocal Rule:**
>    $$
>    e^{-x} = \frac{1}{e^x}
>    $$
> 3. **Quotient Rule:**
>    $$
>    \frac{e^{x_1}}{e^{x_2}} = e^{x_1 - x_2}
>    $$
> 4. **Power of a Power Rule:**
>    $$
>    (e^{x_1})^{x_2} = e^{x_1 x_2} = (e^{x_2})^{x_1}
>    $$

### Proof of Law 1: Product Rule

**Proof:** Let us set:
$$
y_1 = e^{x_1} \quad \text{and} \quad y_2 = e^{x_2}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both equations converts our exponentials into logarithmic expressions:
$$
x_1 = \ln y_1 \quad \text{and} \quad x_2 = \ln y_2
$$
Adding these two equations together gives:
$$
x_1 + x_2 = \ln y_1 + \ln y_2
$$
Now we apply the Product Rule for logarithms ($\ln y_1 + \ln y_2 = \ln(y_1 y_2)$) to combine the right-hand side:
$$
x_1 + x_2 = \ln(y_1 y_2)
$$
Exponentiating both sides with base $e$ removes the logarithm:
$$
e^{x_1 + x_2} = e^{\ln(y_1 y_2)} = y_1 y_2
$$
Finally, substituting back $y_1 = e^{x_1}$ and $y_2 = e^{x_2}$ yields:
$$
e^{x_1 + x_2} = e^{x_1} \cdot e^{x_2}
$$
This establishes Law 1. $\blacksquare$

### Proof of Law 2: Reciprocal Rule

**Proof:** Setting $x_1 = x$ and $x_2 = -x$ in Law 1 gives:
$$
e^x \cdot e^{-x} = e^{x + (-x)} = e^0
$$
Since $\ln 1 = 0$, we have $e^0 = \ln^{-1}(0) = 1$, so the equation becomes:
$$
e^x \cdot e^{-x} = 1
$$
Because $e^x > 0$ for all real $x$, we can divide both sides by $e^x$ without risk of dividing by zero:
$$
e^{-x} = \frac{1}{e^x}
$$
This establishes Law 2. $\blacksquare$

### Proof of Law 3: Quotient Rule

**Proof:** Combining Law 1 and Law 2 allows us to rewrite the quotient as a product:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{e^{x_1}}{e^{x_2}} &= e^{x_1} \cdot \frac{1}{e^{x_2}} \\
&= e^{x_1} \cdot e^{-x_2} \\
&= e^{x_1 + (-x_2)} \\
&= e^{x_1 - x_2}
\end{aligned}
$$
This establishes Law 3. $\blacksquare$

### Proof of Law 4: Power of a Power Rule

**Proof:** Let us set:
$$
y = (e^{x_1})^{x_2}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
$$
\ln y = \ln\left( (e^{x_1})^{x_2} \right)
$$
Applying the Power Rule for logarithms ($\ln(a^r) = r \ln a$) brings the outer exponent $x_2$ out front:
$$
\ln y = x_2 \ln(e^{x_1})
$$
Since $\ln(e^{x_1}) = x_1$, this simplifies to:
$$
\ln y = x_2 \cdot x_1 = x_1 x_2
$$
Exponentiating both sides with base $e$ isolates $y$:
$$
e^{\ln y} = e^{x_1 x_2} \implies y = e^{x_1 x_2}
$$
Therefore, $(e^{x_1})^{x_2} = e^{x_1 x_2}$. By the commutativity of multiplication in $\mathbb{R}$, $x_1 x_2 = x_2 x_1$, which also gives:
$$
(e^{x_1})^{x_2} = e^{x_1 x_2} = (e^{x_2})^{x_1}
$$
This establishes Law 4. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Forward Reference on the Power Rule for Logarithms
> Notice that to prove Law 4 for arbitrary real exponents $x_2$, we used the logarithmic property $\ln(a^r) = r\ln a$. In Section 6.2 we proved this property only for rational exponents $r$. In Section 6.4, we will rigorously define $a^r$ for all real $r$ and prove $\ln(a^r) = r\ln a$ in two independent ways, completing the logical foundation for real exponents.

## Differentiation of the Exponential Function

### Differentiability and the Derivative of $e^x$

Before computing the derivative of $y = e^x$, how do we know $e^x$ is differentiable in the first place? Recall that $f(x) = \ln x$ is differentiable on $(0, \infty)$ and its derivative is:
$$
f'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \neq 0
$$
Because $f'(x)$ is never zero on $(0, \infty)$, the Inverse Function Derivative Theorem (Theorem 1 of Section 6.1) guarantees that the inverse function $y = e^x$ is differentiable everywhere on $(-\infty, \infty)$.

With differentiability assured, we can find the derivative of $y = e^x$ using implicit differentiation. Since $e^x > 0$, we have $y > 0$, and taking the natural logarithm of both sides yields:
$$
\ln y = x
$$
Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}[\ln y] = \frac{d}{dx}[x]
$$
Applying the Chain Rule to the left-hand side produces:
$$
\frac{1}{y} \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
$$
Multiplying both sides by $y$ and substituting back $y = e^x$, we arrive at one of the most celebrated results in calculus:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = y \implies \frac{d}{dx} e^x = e^x
$$

> [!theorem] Derivative of $e^x$
> The natural exponential function $e^x$ is its own derivative:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} e^x = e^x
> $$

In other words, at every point $(x, e^x)$ on the graph of $y = e^x$, the slope of the tangent line is equal to the height of the curve itself!

> [!note] Theoretical Characterization of $e^x$
> The exponential function $y = e^x$ is the only nonzero function (up to scalar multiplication) that is identical to its own derivative. That is, the general solution to the differential equation:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = y
> $$
> is $y = C e^x$ for an arbitrary constant $C$.

### Chain Rule Generalization and Rate-of-Change Interpretation

What if the exponent is a composite function $u = u(x)$ rather than just $x$? Applying the Chain Rule immediately extends our derivative formula:
$$
\frac{d}{dx} e^u = \frac{d}{du}[e^u] \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = e^u \frac{du}{dx}
$$

> [!theorem] General Derivative Formula for $e^u$
> If $u$ is a differentiable function of $x$, then:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} e^u = e^u \frac{du}{dx}
> $$

In differential notation, this relationship takes the compact form:
$$
d(e^u) = e^u \, du
$$
In particular, when $u = x$, a small change $dx$ in $x$ produces an approximate change in $y = e^x$ given by:
$$
\Delta y \approx dy = e^x \, dx
$$
Looking at this differential relationship from two angles reveals why $e^x$ is the natural prototype for growth models:
- **Absolute rate of change:** Near any point $x$, the instantaneous rate of change $\frac{dy}{dx}$ equals the current function value $e^x$. The larger $e^x$ gets, the faster it grows.
- **Relative (percentage) rate of change:** Dividing the rate of change by the function value itself shows that the relative growth rate of $y = e^x$ is constant:
  $$
  \frac{1}{y} \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{e^x}{e^x} = 1
  $$
  This represents a constant continuous growth rate of $100\%$ per unit of $x$.

### Linearization of $e^x$ at $x = 0$

How does $e^x$ behave for very small values of $x$ near $0$? We can construct the standard linear (tangent line) approximation of $f(x) = e^x$ centered at $a = 0$. Since both the function value and the slope at $x = 0$ equal $1$ ($f(0) = e^0 = 1$ and $f'(0) = e^0 = 1$), the linearization is:
$$
L(x) = f(0) + f'(0)(x - 0) = 1 + 1 \cdot x = 1 + x
$$

> [!theorem] Linear Approximation of $e^x$
> For values of $x$ near $0$, the exponential function is locally approximated by:
> $$
> e^x \approx 1 + x
> $$

Can we say whether $1 + x$ overestimates or underestimates $e^x$? Notice that the second derivative of $f(x) = e^x$ is $f''(x) = e^x > 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. Because the curve $y = e^x$ is strictly concave up everywhere, its tangent line at $x = 0$ must lie strictly below the graph at every point except the point of tangency $(0, 1)$. This gives us a fundamental global inequality:
$$
e^x \ge 1 + x \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
$$
with equality holding if and only if $x = 0$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260929104616.webp)

## Theoretical Foundations: Algebraic vs. Transcendental Classifications

Why is this chapter titled *Transcendental Functions*? To appreciate what makes $\ln x$ and $e^x$ fundamentally different from polynomials, rational functions, and roots, we look at how mathematical analysis classifies numbers and functions according to whether they can be captured by polynomial equations.

### Algebraic vs. Transcendental Numbers

Real and complex numbers are partitioned into two disjoint classes depending on whether they can appear as roots of polynomial equations with rational coefficients.

> [!abstract] Definition: Algebraic and Transcendental Numbers
> - A number $\alpha$ is **algebraic** if it satisfies a polynomial equation of the form:
>   $$
>   a_n \alpha^n + a_{n-1} \alpha^{n-1} + \dots + a_1 \alpha + a_0 = 0
>   $$
>   where the coefficients $a_n, a_{n-1}, \dots, a_0 \in \mathbb{Q}$ are rational numbers and $a_n \neq 0$.
> - A number is **transcendental** if it is not algebraic.

To build intuition for this distinction, consider the following examples and historical milestones:
- **Rational numbers:** Every rational number $p/q$ (with $p, q \in \mathbb{Z}$, $q \neq 0$) is automatically algebraic because it satisfies the first-degree polynomial equation $q x - p = 0$. For instance, $-2$ satisfies $x + 2 = 0$.
- **Irrational radicals:** Many irrational numbers are also algebraic. For example, $\sqrt{3}$ is irrational, yet it is algebraic because it satisfies $x^2 - 3 = 0$.
- **Origin of the term:** Leonhard Euler coined the term *transcendental* to describe quantities that "transcend the power of algebraic methods"—meaning they cannot be produced by any finite combination of algebraic operations and root extractions on rational numbers.
- **Transcendence of $e$ and $\pi$:** Proving that a specific constant is transcendental is notoriously difficult. Charles Hermite first proved that $e$ is transcendental in 1873. Nine years later, in 1882, Ferdinand von Lindemann proved that $\pi$ is transcendental—a landmark result that settled an ancient Greek problem by proving the impossibility of "squaring the circle" using only a compass and straightedge.

### Algebraic vs. Transcendental Functions

This exact same algebraic-versus-transcendental distinction extends from numbers to functions.

> [!abstract] Definition: Algebraic and Transcendental Functions
> - A function $y = f(x)$ is **algebraic** if it satisfies a polynomial equation in $y$ whose coefficients are polynomials in $x$:
>   $$
>   P_n(x) y^n + P_{n-1}(x) y^{n-1} + \dots + P_1(x) y + P_0(x) = 0
>   $$
>   where each $P_i(x)$ is a polynomial in $x$ with rational coefficients.
> - A function is **transcendental** if it is not algebraic.

Which functions belong to the algebraic class? All polynomials and rational functions are algebraic, as are all functions constructed by taking finite combinations of sums, differences, products, quotients, rational powers, and rational roots of polynomials. For instance, the function $y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+1}}$ is algebraic because squaring both sides and clearing the denominator shows that it satisfies:
$$
(x + 1) y^2 - 1 = 0
$$
which has the required polynomial form with $P_2(x) = x + 1$, $P_1(x) = 0$, and $P_0(x) = -1$.

By contrast, functions that cannot satisfy any polynomial relation with polynomial coefficients are **transcendental**. The primary families of elementary transcendental functions include:
- The six elementary trigonometric functions ($\sin x$, $\cos x$, $\tan x$, $\cot x$, $\sec x$, $\csc x$), whose periodicity alone prevents them from satisfying a nonzero polynomial equation.
- The inverse trigonometric functions ($\arcsin x$, $\arccos x$, $\arctan x$, etc.).
- The natural logarithmic function ($\ln x$).
- The natural exponential function ($e^x$).

## Integration Involving the Exponential Function

### The Indefinite and Definite Integral of $e^u$
