Because integration is the inverse operation of differentiation, reversing our derivative formula $\frac{d}{du}[e^u] = e^u$ immediately gives us the integration rule for the exponential function. Just as $e^u$ is its own derivative, it is also its own antiderivative!

> [!theorem] Integral of $e^u$
> If $u$ is a differentiable function of $x$, then:
> $$
> \int e^u \, du = e^u + C
> $$

When evaluating a definite integral via the substitution $u = g(x)$ and $du = g'(x) \, dx$, we transform the limits of integration accordingly:
$$
\int_a^b e^{g(x)} g'(x) \, dx = \int_{g(a)}^{g(b)} e^u \, du = \left[ e^u \right]_{g(a)}^{g(b)} = e^{g(b)} - e^{g(a)}
$$

### Separable Differential Equations and Initial Value Problems

The exponential function frequently appears in first-order differential equations of the form:
$$
e^y \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x)
$$
paired with an initial condition $y(x_0) = y_0$. Notice how all factors involving $y$ and $\frac{dy}{dx}$ sit on the left-hand side, while the right-hand side depends only on $x$. Equations with this structure belong to an important class called separable differential equations.

> [!note] Concept: Separable Differential Equations
> A **separable differential equation** is a first-order ordinary differential equation that can be factored so that all terms involving the dependent variable $y$ appear on one side and all terms involving the independent variable $x$ appear on the other.
> 
> **General Forms:** A first-order equation is separable if it can be written as:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x) h(y)
> $$
> or, expressed in differential form:
> $$
> M(x)\,dx + N(y)\,dy = 0
> $$
> where $g(x)$ and $M(x)$ depend solely on $x$, and $h(y)$ and $N(y)$ depend solely on $y$.

How do we systematically solve an initial value problem of the form $e^y \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x)$ with $y(x_0) = y_0$? We follow a five-step procedure:
1. **Integration:** Integrate both sides with respect to $x$ and use the substitution $dy = \frac{dy}{dx} \, dx$ on the left:
   $$
   \int e^y \frac{dy}{dx} \, dx = \int g(x) \, dx \implies \int e^y \, dy = \int g(x) \, dx
   $$
   Evaluating both integrals yields the implicit general solution:
   $$
   e^y = G(x) + C
   $$
   where $G'(x) = g(x)$ is an antiderivative of $g(x)$ and $C$ is the constant of integration.
2. **Applying the Initial Condition:** Substitute the initial point $(x_0, y_0)$ to uniquely determine $C$:
   $$
   e^{y_0} = G(x_0) + C \implies C = e^{y_0} - G(x_0)
   $$
   Inserting this value of $C$ fixes the particular implicit relation $e^y = G(x) + C$.
3. **Explicit Isolation of $y$:** Take the natural logarithm of both sides to solve explicitly for $y$:
   $$
   \ln(e^y) = \ln(G(x) + C) \implies y = \ln(G(x) + C)
   $$
4. **Domain of the Solution:** Because the natural logarithm is defined only for positive inputs, the explicit solution $y = \ln(G(x) + C)$ is valid on the largest open connected interval containing $x_0$ on which the argument remains strictly positive:
   $$
   \operatorname{Dom}(y) = \{ x \in \mathbb{R} \mid G(x) + C > 0 \}
   $$
5. **Verification:** We can always check our solution by differentiating $y = \ln(G(x) + C)$ and confirming that it reproduces the original differential equation:
   $$
   e^y \frac{dy}{dx} = e^{\ln(G(x) + C)} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(G(x) + C)] = (G(x) + C) \cdot \frac{G'(x)}{G(x) + C} = G'(x) = g(x)
   $$

## Advanced Theoretical Results and Geometric Dualities

### Complementary Area Duality for Inverse Functions

Because $y = \ln x$ and $x = e^y$ describe the exact same curve in the $xy$-plane viewed from perpendicular axes, the areas beneath their graphs fit together like two puzzle pieces inside a rectangle.

> [!theorem] Integral Area Duality
> For any real number $a > 1$:
> $$
> \int_1^a \ln x \, dx + \int_0^{\ln a} e^y \, dy = a \ln a
> $$

**Proof:** Let us see how this identity emerges geometrically and analytically from the graph of $y = \ln x$. As $x$ varies from $1$ to $a$, the height $y = \ln x$ varies from $\ln 1 = 0$ to $\ln a$:
- The first integral, $\int_1^a \ln x \, dx$, measures the area of the region lying **below** the curve $y = \ln x$ and above the $x$-axis from $x = 1$ to $x = a$.
- Solving $y = \ln x$ for $x$ gives $x = e^y$. Thus, integrating with respect to $y$ in the second integral, $\int_0^{\ln a} e^y \, dy$, measures the area of the region lying to the **left** of the curve $x = e^y$ (that is, above $y = \ln x$) and to the right of the $y$-axis from $y = 0$ to $y = \ln a$.

As illustrated in the figure below, these two non-overlapping regions partition the entire bounding rectangle $[0, a] \times [0, \ln a]$ in the first quadrant! Therefore, the sum of the two integrals must equal the total area of the circumscribed rectangle of base $a$ and height $\ln a$:
$$
\text{Total Area} = \text{base} \times \text{height} = a \cdot \ln a = a \ln a \quad \blacksquare
$$

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927172039.webp)

### The Geometric, Logarithmic, and Arithmetic Mean Inequality

The geometry of the curve $y = e^x$ also yields an elegant calculus proof of a famous three-part inequality comparing the **geometric mean**, the **logarithmic mean**, and the **arithmetic mean** of two positive numbers.

First, recall that for $f(x) = e^x$, both the first and second derivatives are strictly positive across the entire real line:
$$
f'(x) = e^x, \quad f''(x) = e^x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
$$
Because $f''(x) > 0$ everywhere, the graph of $y = e^x$ is **strictly concave up** on every interval.

Now let $0 < a < b$, and consider the definite integral of $f(x) = e^x$ over the interval $[\ln a, \ln b]$. The width of this interval is:
$$
\Delta x = \ln b - \ln a = \ln\left(\frac{b}{a}\right) > 0
$$
and its midpoint is:
$$
m = \frac{\ln a + \ln b}{2}
$$
Because $y = e^x$ is strictly concave up on $[\ln a, \ln b]$, we can trap the exact area $\int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx$ between a tangent-line underestimate and a secant-line overestimate:
1. **Midpoint Rule (Tangent Underestimate):** The tangent line drawn to $y = e^x$ at the midpoint $x = m$ lies strictly below the curve everywhere except at the point of tangency. Moreover, because the midpoint $m$ is horizontally centered in $[\ln a, \ln b]$, the trapezoid under this sloped tangent line has the exact same area as the rectangle of height $f(m)$ and width $\ln b - \ln a$. Consequently, the midpoint rectangle strictly underestimates the integral:
   $$
   f(m) \cdot (\ln b - \ln a) < \int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx
   $$
   Substituting $f(m) = e^{\frac{\ln a + \ln b}{2}}$ gives:
   $$
   e^{\frac{\ln a + \ln b}{2}} (\ln b - \ln a) < \int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx
   $$
2. **Trapezoidal Rule (Secant Overestimate):** The secant line segment connecting the left endpoint $(\ln a, e^{\ln a})$ to the right endpoint $(\ln b, e^{\ln b})$ lies strictly above the concave-up curve across the open interval $(\ln a, \ln b)$. Therefore, the area of the secant trapezoid strictly overestimates the integral:
   $$
   \int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx < \frac{e^{\ln a} + e^{\ln b}}{2} (\ln b - \ln a)
   $$

Combining both geometric bounds places the integral inside a strict double inequality:
$$
e^{\frac{\ln a + \ln b}{2}} (\ln b - \ln a) < \int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx < \frac{e^{\ln a} + e^{\ln b}}{2} (\ln b - \ln a)
$$

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927173128.webp)

Now let us simplify each of the three expressions algebraically:
- **Central integral:** By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus and the inverse identity $e^{\ln x} = x$:
  $$
  \int_{\ln a}^{\ln b} e^x \, dx = \left[ e^x \right]_{\ln a}^{\ln b} = e^{\ln b} - e^{\ln a} = b - a
  $$
- **Left factor (Geometric Mean):** Using logarithmic and exponential laws:
  $$
  e^{\frac{\ln a + \ln b}{2}} = e^{\frac{1}{2}\ln(ab)} = \left( e^{\ln(ab)} \right)^{1/2} = \sqrt{ab}
  $$
- **Right factor (Arithmetic Mean):** Simplifying $e^{\ln a} = a$ and $e^{\ln b} = b$:
  $$
  \frac{e^{\ln a} + e^{\ln b}}{2} = \frac{a + b}{2}
  $$

Substituting these three simplified expressions back into our double inequality yields:
$$
\sqrt{ab} \, (\ln b - \ln a) < b - a < \frac{a + b}{2} \, (\ln b - \ln a)
$$
Finally, since $0 < a < b$ implies $\ln b - \ln a > 0$, we can divide all three parts by $\ln b - \ln a$ while preserving the strict inequalities:
$$
\sqrt{ab} < \frac{b - a}{\ln b - \ln a} < \frac{a + b}{2}
$$

> [!theorem] The Geometric, Logarithmic, and Arithmetic Mean Inequality
> For any two distinct positive numbers $0 < a < b$:
> $$
> \sqrt{ab} < \frac{b - a}{\ln b - \ln a} < \frac{a + b}{2}
> $$
> In words, the logarithmic mean of two distinct positive numbers is strictly bounded between their geometric mean and their arithmetic mean:
> $$
> \text{Geometric Mean} < \text{Logarithmic Mean} < \text{Arithmetic Mean}
> $$

> [!note] Theoretical Significance of the Logarithmic Mean
> The middle quantity in this inequality,
> $$
> L(a, b) = \frac{b - a}{\ln b - \ln a}
> $$
> is called the **Logarithmic Mean** of $a$ and $b$. It arises naturally across engineering and physical transport phenomena—most notably in heat exchanger design as the *Logarithmic Mean Temperature Difference* (LMTD) and in mass transfer across diffusion membranes—whenever a driving force decays exponentially between two boundary states.

> [!note] Intuition: Area of a Trapezoid via the Midpoint Height
> In Step 1 of the derivation above, we used a neat geometric fact: the area under any straight line (such as the tangent line at $x = m$) over an interval equals its height at the midpoint times the interval width. In general, for a trapezoid with parallel sides of lengths $l_1$ and $l_2$ and width (or height) $h$, the standard area formula is $\frac{l_1 + l_2}{2} \cdot h$. Because the cross-sectional length halfway between the two parallel sides is the average $l_{\text{mid}} = \frac{l_1 + l_2}{2}$, the area of any trapezoid is simply:
> $$
> \text{Area} = l_{\text{mid}} \cdot h
> $$
> Visually, rotating the top edge about its midpoint slices off a small triangle on the taller side and rotates it to perfectly fill the missing triangle on the shorter side, turning the trapezoid into a rectangle of height $l_{\text{mid}}$ and base $h$.

---

# 6.4 $a^x$ and $\log_a x$

## The General Exponential Function $a^x$

### Definition of Arbitrary Real Powers

How do we raise a positive number $a$ to an arbitrary real power $x$? In elementary algebra, we build up $a^n$ step by step for integer and rational exponents through repeated multiplication, reciprocals, and roots:
$$
a^n = \underbrace{a \cdot a \dots a}_{n \text{ factors}}, \quad a^{-n} = \frac{1}{a^n}, \quad a^{p/q} = \sqrt[q]{a^p}
$$
However, elementary algebra hits a wall when we encounter an irrational exponent such as $2^{\sqrt{3}}$ or $5^\pi$. Because an irrational number cannot be expressed as a ratio of integers $p/q$, we cannot interpret $a^x$ as taking a $q$-th root of a $p$-th power. How can we extend exponentiation so that $a^x$ makes rigorous sense for every real number $x$?

The key lies in the natural exponential function $e^x$ and the natural logarithm $\ln x$ that we developed in the preceding sections. Because $e^u$ is already defined for all real numbers $u \in (-\infty, \infty)$, and because any positive base $a > 0$ can be written as $a = e^{\ln a}$, we can rewrite any rational power $a^x$ as $(e^{\ln a})^x = e^{x \ln a}$. Notice that the right-hand side, $e^{x \ln a}$, is well-defined whether $x$ is rational or irrational! We therefore adopt this identity as our formal definition of exponentiation for any positive base $a > 0$ and any real exponent $x$.

> [!abstract] Definition: General Exponential Function $a^x$
> For any base $a > 0$ and any real exponent $x$:
> $$
> a^x = e^{x \ln a}
> $$
> 
> **Domain and Range:**
> - $\operatorname{Dom}(a^x) = (-\infty, \infty)$
> - For $a \neq 1$: $\operatorname{Ran}(a^x) = (0, \infty)$
> - For $a = 1$: $1^x = e^{x \ln 1} = e^{x \cdot 0} = e^0 = 1$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$, so $\operatorname{Ran}(1^x) = \{1\}$

In other words, to compute $a^x$, we scale the natural logarithm of the base by $x$ and feed the result into the natural exponential function.

> [!note] Justification of the Definition
> When $x$ is a rational number, the equation $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$ is a theorem that we have already proved using the rational power rule for $\ln$. When $x$ is irrational, this same equation serves as the *definition* of what the symbol $a^x$ means, seamlessly filling in the gaps between rational exponents so that $a^x$ becomes a smooth, continuous function on the entire real line.
> 
> If defining irrational powers via $e^{x \ln a}$ feels like a shortcut, take a look at the final subsection of this section (*Another Path*), where we construct $a^x$ for irrational $x$ directly as a limit of rational powers and show that both approaches yield the exact same function.

### Laws of Exponents for General Bases

Does our new definition of $a^x$ obey the familiar rules of exponents from algebra even when the exponents are irrational? Because $a^x$ is built directly out of the natural exponential function $e^u$, it inherits all the algebraic laws of exponents automatically.

> [!summary] Summary: Laws of Exponents for $a > 0$
> For any positive base $a > 0$ and any real numbers $x$ and $y$:
> 1. **Product Rule:**
>    $$
>    a^x \cdot a^y = a^{x+y}
>    $$
> 2. **Reciprocal Rule:**
>    $$
>    a^{-x} = \frac{1}{a^x}
>    $$
> 3. **Quotient Rule:**
>    $$
>    \frac{a^x}{a^y} = a^{x-y}
>    $$
> 4. **Power of a Power Rule:**
>    $$
>    (a^x)^y = a^{xy} = (a^y)^x
>    $$

Let us verify each of these four laws directly from the definition $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$.

**Proof of Law 1 (Product Rule):**
Using the definition of $a^x$ together with the additive exponent law $e^{u_1} \cdot e^{u_2} = e^{u_1 + u_2}$ for the natural exponential function, we can combine the two factors into a single power of $e$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
a^x \cdot a^y &= e^{x \ln a} \cdot e^{y \ln a} \\
&= e^{x \ln a + y \ln a} \\
&= e^{(x+y) \ln a} \\
&= a^{x+y}
\end{aligned}
$$
This establishes Law 1. $\blacksquare$

**Proof of Law 2 (Reciprocal Rule):**
If we set $y = -x$ in Law 1, the exponents cancel to zero:
$$
a^x \cdot a^{-x} = a^{x + (-x)} = a^0 = e^{0 \ln a} = e^0 = 1
$$
Since $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$ is strictly positive for all $x$, we can divide both sides by $a^x$ to obtain:
$$
a^{-x} = \frac{1}{a^x}
$$
This establishes Law 2. $\blacksquare$

**Proof of Law 3 (Quotient Rule):**
We can rewrite division by $a^y$ as multiplication by its reciprocal and then apply Law 2 followed by Law 1:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{a^x}{a^y} &= a^x \cdot \frac{1}{a^y} \\
&= a^x \cdot a^{-y} \\
&= a^{x + (-y)} \\
&= a^{x-y}
\end{aligned}
$$
This establishes Law 3. $\blacksquare$

**Proof of Law 4 (Power of a Power Rule):**
Applying the definition of general exponentiation to the positive base $a^x$ raised to the exponent $y$, we write:
$$
(a^x)^y = e^{y \ln(a^x)}
$$
Because $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$, taking the natural logarithm of both sides cancels the exponential and gives:
$$
\ln(a^x) = \ln\left(e^{x \ln a}\right) = x \ln a
$$
Substituting this back into the exponent on the right-hand side yields:
$$
(a^x)^y = e^{y(x \ln a)} = e^{(xy) \ln a} = a^{xy}
$$
Finally, by the commutativity of multiplication of real numbers ($xy = yx$), we also have:
$$
a^{xy} = a^{yx} = (a^y)^x
$$
This establishes Law 4. $\blacksquare$

## The Power Rule in Final Form

### Generalizing the Power Identity for Logarithms

Back in Section 6.2, we proved the power rule for the natural logarithm, $\ln(x^r) = r \ln x$, under the restriction that $r$ had to be a rational number. Now that $x^n$ is defined for any arbitrary real exponent $n$, we can immediately lift that restriction.

> [!theorem] Logarithmic Power Identity for Arbitrary Real Exponents
> For any $x > 0$ and any real number $n \in \mathbb{R}$:
> $$
> \ln(x^n) = n \ln x
> $$

**Proof:**
By the definition of $x^n$ with base $x > 0$ and real exponent $n$, we have:
$$
x^n = e^{n \ln x}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides and applying the inverse identity $\ln(e^u) = u$, we find:
$$
\ln(x^n) = \ln\left(e^{n \ln x}\right) = n \ln x \cdot \ln e = n \ln x
$$
This completes the proof. $\blacksquare$

### Derivation of the Derivative of $x^n$

With $x^n$ defined for every real number $n$, we are ready to answer a fundamental question in calculus: does the familiar Power Rule $\frac{d}{dx}(x^n) = n x^{n-1}$ still hold when $n$ is an irrational number like $\sqrt{2}$ or $\pi$?

Let us differentiate $x^n$ for $x > 0$ by writing $x^n = e^{n \ln x}$ and applying the Chain Rule:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx} x^n &= \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{n \ln x} \right) \\
&= e^{n \ln x} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(n \ln x) \\
&= x^n \cdot \left( n \cdot \frac{1}{x} \right) \\
&= n \cdot \frac{x^n}{x} \\
&= n \cdot x^n \cdot x^{-1} \\
&= n x^{n-1}
\end{aligned}
$$
In the final step, we used the general exponent law $x^n \cdot x^{-1} = x^{n-1}$. The result is remarkably clean: the Power Rule works identically for all real exponents!

### The Power Rule (Final Form)

Combining this derivative with the Chain Rule extends the Power Rule to any positive differentiable function $u(x)$ raised to any real power $n$.

> [!theorem] Power Rule for Arbitrary Real Exponents (Final Form)
> If $u$ is a positive differentiable function of $x$ and $n$ is any real number, then $u^n$ is a differentiable function of $x$ and:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} u^n = n u^{n-1} \frac{du}{dx}
> $$
> In differential form:
> $$
> d(u^n) = n u^{n-1} \, du
> $$

> [!note] Completeness of the Power Rule
> Stepping back, notice how the development of the Power Rule across calculus traces four progressive stages of generality:
> 1. **Positive integers $n \in \mathbb{Z}^+$:** Proved in early differential calculus via the Binomial Theorem or repeated application of the Product Rule.
> 2. **All integers $n \in \mathbb{Z}$:** Extended to negative integers via the Quotient Rule.
> 3. **Rational numbers $n \in \mathbb{Q}$:** Extended to fractional powers $n = p/q$ via implicit differentiation of $y^q = x^p$.
> 4. **Arbitrary real numbers $n \in \mathbb{R}$:** Completed here via the natural logarithm and exponential representation $u^n = e^{n \ln u}$.
> 
> This fourth stage brings the story of the Power Rule to completion, confirming that derivatives such as $\frac{d}{dx}\left(x^{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \sqrt{2} x^{\sqrt{2}-1}$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(x^\pi) = \pi x^{\pi-1}$ are mathematically rigorous.

## Differentiation and Analysis of $a^x$

### Derivative of $a^x$ and $a^u$

Now instead of keeping the exponent fixed and varying the base (as in the power function $x^n$), let us keep the base $a > 0$ fixed and vary the exponent $x$. How fast does the exponential function $y = a^x$ change with respect to $x$?

Applying the Chain Rule directly to the definition $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$, where $\ln a$ is a constant, we obtain:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx} a^x &= \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x \ln a} \right) \\
&= e^{x \ln a} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(x \ln a) \\
&= a^x \cdot (\ln a) \\
&= a^x \ln a
\end{aligned}
$$

> [!theorem] Derivative of $a^x$
> For any positive constant $a > 0$:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} a^x = a^x \ln a
> $$

Just like $e^x$, the rate of change of $a^x$ is proportional to its current value $a^x$, with the proportionality constant given by $\ln a$. For instance, $\frac{d}{dx}(2^x) = 2^x \ln 2$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(10^x) = 10^x \ln 10$.

When the exponent is a differentiable function $u = u(x)$ rather than simply $x$, the Chain Rule gives the general differentiation formula.

> [!theorem] Chain Rule for General Exponentials
> If $a > 0$ and $u$ is a differentiable function of $x$, then $a^u$ is a differentiable function of $x$ and:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} a^u = a^u \ln a \frac{du}{dx}
> $$
> In differential form:
> $$
> d(a^u) = a^u \ln a \, du
> $$

> [!note] Why Base $e$ is Natural in Calculus
> Look closely at the factor $\ln a$ in the derivative formula $\frac{d}{dx} a^u = a^u \ln a \frac{du}{dx}$. When we choose the base $a = e$, the scaling factor $\ln a$ becomes $\ln e = 1$, and the formula simplifies to its cleanest possible form:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} e^u = e^u \frac{du}{dx}
> $$
> For any other base $a \neq e$, differentiation introduces an extra numerical multiplier $\ln a \neq 1$. This is why $e$ is the "natural" base for calculus: $e$ is the unique positive base for which the exponential function is identical to its own derivative, crossing the $y$-axis at $(0, 1)$ with a slope of exactly $1$.

### Monotonicity, Concavity, and Asymptotic Behavior of $y = a^x$

Let us use the first and second derivatives of $y = a^x$ to understand the geometry of exponential curves for different choices of the base $a > 0$.

1. **First Derivative and Monotonicity:**
   Because $a^x = e^{x \ln a} > 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$, the sign of the first derivative $\frac{d}{dx} a^x = a^x \ln a$ is determined entirely by the sign of the constant $\ln a$:
   - **Case $a > 1$:** Here $\ln a > 0$, so:
     $$
     \frac{d}{dx} a^x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
     $$
     Thus, $y = a^x$ is **strictly increasing** on $(-\infty, \infty)$, modeling exponential growth.
   - **Case $0 < a < 1$:** Here $\ln a < 0$, so:
     $$
     \frac{d}{dx} a^x < 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
     $$
     Thus, $y = a^x$ is **strictly decreasing** on $(-\infty, \infty)$, modeling exponential decay.
   - **Case $a = 1$:** Here $\ln 1 = 0$, so $\frac{d}{dx}(1^x) = 0$ everywhere, and $y = 1$ is simply a horizontal constant line.

   Because $a^x$ is strictly monotonic for every $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$, it is a one-to-one function on $(-\infty, \infty)$ and therefore possesses a unique inverse function.

2. **Second Derivative and Concavity:**
   Differentiating $\frac{d}{dx} a^x = a^x \ln a$ a second time brings down another factor of $\ln a$:
   $$
   \frac{d^2}{dx^2} a^x = \frac{d}{dx}(a^x \ln a) = (\ln a) \frac{d}{dx}(a^x) = (\ln a)(a^x \ln a) = (\ln a)^2 a^x
   $$
   For every $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$, both $(\ln a)^2$ and $a^x$ are strictly positive:
   $$
   (\ln a)^2 > 0 \quad \text{and} \quad a^x > 0 \implies \frac{d^2}{dx^2} a^x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
   $$
   Because its second derivative is strictly positive everywhere, the graph of $y = a^x$ is **strictly concave up** across its entire domain $(-\infty, \infty)$ for all $a \neq 1$, whether the curve is growing ($a > 1$) or decaying ($0 < a < 1$).

3. **Asymptotic Limits:**
   The end behavior of $y = a^x$ as $x \to \pm\infty$ reverses depending on whether $a > 1$ or $0 < a < 1$:
   - For $a > 1$:
     $$
     \lim_{x \to \infty} a^x = \infty \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to -\infty} a^x = 0
     $$
     The negative $x$-axis ($y = 0$) is a horizontal asymptote as $x \to -\infty$.
   - For $0 < a < 1$:
     $$
     \lim_{x \to \infty} a^x = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to -\infty} a^x = \infty
     $$
     The positive $x$-axis ($y = 0$) is a horizontal asymptote as $x \to \infty$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927184044.webp)

## Differentiation of Variable-Base, Variable-Exponent Functions

What happens when a function has a variable $x$ in *both* the base and the exponent, such as $y = x^x$ or $y = (\sin x)^{\cos x}$? In general, consider a function of the form:
$$
y = f(x)^{g(x)} \quad (f(x) > 0)
$$
Be careful here: neither the Power Rule ($\frac{d}{dx} u^n = n u^{n-1} u'$, which requires a constant exponent $n$) nor the Exponential Rule ($\frac{d}{dx} a^u = a^u \ln a \, u'$, which requires a constant base $a$) applies on its own! Instead, we have two equivalent, reliable techniques for differentiating $y = f(x)^{g(x)}$, both of which use the natural logarithm to bring the variable exponent down to the base level.

### Method 1: Converting to Base $e$

Using the fundamental identity $A = e^{\ln A}$, we first rewrite $f(x)^{g(x)}$ as a base-$e$ exponential:
$$
y = f(x)^{g(x)} = e^{\ln\left( [f(x)]^{g(x)} \right)} = e^{g(x) \ln f(x)}
$$
Now the base is the constant $e$, so we can differentiate with respect to $x$ using the Chain Rule for $e^u$ followed by the Product Rule on the exponent $g(x) \ln f(x)$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{dy}{dx} &= \frac{d}{dx} \left[ e^{g(x) \ln f(x)} \right] \\
&= e^{g(x) \ln f(x)} \cdot \frac{d}{dx} [g(x) \ln f(x)] \\
&= f(x)^{g(x)} \cdot \left( g'(x) \ln f(x) + g(x) \cdot \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} \right)
\end{aligned}
$$

### Method 2: Logarithmic Differentiation

Alternatively, we can take the natural logarithm of both sides of $y = f(x)^{g(x)}$ before differentiating:
$$
\ln y = \ln\left( [f(x)]^{g(x)} \right) = g(x) \ln f(x)
$$
Differentiating both sides implicitly with respect to $x$ using the Chain Rule on the left and the Product Rule on the right gives:
$$
\frac{1}{y} \frac{dy}{dx} = g'(x) \ln f(x) + g(x) \cdot \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}
$$
Multiplying both sides by $y = f(x)^{g(x)}$ yields the exact same formula:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x)^{g(x)} \left( g'(x) \ln f(x) + g(x) \frac{f'(x)}{f(x)} \right)
$$

> [!note] Analytical Decomposition of the Derivative
> If we distribute $f(x)^{g(x)}$ across the two terms inside the parentheses and simplify $f(x)^{g(x)} / f(x) = f(x)^{g(x)-1}$, something remarkable appears:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \left[ f(x)^{g(x)} \right] = \underbrace{g(x) [f(x)]^{g(x)-1} f'(x)}_{\text{Derivative treating exponent as constant (Power Rule)}} + \underbrace{[f(x)]^{g(x)} \ln(f(x)) g'(x)}_{\text{Derivative treating base as constant (Exponential Rule)}}
> $$
> In other words, when both the base and the exponent vary simultaneously, the total rate of change is simply the **sum** of the Power Rule contribution (pretending the exponent is constant) and the Exponential Rule contribution (pretending the base is constant)!

## Integration Involving $a^u$

### The Indefinite and Definite Integral of $a^u$

Because integration is the inverse of differentiation, we can find the antiderivative of $a^u$ by reversing our derivative formula $\frac{d}{dx}(a^u) = a^u \ln a \frac{du}{dx}$. Assuming $a > 0$ and $a \neq 1$ (so that $\ln a \neq 0$), we divide both sides of the derivative identity by the constant $\ln a$:
$$
a^u \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{1}{\ln a} \frac{d}{dx}(a^u)
$$
Integrating both sides with respect to $x$ gives the general integration formula:
$$
\int a^u \frac{du}{dx} \, dx = \frac{1}{\ln a} \int \frac{d}{dx}(a^u) \, dx = \frac{1}{\ln a} a^u + C
$$

> [!theorem] Integral of $a^u$
> For any positive constant $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$:
> $$
> \int a^u \, du = \frac{a^u}{\ln a} + C
> $$

Notice how we divide by $\ln a$ when integrating $a^u$, whereas we multiply by $\ln a$ when differentiating $a^u$. When $a = e$, $\ln e = 1$, and this formula reduces to the familiar $\int e^u \, du = e^u + C$.

For definite integrals involving a substitution $u = g(x)$, we transform the limits of integration just as we did with $e^u$:
$$
\int_{x_1}^{x_2} a^{g(x)} g'(x) \, dx = \int_{g(x_1)}^{g(x_2)} a^u \, du = \left[ \frac{a^u}{\ln a} \right]_{g(x_1)}^{g(x_2)} = \frac{a^{g(x_2)} - a^{g(x_1)}}{\ln a}
$$

## Logarithmic Functions with Base $a$

### Invertibility and Definition of $\log_a x$

Now let us turn to the inverse of $y = a^x$. For any base $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$, we saw earlier that:
1. The exponential function $f(x) = a^x$ is continuous and strictly monotonic on $(-\infty, \infty)$, with range $(0, \infty)$.
2. Its derivative $f'(x) = a^x \ln a$ is strictly nonzero everywhere on $(-\infty, \infty)$.

By the Inverse Function Theorem, $f(x) = a^x$ is guaranteed to have a unique, differentiable inverse function defined on $(0, \infty)$. We call this inverse the **base-$a$ logarithm**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Base $a$ Logarithm Function
> For any positive constant $a \neq 1$, the function $\log_a x$ (read "the logarithm of $x$ to the base $a$") is the inverse of the exponential function $a^x$:
> $$
> y = \log_a x \iff a^y = x
> $$
> 
> **Domain and Range:**
> - $\operatorname{Dom}(\log_a x) = \operatorname{Ran}(a^x) = (0, \infty)$
> - $\operatorname{Ran}(\log_a x) = \operatorname{Dom}(a^x) = (-\infty, \infty)$

In plain words, $\log_a x$ answers the question: *"To what power must we raise the base $a$ in order to get $x$?"* When the base is $a = e$, the base-$e$ logarithm is simply the natural logarithm: $\log_e x = \ln x$.

### Fundamental Inverse Identities and Graphs

Because $f(x) = a^x$ and $f^{-1}(x) = \log_a x$ are inverse functions of one another, composing them in either order undoes the operation and returns the original input.

> [!theorem] Fundamental Inverse Identities for $\log_a x$ and $a^x$
> 1. For all $x > 0$:
>    $$
>    a^{\log_a x} = x
>    $$
> 2. For all $x \in (-\infty, \infty)$:
>    $$
>    \log_a(a^x) = x
>    $$

Geometrically, the graph of $y = \log_a x$ is the reflection of the graph of $y = a^x$ across the diagonal line $y = x$:
- The $y$-intercept $(0, 1)$ of $y = a^x$ reflects across $y = x$ to become the $x$-intercept $(1, 0)$ of $y = \log_a x$ (reflecting the fact that $\log_a 1 = 0$ for every base $a$).
- The horizontal asymptote $y = 0$ of $y = a^x$ reflects across $y = x$ to become the vertical asymptote $x = 0$ (the $y$-axis) of $y = \log_a x$.

![](Pasted%20image%2020260927184318.webp)

### Evaluation of $\log_a x$ and the Change of Base Formula

How do we actually compute a logarithm like $\log_2 10$ or $\log_5 3$ on a scientific calculator that only has a natural logarithm ($\ln$) button, and how do we differentiate or integrate $\log_a x$? The answer is wonderfully simple: every base-$a$ logarithm is just a constant multiple of the natural logarithm $\ln x$.

> [!theorem] Change of Base Formula (to Natural Logarithms)
> For any base $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$, and any $x > 0$:
> $$
> \log_a x = \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} = \frac{1}{\ln a} \cdot \ln x
> $$

**Proof:**
We begin with the fundamental inverse identity for $x > 0$:
$$
a^{\log_a x} = x
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
$$
\ln\left( a^{\log_a x} \right) = \ln x
$$
Applying the power rule for the natural logarithm ($\ln(a^n) = n \ln a$) brings the exponent $\log_a x$ out in front:
$$
\log_a x \cdot \ln a = \ln x
$$
Because $a \neq 1$, we know that $\ln a \neq 0$. Dividing both sides by $\ln a$ yields:
$$
\log_a x = \frac{\ln x}{\ln a}
$$
This completes the proof. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] General Change of Base Between Arbitrary Bases
> The exact same argument allows us to convert a logarithm from base $a$ to any other valid base $b$ ($a, b > 0$ and $a, b \neq 1$). Taking $\log_b$ of both sides of $a^{\log_a x} = x$ gives:
> $$
> \log_a x = \frac{\log_b x}{\log_b a}
> $$
> In particular, setting $x = b$ in this formula and using $\log_b b = 1$ reveals a neat reciprocal relationship between switching bases:
> $$
> \log_a b = \frac{\log_b b}{\log_b a} = \frac{1}{\log_b a}
> $$

### Arithmetic Properties of Base $a$ Logarithms

Because $\log_a x = \frac{1}{\ln a} \ln x$ is simply a constant multiple of $\ln x$, every algebraic law that holds for the natural logarithm carries over unchanged to logarithms of any base $a$.

> [!summary] Summary: Properties of Base $a$ Logarithms
> For any positive numbers $x > 0$ and $y > 0$, any base $a > 0$ ($a \neq 1$), and any real number $r$:
> 1. **Product Rule:**
>    $$
>    \log_a(xy) = \log_a x + \log_a y
>    $$
> 2. **Quotient Rule:**
>    $$
>    \log_a\left( \frac{x}{y} \right) = \log_a x - \log_a y
>    $$
> 3. **Reciprocal Rule:**
>    $$
>    \log_a\left( \frac{1}{y} \right) = -\log_a y
>    $$
> 4. **Power Rule:**
>    $$
>    \log_a(x^r) = r \log_a x
>    $$

**Proof:**
Using the Change of Base formula $\log_a u = \frac{\ln u}{\ln a}$, each rule follows immediately from the corresponding property of $\ln$:
1. **Product Rule:**
   $$
   \log_a(xy) = \frac{\ln(xy)}{\ln a} = \frac{\ln x + \ln y}{\ln a} = \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} + \frac{\ln y}{\ln a} = \log_a x + \log_a y
   $$
2. **Quotient Rule:**
   $$
   \log_a\left( \frac{x}{y} \right) = \frac{\ln(x/y)}{\ln a} = \frac{\ln x - \ln y}{\ln a} = \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} - \frac{\ln y}{\ln a} = \log_a x - \log_a y
   $$
3. **Reciprocal Rule:**
   $$
   \log_a\left( \frac{1}{y} \right) = \frac{\ln(1/y)}{\ln a} = \frac{-\ln y}{\ln a} = -\frac{\ln y}{\ln a} = -\log_a y
   $$
4. **Power Rule:**
   $$
   \log_a(x^r) = \frac{\ln(x^r)}{\ln a} = \frac{r \ln x}{\ln a} = r \cdot \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} = r \log_a x
   $$
This establishes all four properties. $\blacksquare$

## Calculus of Base $a$ Logarithms

### Differentiation of $\log_a u$

To differentiate a base-$a$ logarithm $\log_a u$, we do not need to memorize a brand-new technique: we simply rewrite $\log_a u = \frac{1}{\ln a} \ln u$ and pull out the constant factor $\frac{1}{\ln a}$:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}[\log_a u] = \frac{d}{dx}\left( \frac{1}{\ln a} \ln u \right) = \frac{1}{\ln a} \frac{d}{dx}(\ln u) = \frac{1}{\ln a} \cdot \frac{1}{u} \frac{du}{dx}
$$

> [!theorem] Derivative of $\log_a u$
> If $u$ is a positive differentiable function of $x$, and $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$, then:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \log_a u = \frac{1}{\ln a} \cdot \frac{1}{u} \frac{du}{dx}
> $$
> For the elementary case $u = x$:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \log_a x = \frac{1}{x \ln a}
> $$
> In differential form:
> $$
> d(\log_a u) = \frac{1}{\ln a} \frac{du}{u}
> $$

Once again, notice how choosing $a = e$ makes the factor $\frac{1}{\ln a}$ equal to $1$, recovering $\frac{d}{dx} \ln u = \frac{1}{u} \frac{du}{dx}$.

> [!warning] Notational Caution: Base Specification
> In advanced mathematics, theoretical analysis, and many computer algebra systems, an unadorned $\log x$ without an explicit base usually means the natural logarithm $\ln x$. In engineering, chemistry, and introductory sciences, however, $\log x$ frequently denotes the base-$10$ (common) logarithm $\log_{10} x$, while in computer science it often denotes the base-$2$ (binary) logarithm $\log_2 x$. To prevent any ambiguity in calculus, we always write $\ln x$ for base $e$ and explicitly write the subscript in $\log_{10} x$ or $\log_a x$ for other bases.

### Integration Involving $\log_a x$

Whenever an integral contains a base-$a$ logarithm, the cleanest strategy is to convert $\log_a x$ to the natural logarithm first using the Change of Base formula:
$$
\log_a x = \frac{\ln x}{\ln a}
$$
For example, to find the general antiderivative $\int \log_a x \, dx$, we factor out $\frac{1}{\ln a}$ and use the standard antiderivative $\int \ln x \, dx = x \ln x - x + C$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int \log_a x \, dx &= \int \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} \, dx \\
&= \frac{1}{\ln a} \int \ln x \, dx \\
&= \frac{1}{\ln a} (x \ln x - x) + C \\
&= x \frac{\ln x}{\ln a} - \frac{x}{\ln a} + C \\
&= x \log_a x - \frac{x}{\ln a} + C
\end{aligned}
$$

> [!theorem] Antiderivative of $\log_a x$
> For any base $a > 0$ with $a \neq 1$:
> $$
> \int \log_a x \, dx = x \log_a x - \frac{x}{\ln a} + C
> $$

## Base 10 (Common) Logarithms in Scientific Measurement

Base-$10$ logarithms, traditionally called **common logarithms**, play a central role in science and engineering because our everyday decimal number system is built on powers of $10$. In nature, physical quantities—such as the ground motion of earthquakes, the concentration of ions in chemical solutions, or the acoustic power of sound waves—often span an enormous range covering ten or more orders of magnitude. Common logarithms compress these vast multiplicative scales into compact, additive scales where each unit step represents a tenfold change.

### Earthquake Magnitude: The Richter Scale

The Richter scale quantifies earthquake magnitude by comparing the recorded amplitude of seismic ground motion to an empirical baseline.

> [!abstract] Definition: Richter Magnitude Scale
> The magnitude $R$ of an earthquake on the Richter scale is defined by:
> $$
> R = \log_{10}\left( \frac{a}{T} \right) + B
> $$
> where:
> - $a$ is the amplitude of the vertical ground motion in microns ($1 \text{ micron} = 10^{-6} \text{ m}$) recorded by a receiving seismograph,
> - $T$ is the period of the seismic wave in seconds,
> - $B$ is an empirical distance-correction factor that accounts for the weakening of seismic waves as the distance from the earthquake's epicenter increases.

How does a change in Richter magnitude translate into actual ground motion? Suppose two earthquakes at the same location have waves of the same period $T$ (and thus the same correction factor $B$), with amplitudes $a_1$ and $a_2$. Subtracting their Richter magnitudes cancels $T$ and $B$:
$$
\Delta R = R_2 - R_1 = \log_{10}\left( \frac{a_2}{T} \right) - \log_{10}\left( \frac{a_1}{T} \right) = \log_{10}\left( \frac{a_2}{a_1} \right)
$$
Exponentiating both sides with base $10$ gives the amplitude ratio:
$$
\frac{a_2}{a_1} = 10^{\Delta R}
$$
This reveals the dramatic scaling behind earthquake numbers: every **1-unit increase** in Richter magnitude ($\Delta R = 1$) corresponds to a **tenfold ($10\times$) increase** in ground motion amplitude, while a **2-unit increase** ($\Delta R = 2$, say from magnitude $5$ to magnitude $7$) represents a **hundredfold ($100\times$) increase** in vertical ground motion!

### Acidity and the pH Scale

In chemistry, the acidity of an aqueous solution is determined by its concentration of hydronium ions, denoted $[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]$ and measured in moles per liter ($\text{mol/L}$). Because $[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]$ varies from more than $1 \text{ mol/L}$ in concentrated strong acids down to less than $10^{-14} \text{ mol/L}$ in concentrated strong bases, chemists index acidity logarithmically on the $\text{pH}$ scale.

> [!abstract] Definition: The pH Scale
> The $\text{pH}$ (potential of hydrogen) of a solution is defined as the common logarithm of the reciprocal of its hydronium ion concentration:
> $$
> \text{pH} = \log_{10}\left( \frac{1}{[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]} \right) = -\log_{10}[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]
> $$

Inverting this definition by exponentiating with base $10$ expresses the ion concentration directly in terms of $\text{pH}$:
$$
[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+] = 10^{-\text{pH}}
$$
In pure water at $25^\circ\text{C}$, the hydronium ion concentration is $10^{-7} \text{ mol/L}$, which sets the neutral benchmark at $\text{pH} = -\log_{10}(10^{-7}) = 7$:
- Solutions with $\text{pH} < 7$ are **acidic** ($[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+] > 10^{-7} \text{ mol/L}$).
- Solutions with $\text{pH} = 7$ are **neutral** ($[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+] = 10^{-7} \text{ mol/L}$).
- Solutions with $\text{pH} > 7$ are **basic** or **alkaline** ($[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+] < 10^{-7} \text{ mol/L}$).

Because of the negative sign in the definition, lower $\text{pH}$ values correspond to higher acidity:
$$
\text{pH}_1 - \text{pH}_2 = \log_{10}\left( \frac{[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]_2}{[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]_1} \right) \implies \frac{[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]_2}{[\text{H}_3\text{O}^+]_1} = 10^{\text{pH}_1 - \text{pH}_2}
$$
Thus, a **decrease of $1$ unit** in $\text{pH}$ corresponds to a **tenfold ($10\times$) increase** in hydronium ion concentration.

### Acoustic Power: The Decibel (dB) Scale

Our ears respond to sound intensity logarithmically rather than linearly: a sound must be multiplied by roughly a factor of $10$ in physical acoustic power before we perceive it as twice as loud. Because of this, sound levels are measured on the logarithmic **decibel ($\text{dB}$)** scale, named in honor of Alexander Graham Bell ($1 \text{ decibel} = \frac{1}{10} \text{ bel}$).

> [!abstract] Definition: Decibel Sound Level Scale
> If $I$ is the acoustic intensity (power per unit area) of a sound in watts per square meter ($\text{W/m}^2$), the sound level $\beta$ in decibels ($\text{dB}$) is defined by:
> $$
> \text{Sound level} = 10 \log_{10}\left( I \times 10^{12} \right) = 10 \log_{10}\left( \frac{I}{I_0} \right) \text{ dB}
> $$
> where $I_0 = 10^{-12} \text{ W/m}^2$ is the standard reference threshold of human hearing at $1000 \text{ Hz}$ (corresponding to $0 \text{ dB}$).

What happens to the decibel reading when we double the physical intensity of a sound source from $I$ to $2I$? Using the product rule for $\log_{10}$, we can calculate the new sound level directly:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\text{Sound level}(2I) &= 10 \log_{10}\left( 2I \times 10^{12} \right) \\
&= 10 \log_{10}\left( 2 \cdot (I \times 10^{12}) \right) \\
&= 10 \left[ \log_{10} 2 + \log_{10}\left( I \times 10^{12} \right) \right] \\
&= 10 \log_{10}\left( I \times 10^{12} \right) + 10 \log_{10} 2 \\
&= \text{Original Sound level} + 10 \log_{10} 2
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $\log_{10} 2 \approx 0.30103$, the additive increase in decibels is:
$$
10 \log_{10} 2 \approx 10(0.30103) \approx 3.01 \approx 3 \text{ dB}
$$

> [!theorem] Acoustic Doubling Invariance
> Doubling the physical intensity of any sound source increases the sound level by approximately $3 \text{ dB}$, regardless of the initial sound intensity:
> $$
> \Delta \beta = 10 \log_{10} 2 \approx 3 \text{ dB}
> $$

## Another Path: Irrational Exponents via Limits

At the beginning of this section, we defined $a^x = e^{x \ln a}$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. As we noted there, this equation is a provable theorem when $x$ is rational, and we extended it to all real $x$ to define $a^x$ when $x$ is irrational. While that definition is completely rigorous and immediately yields all the algebraic and calculus properties we expect, it can initially feel like a sleight of hand.

There is another, highly intuitive way to define $x^n$ (or $a^x$) when the exponent is irrational: **approximate the irrational exponent $n$ by a sequence of rational numbers $r_k \to n$**, and define $x^n$ as the limit of the rational powers $x^{r_k}$! For instance, to compute $2^\pi$, we can evaluate the rational powers $2^3, 2^{3.1}, 2^{3.14}, 2^{3.141}, 2^{3.1415}, \dots$ and take their limit. Let us make this sequential construction precise and prove that it leads to the exact same function.

> [!abstract] Definition: $x^n$ for Arbitrary Real $n$ via Rational Sequences
> Let $x > 0$. For rational $n$, $x^n$ has its usual elementary algebraic meaning. For irrational $n$, choose any sequence of rational numbers $r_k \to n$ (which is always possible because $\mathbb{Q}$ is dense in $\mathbb{R}$) and define:
> $$
> x^n := \lim_{k \to \infty} x^{r_k}
> $$
> (Notice that for rational $n$, this agrees with the usual meaning by taking the constant sequence $r_k = n$.)
> 
> For this definition to be mathematically legitimate (well-defined), two conditions must hold:
> 1. **Existence:** The limit $\lim_{k \to \infty} x^{r_k}$ must exist for every sequence of rationals $r_k \to n$.
> 2. **Independence:** The limit must produce the exact same value for every rational sequence $r_k \to n$, so that $x^n$ names a single, unambiguous real number.

> [!theorem] Well-Definedness of $x^n$ and the Power Rule for Logarithms
> For $x > 0$ and any real number $n$, the limit defining $x^n$ exists and does not depend on the choice of the rational sequence $r_k \to n$. Moreover:
> $$
> \ln(x^n) = n \ln x \quad \text{for all real } n
> $$

**Proof:**
1. **Rational exponents in terms of $\ln^{-1}$:** For any rational number $r$, the rational power rule for the natural logarithm (proved in Section 6.2) gives $\ln(x^r) = r \ln x$. Because $\ln$ is one-to-one, we can apply its inverse function $\ln^{-1}$ (which is $e^u$) to both sides to write:
   $$
   x^r = \ln^{-1}(r \ln x) \quad \text{for rational } r
   $$
2. **Continuity of $\ln^{-1}$:** The natural logarithm $\ln$ is continuous and strictly increasing on $(0, \infty)$ with range $(-\infty, \infty)$. By Theorem 1 of Section 6.1 (the continuity of inverse functions), its inverse $\ln^{-1}$ exists on all of $(-\infty, \infty)$ and is continuous everywhere.
3. **Passing to the limit:** Let $r_k \to n$ be any sequence of rational numbers converging to $n$. Since $\ln x$ is a fixed real constant, the sequence of products $r_k \ln x$ converges to $n \ln x$ as $k \to \infty$. By the continuity of $\ln^{-1}$, we can pass the limit inside $\ln^{-1}$:
   $$
   x^{r_k} = \ln^{-1}(r_k \ln x) \longrightarrow \ln^{-1}(n \ln x) \quad \text{as } k \to \infty
   $$
4. **Conclusion:** The limit $\lim_{k \to \infty} x^{r_k}$ exists, and its value $\ln^{-1}(n \ln x)$ depends only on $x$ and $n$, not on the particular sequence of rationals $r_k$ chosen to approximate $n$. This proves both existence and independence, establishing that $x^n$ is well-defined and satisfies:
   $$
   x^n = \ln^{-1}(n \ln x) = e^{n \ln x}
   $$
   Finally, applying $\ln$ to both sides immediately gives $\ln(x^n) = n \ln x$. $\blacksquare$

---

# 6.6 L'Hôpital's Rule

## Historical Background and Origins

In the late seventeenth century, Johann (John) Bernoulli (1667–1748) discovered a revolutionary method for calculating the limits of fractions whose numerators and denominators simultaneously approach zero. At the time, such limits posed severe analytical hurdles because direct substitution led to the meaningless expression $\frac{0}{0}$.

Why, then, do we call this result **l'Hôpital's Rule** rather than Bernoulli's Rule? The rule bears the name of Guillaume François Antoine de l'Hôpital (1661–1704), Marquis de Sainte-Mesme, a wealthy French nobleman and accomplished mathematician. In 1694, l'Hôpital entered into a remarkable financial arrangement with Bernoulli, offering him an annual retainer of 300 pounds to solve mathematical problems for him, keep him apprised of the latest developments in the newly invented calculus, and grant l'Hôpital exclusive permission to use Bernoulli's discoveries.

Among the problems Bernoulli solved under this contract was the evaluation of $\frac{0}{0}$ indeterminate forms. In 1696, l'Hôpital published *Analyse des Infiniment Petits pour l'Intelligence des Lignes Courbes* ("Analysis of the Infinitely Small for the Understanding of Curved Lines"), which stands as the very first textbook on differential calculus ever printed. L'Hôpital published the work anonymously, acknowledging in the preface his general debt to both Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz and Johann Bernoulli without specifying individual theorems.

Following l'Hôpital's death in 1704, the publisher reissued the book explicitly under l'Hôpital's name. In 1721, Bernoulli publicly claimed full authorship of the $\frac{0}{0}$ theorem and accused l'Hôpital of plagiarism. Although historical scholarship and surviving correspondence later verified Bernoulli's discovery beyond doubt, mathematical tradition permanently affixed the name **l'Hôpital's Rule** to this fundamental tool of analysis.

## Indeterminate Quotients

### The Nature of Indeterminate Forms

Suppose we want to investigate the limit of a quotient of two functions as $x$ approaches $a$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}
$$
Normally, our first instinct is to reach for the Limit Quotient Rule, which tells us that if $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = L$ and $\lim_{x \to a} g(x) = M \neq 0$, we can simply divide the individual limits:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{L}{M}
$$
But what happens if both the numerator and the denominator vanish simultaneously as $x \to a$, so that
$$
\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} g(x) = 0
$$
In this situation, the Limit Quotient Rule breaks down completely because direct substitution yields the undefined expression $\frac{0}{0}$.

> [!abstract] Definition: Indeterminate Form of Type $\frac{0}{0}$
> An expression $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ is said to be an **indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$** at $x = a$ if:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} g(x) = 0
> $$

Why do we call this form *indeterminate*? Because knowing that the numerator and denominator both head toward zero tells us nothing about what happens to their ratio. Think of it as a tug-of-war: a shrinking numerator tries to pull the fraction toward $0$, while a shrinking denominator tries to blow the fraction up toward $\pm\infty$. Depending on the relative speeds at which $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ approach zero, the quotient might converge to $0$, diverge to $\pm\infty$, oscillate without a limit, or settle on any finite real number in between. The symbol $\frac{0}{0}$ is never a number or a valid arithmetic operation—it is merely a shorthand label for this tug-of-war between two vanishing functions.

### The Foundation of Differential Calculus as an Indeterminate Form

It is tempting to think of indeterminate forms as rare, pathological edge cases, but they are actually the bedrock upon which all of differential calculus is built! Consider the limit definition of the derivative at $x = a$:
$$
f'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}
$$
Notice that for any continuous function $f$, as $x \to a$, the numerator $f(x) - f(a)$ approaches $0$ and the denominator $x - a$ also approaches $0$. Every single derivative we ever compute is universally an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$! The entire purpose of differential calculus is to extract well-defined finite rates of change from this indeterminate ratio of vanishing increments.

### Geometric and Linearization Interpretation

How can derivatives help us resolve a general $\frac{0}{0}$ limit $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$? We can build a clear geometric intuition by zooming in close to $x = a$ and using local linear approximations. Suppose $f$ and $g$ are both differentiable at $x = a$ and satisfy $f(a) = g(a) = 0$. Near $x = a$, each function behaves almost exactly like its tangent line:
$$
f(x) \approx f(a) + f'(a)(x - a) = f'(a)(x - a)
$$
$$
g(x) \approx g(a) + g'(a)(x - a) = g'(a)(x - a)
$$
If we form the quotient of these two functions for $x \neq a$, the common horizontal displacement $(x - a)$ cancels out cleanly:
$$
\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \approx \frac{f'(a)(x - a)}{g'(a)(x - a)} = \frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}
$$
As $x$ gets closer and closer to $a$, the error terms in our tangent line approximations vanish much faster than $(x - a)$ itself. This tells us something remarkable: near a common root $x = a$, the ratio of two functions is governed entirely by the ratio of their slopes, $\frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}$.

## L'Hôpital's Rule (First Form)

### Pointwise Derivative Formulation

We can turn our linearization intuition into a quick, rigorous theorem whenever the derivatives $f'(a)$ and $g'(a)$ exist right at the point $x = a$ and the denominator's derivative is nonzero.

> [!theorem] Theorem 2: L'Hôpital's Rule (First Form)
> Suppose that $f(a) = g(a) = 0$, that $f'(a)$ and $g'(a)$ exist, and that $g'(a) \neq 0$. Then:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}
> $$

**Proof:** By hypothesis, $f(a) = 0$ and $g(a) = 0$, and the derivatives $f'(a)$ and $g'(a)$ exist as two-sided limits:
$$
f'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a} \quad \text{and} \quad g'(a) = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{g(x) - g(a)}{x - a}
$$
Working backward from $\frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}$, since $g'(a) \neq 0$, the Limit Quotient Rule allows us to write the ratio of the two limits as the limit of their ratio:
$$
\frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)} = \frac{\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}}{\lim_{x \to a} \frac{g(x) - g(a)}{x - a}} = \lim_{x \to a} \left( \frac{\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}}{\frac{g(x) - g(a)}{x - a}} \right)
$$
For any $x \neq a$, we can cancel the common factor $(x - a)$ in the numerator and denominator:
$$
\frac{\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{x - a}}{\frac{g(x) - g(a)}{x - a}} = \frac{f(x) - f(a)}{g(x) - g(a)}
$$
Now, substituting our initial conditions $f(a) = 0$ and $g(a) = 0$ simplifies the fraction directly to the original quotient:
$$
\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{g(x) - g(a)} = \frac{f(x) - 0}{g(x) - 0} = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}
$$
Taking the limit as $x \to a$ yields:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}
$$
which establishes the theorem. $\blacksquare$

> [!warning] Notational Caution: Quotient of Derivatives vs. Derivative of a Quotient
> When applying l'Hôpital's Rule to evaluate $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$, you must differentiate the numerator and denominator **separately** and divide the resulting derivatives:
> $$
> \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
> $$
> Never take the derivative of the entire fraction using the Quotient Rule:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx} \left[ \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \right] = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2} \neq \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
> $$
> Always remember: l'Hôpital's Rule uses $\frac{f'}{g'}$, never $\left(\frac{f}{g}\right)'$.

### Theoretical Limitations of the First Form

Although Theorem 2 is wonderfully simple to prove, it is too restrictive for many of the limits we encounter in practice:
1. **Pointwise differentiability at $x = a$:** It requires $f'(a)$ and $g'(a)$ to exist at the exact point $x = a$, which fails whenever $f$ or $g$ are differentiable on a punctured neighborhood around $a$ (such as expressions involving $\sqrt{x}$, $\ln x$, or $\frac{1}{x}$) without being differentiable at $a$ itself.
2. **No mechanism for iteration:** It requires $g'(a) \neq 0$. If both $f'(a) = 0$ and $g'(a) = 0$, Theorem 2 merely hands us another $\frac{0}{0}$ expression and gives us no way to apply the rule a second time.
3. **Finite points only:** Because it relies on evaluating derivatives at a specific real number $a$, it cannot be applied directly to limits at infinity ($x \to \pm\infty$).

To overcome these three hurdles, we need a stronger version of l'Hôpital's Rule that replaces pointwise values $\frac{f'(a)}{g'(a)}$ with the limit of the derivative ratio $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}$.

## Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem

How can we link the ratio $\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{g(x) - g(a)}$ to the ratio of derivatives $\frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}$ without dividing two separate limits at $a$? You might be tempted to apply the ordinary Mean Value Theorem to $f$ and $g$ separately on $[a, x]$, writing $f(x) - f(a) = f'(c_1)(x - a)$ and $g(x) - g(a) = g'(c_2)(x - a)$. The catch is that there is no guarantee that the two intermediate points $c_1$ and $c_2$ are the same!

To guarantee that both derivatives are evaluated at the **exact same intermediate point** $c$, we need a generalization of the classical Mean Value Theorem formulated by Augustin-Louis Cauchy (1789–1857).

> [!theorem] Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem
> Suppose functions $f$ and $g$ are continuous on a closed interval $[a, b]$ and differentiable on the open interval $(a, b)$. Then there exists at least one point $c \in (a, b)$ such that:
> $$
> [f(b) - f(a)] g'(c) = [g(b) - g(a)] f'(c)
> $$
> If, in addition, $g'(x) \neq 0$ for all $x \in (a, b)$, then $g(b) \neq g(a)$, and the equation can be written in ratio form as:
> $$
> \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{g(b) - g(a)} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}
> $$

**Proof:** Just as we proved the ordinary Mean Value Theorem by tilting the function to satisfy Rolle's Theorem, we construct an auxiliary linear combination $h(x)$ on $[a, b]$ designed so that its endpoint values match:
$$
h(x) = [f(b) - f(a)] g(x) - [g(b) - g(a)] f(x)
$$
Let's verify the three hypotheses of Rolle's Theorem for $h(x)$:
1. **Continuity on $[a, b]$:** Because $f$ and $g$ are continuous on $[a, b]$, their linear combination $h(x)$ is continuous on $[a, b]$.
2. **Differentiability on $(a, b)$:** Because $f$ and $g$ are differentiable on $(a, b)$, $h(x)$ is differentiable on $(a, b)$, with derivative:
   $$
   h'(x) = [f(b) - f(a)] g'(x) - [g(b) - g(a)] f'(x)
   $$
3. **Equal endpoint values:** Evaluating $h(x)$ at $x = a$ and $x = b$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   h(a) &= [f(b) - f(a)] g(a) - [g(b) - g(a)] f(a) \\
   &= f(b)g(a) - f(a)g(a) - g(b)f(a) + g(a)f(a) \\
   &= f(b)g(a) - g(b)f(a)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   and
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   h(b) &= [f(b) - f(a)] g(b) - [g(b) - g(a)] f(b) \\
   &= f(b)g(b) - f(a)g(b) - g(b)f(b) + g(a)f(b) \\
   &= g(a)f(b) - f(a)g(b)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Comparing the two simplified expressions, we see that $h(a) = h(b)$.

By Rolle's Theorem, there exists at least one number $c \in (a, b)$ such that $h'(c) = 0$. Setting $h'(c) = 0$ immediately gives the first conclusion:
$$
[f(b) - f(a)] g'(c) - [g(b) - g(a)] f'(c) = 0 \implies [f(b) - f(a)] g'(c) = [g(b) - g(a)] f'(c)
$$
Furthermore, suppose $g'(x) \neq 0$ for all $x \in (a, b)$. Why does this guarantee that we aren't dividing by zero when we form $\frac{f(b) - f(a)}{g(b) - g(a)}$? If $g(b)$ were equal to $g(a)$, then Rolle's Theorem applied to $g$ alone would guarantee a point in $(a, b)$ where $g'$ vanishes, contradicting our assumption that $g'(x) \neq 0$ on $(a, b)$. Thus $g(b) \neq g(a)$ and $g'(c) \neq 0$, so dividing both sides by $[g(b) - g(a)] g'(c)$ yields:
$$
\frac{f(b) - f(a)}{g(b) - g(a)} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}
$$
This establishes the theorem. $\blacksquare$

> [!note] Geometric Interpretation of Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem
> We can visualize Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem by treating $x = g(t)$ and $y = f(t)$ for $t \in [a, b]$ as a parametric curve in the $xy$-plane. The secant chord connecting the endpoints $(g(a), f(a))$ and $(g(b), f(b))$ has slope:
> $$
> m_{\text{chord}} = \frac{f(b) - f(a)}{g(b) - g(a)}
> $$
> Meanwhile, the instantaneous slope of the tangent line to the parametric curve at parameter value $t = c$ is:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}
> $$
> Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem states geometrically that there is at least one interior moment $t = c$ where the tangent line to the parametric trajectory is parallel to the chord joining its endpoints. We will explore the rigorous foundations of this parametric geometry at the end of this section.

## L'Hôpital's Rule (Stronger Form)

### Formulation for the Indeterminate Form $\frac{0}{0}$

Equipped with Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem, we can now state and prove the full, general version of l'Hôpital's Rule for $\frac{0}{0}$ forms. Notice how this stronger version only requires differentiability on a punctured neighborhood of $a$.

> [!theorem] Theorem 3: L'Hôpital's Rule (Stronger Form)
> Let $I$ be an open interval containing $a$. Suppose that:
> 1. $f$ and $g$ are **continuous on $I$**, with $f(a) = g(a) = 0$,
> 2. $f$ and $g$ are **differentiable on $I \setminus \{a\}$**, and
> 3. $g'(x) \neq 0$ for all $x \in I \setminus \{a\}$.
> 
> Then:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
> $$
> provided the limit on the right-hand side exists as a finite real number $L$, or equals $\infty$ or $-\infty$.

> [!note] Theoretical Remark: Continuity at $x = a$ and Continuous Extensions
> Why must continuity on all of $I$ (including at $x = a$) be explicitly stated when we relax differentiability at $a$?
> 
> 1. **Cauchy's MVT Requirement:** In our proof below, applying Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem on the closed interval $[a, x]$ strictly requires the functions to be **continuous on $[a, x]$** and **differentiable on $(a, x)$**. While differentiability on $I \setminus \{a\}$ automatically gives us continuity on $(a, x]$, it does not guarantee continuity at the boundary point $a$. If a function had $f(a) = 0$ but $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) \neq 0$ (a removable discontinuity), Cauchy's MVT would fail on $[a, x]$.
> 2. **Textbook Simplification:** Introductory textbooks (such as Thomas & Finney) often state the hypothesis more simply as *"differentiable on an open interval $I$ containing $a$"*. By assuming differentiability everywhere on $I$ (including at $a$), continuity on $I$ comes along for free because differentiability implies continuity.
> 3. **The Punctured / Limit Perspective:** In practice, if $f$ and $g$ are initially defined only on the punctured interval $I \setminus \{a\}$ with $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0$ and $\lim_{x \to a} g(x) = 0$, we can simply define (or redefine) their values at $a$ by setting $f(a) = 0$ and $g(a) = 0$. This continuous extension makes $f$ and $g$ continuous on all of $I$ without altering their limits or their derivatives for $x \neq a$, thereby satisfying the exact hypotheses of Theorem 3.

**Proof:** We establish the two-sided limit by showing that both the right-hand limit ($x \to a^+$) and the left-hand limit ($x \to a^-$) equal the limit of the derivative ratio.

**Case 1: Right-hand limit ($x \to a^+$)**
Choose any $x \in I$ with $x > a$, so that the closed interval $[a, x]$ lies inside $I$. By hypothesis:
- $f$ and $g$ are continuous on $I$, and therefore continuous on the closed subinterval $[a, x]$.
- $f$ and $g$ are differentiable on $I \setminus \{a\}$, and therefore differentiable on the open subinterval $(a, x)$.
- $g'(t) \neq 0$ for all $t \in (a, x)$.

Applying Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem to $f$ and $g$ on $[a, x]$, we know there exists an intermediate point $c_x$ strictly between $a$ and $x$ ($a < c_x < x$) such that:
$$
\frac{f(x) - f(a)}{g(x) - g(a)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)}
$$
Because $f(a) = 0$ and $g(a) = 0$, the left-hand side simplifies directly to $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$:
$$
\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)}
$$
Now let $x \to a^+$. Since $c_x$ is trapped between $a$ and $x$ ($a < c_x < x$), the Squeeze Theorem forces $c_x \to a^+$ as $x \to a^+$.

Let's make this completely rigorous using an $\epsilon$-$\delta$ argument. Suppose first that $\lim_{t \to a^+} \frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} = L \in \mathbb{R}$. By the formal definition of a right-hand limit, for every $\epsilon > 0$, there exists a $\delta > 0$ such that for all $t \in I$:
$$
0 < t - a < \delta \implies \left| \frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} - L \right| < \epsilon
$$
Whenever we choose $x$ satisfying $0 < x - a < \delta$, the corresponding Cauchy point $c_x \in (a, x)$ automatically satisfies $0 < c_x - a < x - a < \delta$. Therefore, $t = c_x$ falls inside the $\delta$-neighborhood, which implies:
$$
\left| \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)} - L \right| < \epsilon \implies \left| \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} - L \right| < \epsilon
$$
This proves that:
$$
\lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = L
$$
What if $\lim_{t \to a^+} \frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} = \infty$? Then for every $M > 0$, there exists $\delta > 0$ such that for all $t$ with $0 < t - a < \delta$, we have $\frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} > M$. For any $x$ satisfying $0 < x - a < \delta$, the intermediate point $c_x$ also satisfies $0 < c_x - a < \delta$, so:
$$
\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)} > M \implies \lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \infty
$$
An identical argument holds if the right-hand limit is $-\infty$.

**Case 2: Left-hand limit ($x \to a^-$)**
For any $x \in I$ with $x < a$, we apply Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem on the closed interval $[x, a]$ to obtain an intermediate point $c_x \in (x, a)$ such that:
$$
\frac{f(a) - f(x)}{g(a) - g(x)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)} \implies \frac{-f(x)}{-g(x)} = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)}
$$
As $x \to a^-$, the Squeeze Theorem forces $c_x \to a^-$, and the exact same $\epsilon$-$\delta$ argument yields:
$$
\lim_{x \to a^-} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{c_x \to a^-} \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)} = L
$$

**Conclusion:**
Because both one-sided limits exist and equal $L$, the two-sided limit exists and equals $L$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = L = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
$$
This completes the proof. $\blacksquare$

### Repeated Application (Iteration) of the Rule

One of the greatest practical benefits of Theorem 3 over Theorem 2 is that we can apply it **repeatedly**. Suppose that after differentiating the numerator and denominator once, the new limit $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}$ is still an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} f'(x) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} g'(x) = 0
$$
Provided $f'$ and $g'$ satisfy the same differentiability hypotheses on $I \setminus \{a\}$ (with $g''(x) \neq 0$), we can simply apply Theorem 3 a second time to the ratio $\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f''(x)}{g''(x)}
$$
We can continue differentiating step by step until the ratio of higher derivatives finally reaches a determinate form—whether a finite real number, a nonzero constant divided by zero (indicating $\pm\infty$), or a limit that can be evaluated by other means.

## The Indeterminate Form $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$

### Theoretical Extension to Infinite Quotients

What if, instead of both functions shrinking to zero, both the numerator and the denominator blow up without bound as $x \to a$? Just like $\frac{0}{0}$, a ratio of two exploding functions is a tug-of-war: a growing numerator pulls the fraction toward $\pm\infty$, while a growing denominator pulls it toward $0$. Remarkably, l'Hôpital's Rule works in the exact same way for these infinite quotients!

> [!abstract] Definition: Indeterminate Form of Type $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$
> An expression $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ is said to be an **indeterminate form of type $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$** if:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} |f(x)| = \infty \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} |g(x)| = \infty
> $$
> where $x \to a$ may represent a two-sided limit, a one-sided limit ($x \to a^+$ or $x \to a^-$), or a limit at infinity ($x \to \infty$ or $x \to -\infty$).

> [!theorem] L'Hôpital's Rule for $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$
> Suppose that $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = \pm\infty$ and $\lim_{x \to a} g(x) = \pm\infty$, and that $f$ and $g$ are differentiable on an open interval $I$ containing $a$ (except possibly at $a$) with $g'(x) \neq 0$ for $x \neq a$. Then:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
> $$
> provided the limit on the right exists (as a finite real number $L$, or as $\pm\infty$).

Why is the proof of the $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ case much subtler than the $\frac{0}{0}$ case? In the $\frac{0}{0}$ proof, we applied Cauchy's MVT directly on the interval $[a, x]$ using the endpoint values $f(a) = g(a) = 0$. Here, $f$ and $g$ blow up at $x = a$, so they are not even defined (let alone continuous!) at the endpoint $a$. Instead, we have to anchor our interval at a small fixed point $x_0$ near $a$, apply Cauchy's MVT on $[x, x_0]$, and then let $x \to a^+$ so that the exploding values $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ overwhelm the fixed anchor values $f(x_0)$ and $g(x_0)$.

**Proof:** We prove the finite right-hand limit case where $\lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)} = L \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\lim_{x \to a^+} g(x) = \infty$.

Let $\epsilon > 0$ be given. Our strategy is to split our allowable error tolerance $\epsilon$ into two halves: we use $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ to trap the derivative ratio $\frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)}$ near $L$, and we use the remaining $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ to absorb the leftover terms involving the fixed anchor point $x_0$ as $g(x) \to \infty$.

**Step 1: Establishing the $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ derivative bound**
Because $\lim_{t \to a^+} \frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} = L$, there exists a point $x_0 > a$ in $I$ sufficiently close to $a$ such that for all $t$:
$$
a < t \le x_0 \implies L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} < \frac{f'(t)}{g'(t)} < L + \frac{\epsilon}{2}
$$
Once we choose this point $x_0$, we hold $x_0$ **strictly fixed** for the rest of the proof.

**Step 2: Applying Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem on $[x, x_0]$**
Now choose any $x$ strictly between $a$ and $x_0$ ($a < x < x_0$). Since $f$ and $g$ are differentiable (and hence continuous) on $[x, x_0] \subset I \setminus \{a\}$, Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem guarantees an intermediate point $c \in (x, x_0)$ such that:
$$
\frac{f(x) - f(x_0)}{g(x) - g(x_0)} = \frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}
$$
Because $a < x < c < x_0$, the point $c$ lies in $(a, x_0]$, which means $\frac{f'(c)}{g'(c)}$ is trapped inside our $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$-band:
$$
L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} < \frac{f(x) - f(x_0)}{g(x) - g(x_0)} < L + \frac{\epsilon}{2}
$$

**Step 3: Isolating $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ algebraically**
How do we extract $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ from the difference quotient $\frac{f(x) - f(x_0)}{g(x) - g(x_0)}$? Because $\lim_{x \to a^+} g(x) = \infty$ while $g(x_0)$ is a fixed constant, we can restrict $x$ close enough to $a$ so that $g(x) > g(x_0)$ and $g(x) > 0$. This ensures that the factor
$$
\frac{g(x) - g(x_0)}{g(x)} = 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)}
$$
is strictly positive. Multiplying our three-part inequality across by $1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} > 0$ preserves the inequality signs:
$$
\left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) < \frac{f(x) - f(x_0)}{g(x)} < \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right)
$$
Splitting the middle fraction as $\frac{f(x) - f(x_0)}{g(x)} = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} - \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)}$ and adding $\frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)}$ to all three parts isolates our target quotient $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$:
$$
\left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} < \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} < \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)}
$$

**Step 4: Absorbing the fixed endpoint terms as $x \to a^+$**
Remember that $x_0$ is fixed, so $f(x_0)$ and $g(x_0)$ are just constants. As we let $x \to a^+$, the denominator $g(x) \to \infty$, which wipes out both fractions containing $x_0$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} = 0
$$
Therefore, as $x \to a^+$, the left-hand and right-hand bounding expressions converge to $L - \frac{\epsilon}{2}$ and $L + \frac{\epsilon}{2}$, respectively:
$$
\lim_{x \to a^+} \left[ \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} \right] = \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right)(1 - 0) + 0 = L - \frac{\epsilon}{2}
$$
$$
\lim_{x \to a^+} \left[ \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} \right] = \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right)(1 - 0) + 0 = L + \frac{\epsilon}{2}
$$
Applying the definition of a limit with tolerance $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ to each of these two bounding expressions:
1. There exists $\delta_1 > 0$ such that for all $x \in (a, a + \delta_1)$, the lower bound stays within $\frac{\epsilon}{2}$ of $L - \frac{\epsilon}{2}$:
   $$
   \left| \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} - \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \right| < \frac{\epsilon}{2}
   $$
   Unpacking the absolute value gives:
   $$
   \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) - \frac{\epsilon}{2} < \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} < \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) + \frac{\epsilon}{2}
   $$
   which simplifies to:
   $$
   L - \epsilon < \left( L - \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} < L
   $$
2. Similarly, there exists $\delta_2 > 0$ such that for all $x \in (a, a + \delta_2)$, the upper bound stays below $\left(L + \frac{\epsilon}{2}\right) + \frac{\epsilon}{2}$:
   $$
   \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x_0)}{g(x)} \right) + \frac{f(x_0)}{g(x)} < \left( L + \frac{\epsilon}{2} \right) + \frac{\epsilon}{2} = L + \epsilon
   $$

**Conclusion:**
Setting $\delta = \min(\delta_1, \delta_2, x_0 - a) > 0$, whenever $0 < x - a < \delta$, both bounds hold simultaneously and trap $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ within $\epsilon$ of $L$:
$$
L - \epsilon < \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} < L + \epsilon \iff \left| \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} - L \right| < \epsilon
$$
Thus:
$$
\lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = L = \lim_{x \to a^+} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
$$
An identical argument establishes the left-hand limit $x \to a^-$ as well as the cases where $L = \pm\infty$. $\blacksquare$

## Limits at Infinity ($x \to \pm\infty$)

Does l'Hôpital's Rule still hold when the independent variable $x$ marches off to $+\infty$ or $-\infty$ instead of approaching a finite number $a$? Yes—and we don't even need a new Cauchy Mean Value Theorem argument to prove it! We can reduce limits as $x \to \infty$ directly to one-sided limits as $t \to 0^+$ via the reciprocal substitution $t = \frac{1}{x}$.

> [!theorem] L'Hôpital's Rule at Infinity
> Let $f$ and $g$ be differentiable on an open interval $(b, \infty)$, with $g'(x) \neq 0$ for all $x > b$. If $\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ produces an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$ or $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$, then:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
> $$
> provided the limit on the right-hand side exists (finite or $\pm\infty$). An identical statement holds for $x \to -\infty$ on $(-\infty, b)$.

**Proof:** Let $t = \frac{1}{x}$, so that as $x \to \infty$, we have $t \to 0^+$. Define two auxiliary functions of $t$ for $t \in \left(0, \frac{1}{b}\right)$:
$$
F(t) = f\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \quad \text{and} \quad G(t) = g\left( \frac{1}{t} \right)
$$
Notice that as $t \to 0^+$, both $F(t) \to 0$ and $G(t) \to 0$ (in the $\frac{0}{0}$ case), or both $|F(t)| \to \infty$ and $|G(t)| \to \infty$ (in the $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ case).

By the Chain Rule, $F(t)$ and $G(t)$ are differentiable with respect to $t$ for $0 < t < \frac{1}{b}$:
$$
F'(t) = f'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \cdot \frac{d}{dt}\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) = f'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \cdot \left( -\frac{1}{t^2} \right)
$$
$$
G'(t) = g'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \cdot \frac{d}{dt}\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) = g'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \cdot \left( -\frac{1}{t^2} \right)
$$
Applying our established l'Hôpital's Rule for the finite one-sided limit as $t \to 0^+$, notice how the Chain Rule factors $\left(-\frac{1}{t^2}\right)$ cancel out cleanly:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} &= \lim_{t \to 0^+} \frac{F(t)}{G(t)} \\
&= \lim_{t \to 0^+} \frac{F'(t)}{G'(t)} \\
&= \lim_{t \to 0^+} \frac{f'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \left( -\frac{1}{t^2} \right)}{g'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right) \left( -\frac{1}{t^2} \right)} \\
&= \lim_{t \to 0^+} \frac{f'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right)}{g'\left( \frac{1}{t} \right)} \\
&= \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
\end{aligned}
$$
Replacing $t = \frac{1}{x}$ with $t \to 0^-$ proves the analogous result for $x \to -\infty$. $\blacksquare$

## Other Indeterminate Forms

Keep in mind a crucial rule of thumb: **l'Hôpital's Rule applies directly only to quotients of the form $\frac{0}{0}$ and $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$**. However, in calculus we frequently encounter five other indeterminate forms:
- **Indeterminate products:** $0 \cdot \infty$
- **Indeterminate differences:** $\infty - \infty$
- **Indeterminate powers:** $1^\infty$, $0^0$, and $\infty^0$

Whenever you meet one of these expressions, do not differentiate its pieces in place! Instead, your goal is to algebraically or logarithmically rewrite the expression until it becomes a $\frac{0}{0}$ or $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ quotient, and *then* apply l'Hôpital's Rule.

### Indeterminate Products ($0 \cdot \infty$)

> [!abstract] Definition: Indeterminate Form of Type $0 \cdot \infty$
> An expression $\lim_{x \to a} [f(x) \cdot g(x)]$ is an **indeterminate form of type $0 \cdot \infty$** if:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} |g(x)| = \infty
> $$

Why is $0 \cdot \infty$ indeterminate? Remember that "$0$" here is not the exact number zero, which annihilates everything; it is a function *approaching* $0$. In the product $f(x) \cdot g(x)$, the factor $f(x)$ tries to drag the product down toward $0$, while $g(x)$ tries to hurl it toward $\pm\infty$. Which factor wins depends on their relative rates of change.

**Converting $0 \cdot \infty$ to a Quotient Form:**
We can turn any product $f(x) \cdot g(x)$ into a quotient by rewriting one of the two factors as the reciprocal of its reciprocal:
1. **Conversion to $\frac{0}{0}$:** Move the infinite factor $g(x)$ into the denominator as $\frac{1}{g(x)}$:
   $$
   f(x) \cdot g(x) = \frac{f(x)}{\frac{1}{g(x)}}
   $$
   Because $\lim_{x \to a} |g(x)| = \infty$, we have $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{1}{g(x)} = 0$, giving a $\frac{0}{0}$ form.
2. **Conversion to $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$:** Move the vanishing factor $f(x)$ into the denominator as $\frac{1}{f(x)}$:
   $$
   f(x) \cdot g(x) = \frac{g(x)}{\frac{1}{f(x)}}
   $$
   Because $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0$, we have $\lim_{x \to a} \left|\frac{1}{f(x)}\right| = \infty$, giving an $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ form.

How do you choose which factor to invert into the denominator? Always ask yourself: *which reciprocal has the cleaner derivative?* For instance, in $\lim_{x \to 0^+} x \ln x$, inverting $x$ gives $\frac{\ln x}{1/x}$, whose derivatives $\frac{1/x}{-1/x^2}$ simplify immediately, whereas inverting $\ln x$ gives $\frac{x}{1/\ln x}$, which becomes progressively messier when differentiated.

### Indeterminate Differences ($\infty - \infty$)

> [!abstract] Definition: Indeterminate Form of Type $\infty - \infty$
> An expression $\lim_{x \to a} [f(x) - g(x)]$ is an **indeterminate form of type $\infty - \infty$** if:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to a} f(x) = \infty \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{x \to a} g(x) = \infty
> $$

In an $\infty - \infty$ limit, two positive quantities both grow without bound, and we are subtracting one from the other. If $f(x)$ grows faster than $g(x)$, the difference goes to $+\infty$; if $g(x)$ grows faster, the difference goes to $-\infty$; and if they grow at nearly the same rate, their infinite parts may cancel out to leave a finite constant.

> [!warning] Distinguishing Indeterminate and Determinate Differences
> While $\infty - \infty$ is a genuine tug-of-war and therefore indeterminate, adding two infinities of the same sign involves no competition at all:
> $$
> \infty + \infty \to \infty \quad \text{and} \quad -\infty - \infty \to -\infty
> $$
> These are **determinate** forms, not indeterminate forms!

**Conversion Techniques for $\infty - \infty$:**
To apply l'Hôpital's Rule to an $\infty - \infty$ expression, we must combine the two terms into a single fraction or product using one of three standard algebraic strategies:
1. **Common Denominators:** When the difference involves fractions—such as $\frac{1}{u(x)} - \frac{1}{v(x)}$ where $u(x) \to 0^+$ and $v(x) \to 0^+$—combine them over a common denominator:
   $$
   \frac{1}{u(x)} - \frac{1}{v(x)} = \frac{v(x) - u(x)}{u(x)v(x)}
   $$
   This immediately converts the $\infty - \infty$ difference into a $\frac{0}{0}$ quotient.
2. **Rationalization (Conjugates):** When square roots or radicals are present, multiply and divide by the algebraic conjugate to eliminate the canceling infinities in the numerator:
   $$
   \sqrt{u(x)} - \sqrt{v(x)} = \frac{u(x) - v(x)}{\sqrt{u(x)} + \sqrt{v(x)}}
   $$
3. **Factoring Out a Dominant Term:** Factoring out one of the exploding functions converts the difference into a product:
   $$
   f(x) - g(x) = f(x) \left( 1 - \frac{g(x)}{f(x)} \right)
   $$
   If $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{g(x)}{f(x)} = 1$, this becomes a $0 \cdot \infty$ product that can be rewritten as a quotient; if $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{g(x)}{f(x)} \neq 1$, the limit is already resolved!

### Indeterminate Powers ($1^\infty$, $0^0$, $\infty^0$)

Indeterminate powers arise when both the base and the exponent vary with $x$:
$$
\lim_{x \to a} [f(x)]^{g(x)}
$$

> [!abstract] Definition: Indeterminate Power Forms
> The limit $\lim_{x \to a} [f(x)]^{g(x)}$ represents an indeterminate form of type:
> 1. **$1^\infty$** if $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 1$ and $\lim_{x \to a} |g(x)| = \infty$.
>    *(Here the base tries to pull the expression toward $1$, while the exponent tries to drive it toward $0$ or $\infty$.)*
> 2. **$0^0$** if $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = 0^+$ and $\lim_{x \to a} g(x) = 0$.
>    *(Here the vanishing positive base pulls the expression toward $0$, while the vanishing exponent pulls any positive number toward $1$.)*
> 3. **$\infty^0$** if $\lim_{x \to a} f(x) = \infty$ and $\lim_{x \to a} g(x) = 0$.
>    *(Here the exploding base pulls the expression toward $\infty$, while the vanishing exponent pulls it toward $1$.)*

> [!note] Determinate Power Forms
> Be careful not to confuse indeterminate powers with **determinate** power forms where the base and exponent work in harmony rather than in opposition:
