3. **Geometric Tracking and Optimization Models:** Inverse trigonometric functions govern physical angles in navigation, robotics, and viewing-angle optimization problems, where planar coordinates or sensor distances determine directional angles.

---

# 6.9 Derivatives of Inverse Trigonometric Functions; Integrals

## Differential Calculus of Inverse Trigonometric Functions

In Section 6.8, we constructed the six inverse trigonometric functions by restricting the domains of the periodic trigonometric functions to canonical intervals on which they are one-to-one and strictly monotonic. A natural question immediately follows: *how do these new transcendental functions behave when we differentiate them?*

Because each restricted trigonometric function is smooth and differentiable, the Inverse Function Derivative Rule (Theorem 1 from Section 6.1) guarantees that each inverse trigonometric function is differentiable at every point in the interior of its domain where the derivative of the original trigonometric function is non-zero. What makes this calculus particularly remarkable is that differentiating these transcendental inverse functions strips away the trigonometry entirely, producing purely algebraic derivatives!

### Master Derivative Table

Before deriving each formula step by step, let us gather all six derivative rules in one place so you can see the structural patterns across the entire family.

> [!summary] Table 6.5: Derivatives of the Inverse Trigonometric Functions
> Let $u = u(x)$ be a differentiable function of $x$. On the open domains specified below, the derivatives of the six inverse trigonometric functions are:
> 
> 1. **Arcsine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\sin^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - u^2}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| < 1
>    $$
> 2. **Arccosine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\cos^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - u^2}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| < 1
>    $$
> 3. **Arctangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{1 + u^2} \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 4. **Arccotangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\cot^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{1 + u^2} \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 5. **Arcsecant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| > 1
>    $$
> 6. **Arccosecant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\csc^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| > 1
>    $$

Take a close look at the pairs in Table 6.5. You do not actually need to memorize six independent formulas—only three! Every "co-" function has the exact same derivative as its partner, except for a minus sign in front.

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on Co-Function Duality
> Notice the exact anti-symmetry between each inverse trigonometric function and its corresponding inverse co-function:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx}(\cos^{-1} u) = -\frac{d}{dx}(\sin^{-1} u)
> $$
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx}(\cot^{-1} u) = -\frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1} u)
> $$
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx}(\csc^{-1} u) = -\frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} u)
> $$
> Why does this happen? It is a direct consequence of the complementary angle identities established in Section 6.8:
> $$
> f^{-1}(u) + \operatorname{cof}^{-1}(u) = \frac{\pi}{2}
> $$
> Because the sum of any inverse trigonometric function and its inverse co-function is the constant $\frac{\pi}{2}$, differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ immediately forces their derivatives to be negatives of one another:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx}(f^{-1}(u)) + \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{cof}^{-1}(u)) = 0 \implies \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{cof}^{-1}(u)) = -\frac{d}{dx}(f^{-1}(u))
> $$

> [!note]- The Origin and History of the "Co-" Trigonometric Functions
> **The Core Principle: Complementary Angles**
> The prefix **"co-"** is an abbreviation of the Latin word **_complementum_** (English: **complementary**). In planar geometry, two acute angles are complementary if their sum equals a right angle ($90^\circ$ or $\frac{\pi}{2}$ radians).
> 
> A "co-function" is defined algebraically as the function evaluated at the complementary angle:
> $$
> \operatorname{cofunction}(\theta) = \operatorname{function}(90^\circ - \theta)
> $$
> - **Cosine** = **co**mplementary **sine** $\implies \cos(\theta) = \sin(90^\circ - \theta)$
> - **Cotangent** = **co**mplementary **tangent** $\implies \cot(\theta) = \tan(90^\circ - \theta)$
> - **Cosecant** = **co**mplementary **secant** $\implies \csc(\theta) = \sec(90^\circ - \theta)$
> 
> **Geometric Duality in a Right Triangle**
> In any right-angled triangle with acute vertices $\alpha$ and $\beta$, the third angle is $90^\circ$, requiring:
> $$
> \alpha + \beta = 90^\circ \implies \beta = 90^\circ - \alpha
> $$
> Because the leg **opposite** to angle $\alpha$ serves simultaneously as the leg **adjacent** to angle $\beta$, switching from $\alpha$ to its complement $\beta$ systematically transposes the roles of the two legs:
> - **Sine $\leftrightarrow$ Cosine:**
>   $$
>   \sin(\alpha) = \frac{\text{Opposite}(\alpha)}{\text{Hypotenuse}} = \frac{\text{Adjacent}(\beta)}{\text{Hypotenuse}} = \cos(\beta) = \cos(90^\circ - \alpha)
>   $$
> - **Tangent $\leftrightarrow$ Cotangent:**
>   $$
>   \tan(\alpha) = \frac{\text{Opposite}(\alpha)}{\text{Adjacent}(\alpha)} = \frac{\text{Adjacent}(\beta)}{\text{Opposite}(\beta)} = \cot(\beta) = \cot(90^\circ - \alpha)
>   $$
> - **Secant $\leftrightarrow$ Cosecant:**
>   $$
>   \sec(\alpha) = \frac{\text{Hypotenuse}}{\text{Adjacent}(\alpha)} = \frac{\text{Hypotenuse}}{\text{Opposite}(\beta)} = \csc(\beta) = \csc(90^\circ - \alpha)
>   $$
> 
> On a unit circle, this corresponds to reflecting the line segments across the diagonal line of symmetry $y = x$.
> 
> **Historical Transmission and Etymology**
> 1. **The Journey of the "Sine":**
>    - **Ancient India (5th–6th Century CE):** Indian astronomer-mathematician **Aryabhata** (*Aryabhatiya*, 499 CE) moved away from the full-chord system used by Ptolemy and Hipparchus, defining the half-chord of a circle arc as **_ardha-jyā_** ("half bow-string"), later shortened to **_jyā_** or **_jīvā_**.
>    - **The Islamic Golden Age (8th–12th Century CE):** Arab scholars translated Indian Sanskrit astronomical texts (such as the *Surya Siddhanta*). They transliterated the foreign term *jīvā* phonetically into Arabic as **_jība_** (a technical loanword with no intrinsic Arabic meaning). Because Arabic manuscripts typically omit short vowels and diacritical marks, the written skeleton appeared as **_jb_** (جيب). Later copyists and readers interpreted this consonantal skeleton as the standard Arabic word **_jayb_**, which literally translates to "fold", "crevice", "pocket", or "bosom of a garment".
>    - **Medieval Europe (12th Century CE):** When scholars such as **Robert of Chester** and **Gerard of Cremona** worked in Toledo translating Arabic mathematical and astronomical treatises into Latin, they translated *jayb* literally rather than phonetically. They chose the Latin word for a fold, gulf, bay, or bosom: **_sinus_**. From this Latin translation, the term entered European vernaculars as **sine**.
> 2. **The Introduction of "Tangent" and "Secant":**
>    - Shadow reckoning (gnomonics) historically developed along a parallel path to circular chords: the shadow of a vertical stick was known as the *umbra recta* (direct shadow), while the shadow of a horizontal gnomon on a vertical wall was known as the *umbra versa* (turned shadow).
>    - In **1583**, Danish mathematician **Thomas Fincke** published *Geometria Rotundi*, seeking modern geometric terminology derived from Latin verb roots: **_tangens_** (from *tangere*, "to touch"), describing the straight line that touches the circle perimeter at a single point without intersecting it; and **_secans_** (from *secare*, "to cut"), describing the ray drawn from the center through the angle that cuts through the circle circumference to meet the tangent line.
> 3. **The Genesis of the "Co-" Prefix (17th Century):**
>    - Before dedicated single words existed, mathematicians referred to the complementary quantities using descriptive Latin phrases: *sinus complementi* ("sine of the complement"), *tangens complementi* ("tangent of the complement"), and *secans complementi* ("secant of the complement").
>    - In **1620**, English mathematician and astronomer **Edmund Gunter** (*Canon Triangulorum*) sought to compact calculation tables for navigation and astronomy. In his printed logarithmic tables, he contracted *sinus complementi* into the compound form **_co.sinus_**.
>    - English mathematician **John Speidell** (*New Logarithmes*, 1619–1624) and Italian mathematician **Bonaventura Cavalieri** (*Directorium Generale Uranometricum*, 1632) adopted and extended this shorthand pattern across the remaining functions, introducing **_cotangens_** and **_cosecans_**.
>    - Over the following decades, the spelling dropped the period and hyphenation, yielding the unified Latin labels **_cosinus_**, **_cotangens_**, and **_cosecans_**, which translated directly into modern mathematical English as **cosine**, **cotangent**, and **cosecant**.
> 4. **Formalization and Modern Notation (18th Century):**
>    - While these names spread throughout the 17th century, notations varied wildly (e.g., $S$, $Si$, $Sin$, $Ta$, $T$, $Sec$).
>    - In **1748**, Swiss mathematician **Leonhard Euler** published his seminal work *Introductio in analysin infinitorum*. Euler shifted trigonometry from the study of geometric line segments on physical diagrams to analytical, dimensionless ratio functions, and systematically fixed the Latin abbreviations:
>      $$
>      \sin, \quad \cos, \quad \tan, \quad \cot, \quad \sec, \quad \csc
>      $$
>      permanently cementing the paired six-function nomenclature used today.

### Theoretical Foundations: Inversion Theorem vs. Implicit Differentiation

How do we actually derive the formulas in Table 6.5? We have two equivalent analytical perspectives at our disposal:

1. **The Inverse Function Derivative Rule (Section 6.1):** If $f$ is differentiable on an interval $I$ with $f'(x) \neq 0$, and $f$ has an inverse $f^{-1}$, then $f^{-1}$ is differentiable at every point $x$ in the interior of the range of $f$, and its derivative is given by the reciprocal of the derivative of $f$ evaluated at $f^{-1}(x)$:
   $$
   (f^{-1})'(x) = \frac{1}{f'(f^{-1}(x))}
   $$
2. **Implicit Differentiation via the Chain Rule:** Once we know that $y = f^{-1}(x)$ is differentiable, we can rewrite the relationship in direct form as $f(y) = x$ and differentiate both sides implicitly with respect to $x$:
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}[f(y)] = \frac{d}{dx}[x] \implies f'(y) \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{f'(y)} = \frac{1}{f'(f^{-1}(x))}
   $$

Notice that both routes lead to the exact same algebraic challenge: we end up with a trigonometric expression $f'(y)$ in the denominator that must be converted back into an algebraic function of $x = f(y)$ using Pythagorean identities and careful sign analysis based on the principal range of $y$.

### The Derivative of Arcsine: $y = \sin^{-1} u$

Let us begin with the arcsine function. Why is $y = \sin^{-1} x$ differentiable on the open interval $(-1, 1)$, even though its domain is the closed interval $[-1, 1]$? Look at the forward function $x = \sin y$ on its restricted domain $[-\pi/2, \pi/2]$. Its derivative with respect to $y$ is $\frac{dx}{dy} = \cos y$, which is strictly positive on the open interior $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$:
$$
\cos y > 0 \quad \text{for all } y \in \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)
$$
Because $\cos y$ never vanishes on this open interval, Theorem 1 of Section 6.1 guarantees that the inverse function $y = \sin^{-1} x$ is differentiable at every $x \in (-1, 1)$. Let us now find its exact formula.

**Proof:**
Let $y = \sin^{-1} x$ for $x \in (-1, 1)$. By the definition of the principal arcsine branch, this is equivalent to writing
$$
\sin y = x \quad \text{with} \quad y \in \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)
$$
Differentiating both sides implicitly with respect to $x$ via the Chain Rule gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\sin y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\cos y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1
\end{aligned}
$$
Because $y$ lies strictly between $-\pi/2$ and $\pi/2$, we know $\cos y > 0$, so we can safely divide by $\cos y$:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\cos y}
$$
Now we need to express $\cos y$ in terms of $x = \sin y$. From the fundamental Pythagorean identity $\cos^2 y + \sin^2 y = 1$, we have
$$
\cos y = \pm \sqrt{1 - \sin^2 y}
$$
Which sign do we choose? Since $\cos y > 0$ throughout the principal range $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$, we must select the positive square root:
$$
\cos y = +\sqrt{1 - \sin^2 y} = \sqrt{1 - x^2}
$$
Substituting this back into our derivative expression yields
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\sin^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}
$$
Finally, if $u = u(x)$ is any differentiable function of $x$ satisfying $|u| < 1$, applying the Chain Rule gives the general form:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\sin^{-1} u) = \frac{d(\sin^{-1} u)}{du} \frac{du}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - u^2}} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
$$

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916610454.webp]]

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916649969.webp]]

> [!note] Singularity and Vertical Tangents at $x = \pm 1$
> What goes wrong at the endpoints $x = 1$ and $x = -1$? Although $\sin^{-1} x$ is well-defined and continuous on the entire closed interval $[-1, 1]$, its derivative exists only on the open interval $(-1, 1)$. As $x$ approaches the endpoints $1^-$ or $-1^+$, the denominator $\sqrt{1 - x^2}$ approaches zero and the slope blows up:
> $$
> \lim_{x \to 1^-} \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}} = \infty, \quad \lim_{x \to -1^+} \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}} = \infty
> $$
> Geometrically, as you can see in the graph of $y = \sin^{-1} x$ above, the curve steepens into **vertical tangents** at the endpoints $(1, \pi/2)$ and $(-1, -\pi/2)$, corresponding to the horizontal tangents of $x = \sin y$ at $y = \pm \pi/2$.

### The Derivative of Arccosine: $y = \cos^{-1} u$

Unlike arcsine, which increases from $-\pi/2$ to $\pi/2$, the arccosine function $y = \cos^{-1} x$ maps $[-1, 1]$ monotonically *downward* onto $[0, \pi]$. Consequently, its derivative is strictly negative throughout $(-1, 1)$. We can establish its formula either instantaneously from the complementary angle identity or from scratch via implicit differentiation.

**Proof (Method 1: Identity Differentiation):**
From Section 6.8, the complementary angle identity relates arcsine and arccosine across their entire shared domain:
$$
\sin^{-1} x + \cos^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2}, \quad x \in [-1, 1]
$$
Solving for $\cos^{-1} x$ gives
$$
\cos^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1} x
$$
Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ on the open interval $|x| < 1$ immediately produces
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\cos^{-1} x) &= \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1} x\right) \\
&= 0 - \frac{d}{dx}(\sin^{-1} x) \\
&= -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}
\end{aligned}
$$
Applying the Chain Rule for a differentiable function $u = u(x)$ with $|u| < 1$ yields
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\cos^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - u^2}} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
$$

**Proof (Method 2: Implicit Differentiation):**
Let $y = \cos^{-1} x$ for $x \in (-1, 1)$. By definition of the principal arccosine branch,
$$
\cos y = x \quad \text{with} \quad y \in (0, \pi)
$$
Differentiating both sides implicitly with respect to $x$ gives
$$
-\sin y \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\sin y}
$$
On the open interval $y \in (0, \pi)$ (Quadrants I and II), the sine function is strictly positive ($\sin y > 0$). Therefore, when we solve $\sin^2 y + \cos^2 y = 1$ for $\sin y$, we must take the positive square root:
$$
\sin y = +\sqrt{1 - \cos^2 y} = \sqrt{1 - x^2}
$$
Substituting $\sin y = \sqrt{1 - x^2}$ into our derivative expression gives
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}} \quad \blacksquare
$$

### The Derivative of Arctangent: $y = \tan^{-1} u$

When we move from arcsine and arccosine to arctangent, the domain restrictions vanish completely! The tangent function $x = \tan y$ is differentiable and strictly increasing on the open interval $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$, where its derivative $\frac{dx}{dy} = \sec^2 y \ge 1 > 0$ never equals zero. Because the range of $\tan y$ on $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$ is the entire real line $\mathbb{R} = (-\infty, \infty)$, the inverse function $y = \tan^{-1} x$ is differentiable for *every* real number $x \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Proof:**
Let $y = \tan^{-1} x$ for $x \in (-\infty, \infty)$. Then by definition,
$$
\tan y = x \quad \text{with} \quad y \in \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)
$$
Differentiating both sides implicitly with respect to $x$ yields
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\tan y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\sec^2 y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $\sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y \ge 1 > 0$ for all $y \in (-\pi/2, \pi/2)$, we can divide by $\sec^2 y$:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2 y}
$$
Notice how clean the algebraic conversion is here: we don't even need a square root! Using the Pythagorean identity $\sec^2 y = 1 + \tan^2 y$ together with $\tan y = x$, we have
$$
\sec^2 y = 1 + x^2
$$
Substituting this directly into the denominator gives
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
$$
Applying the Chain Rule for any differentiable function $u = u(x)$ gives the general formula:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{1 + u^2} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
$$

> [!note] Analytical Insights for Arctangent
> Why is $\tan^{-1} x$ one of the most well-behaved functions in all of analysis? Look at its derivative $\frac{1}{1 + x^2}$:
> 1. **Global Regularity:** Because $1 + x^2 \ge 1$ never vanishes for any real $x$, $\tan^{-1} x$ is infinitely differentiable ($C^\infty$) across the entire real line with no singularities or vertical tangents anywhere.
> 2. **Strict Monotonicity:** Since $\frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} > 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$, $\tan^{-1} x$ is strictly increasing everywhere on its domain.
> 3. **Asymptotic Flattening:** As $x \to \pm \infty$, the derivative decays to zero:
>    $$
>    \lim_{x \to \pm \infty} \frac{1}{1 + x^2} = 0
>    $$
>    Geometrically, this confirms that the graph of $y = \tan^{-1} x$ flattens out horizontally as it approaches its horizontal asymptotes $y = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $y = -\frac{\pi}{2}$.

### The Derivative of Arccotangent: $y = \cot^{-1} u$

The arccotangent function $y = \cot^{-1} x$ maps the entire real line $\mathbb{R}$ monotonically downward onto $(0, \pi)$. Just like arccosine, we can find its derivative either from the complementary angle identity or by implicit differentiation.

**Proof:**
From Section 6.8, the complementary angle identity for arccotangent and arctangent is
$$
\cot^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1} x, \quad x \in (-\infty, \infty)
$$
Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ immediately gives
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\cot^{-1} x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1} x\right) = 0 - \frac{1}{1 + x^2} = -\frac{1}{1 + x^2}
$$
Alternatively, if we differentiate $\cot y = x$ implicitly for $y \in (0, \pi)$ and use the Pythagorean identity $\csc^2 y = 1 + \cot^2 y = 1 + x^2$, we obtain the exact same result:
$$
-\csc^2 y \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\csc^2 y} = -\frac{1}{1 + \cot^2 y} = -\frac{1}{1 + x^2}
$$
By the Chain Rule, for any differentiable function $u = u(x)$,
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\cot^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{1 + u^2} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
$$

### The Derivative of Arcsecant: $y = \sec^{-1} u$ and the Absolute Value

Look back at Formula 5 in Table 6.5:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| > 1
$$
Why is there an absolute value $|u|$ in the denominator of the arcsecant derivative? Let us think geometrically first. As you can see in the graph of $y = \sec^{-1} x$ below, on both branches of its domain—for $x < -1$ as well as for $x > 1$—as $x$ moves from left to right, the height $y = \sec^{-1} x$ increases! That means the slope $\frac{dy}{dx}$ of $y = \sec^{-1} x$ must be **strictly positive** on both $(-\infty, -1)$ and $(1, \infty)$. If we wrote $x\sqrt{x^2 - 1}$ without the absolute value, the formula would give a negative slope when $x < -1$, which would contradict the increasing graph!

Let us see exactly how this absolute value emerges algebraically from quadrant analysis on the principal range
$$
y \in \left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right) \cup \left(\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi\right] \quad \text{for } |x| \ge 1
$$
Note that at $x = \pm 1$ (corresponding to $y = 0$ and $y = \pi$), the curve has vertical tangents, so differentiability holds on the open domain $(-\infty, -1) \cup (1, \infty)$, where $y \in (0, \pi/2) \cup (\pi/2, \pi)$.

**Proof:**
Let $y = \sec^{-1} x$ for $|x| > 1$. By definition of the principal arcsecant function,
$$
\sec y = x \quad \text{where } y \in \left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right) \cup \left(\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi\right)
$$
Differentiating both sides implicitly with respect to $x$ gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\sec y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\sec y \tan y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1
\end{aligned}
$$
On $(0, \pi/2) \cup (\pi/2, \pi)$, neither $\sec y$ nor $\tan y$ is zero, so we can divide to solve for $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec y \tan y}
$$
Now we must express the product $\sec y \tan y$ in terms of $x = \sec y$ using the Pythagorean identity $\tan^2 y = \sec^2 y - 1 = x^2 - 1$, which gives $\tan y = \pm\sqrt{x^2 - 1}$. To determine the correct sign for $\tan y$, we examine the two quadrants of the principal range separately:

1. **Quadrant I ($x > 1$, so $y \in (0, \pi/2)$):**
   In the first quadrant, both secant and tangent are positive:
   $$
   \sec y = x > 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \tan y = +\sqrt{\sec^2 y - 1} = \sqrt{x^2 - 1} > 0
   $$
   Therefore, their product is positive:
   $$
   \sec y \tan y = x\sqrt{x^2 - 1}
   $$
   and the derivative on the right branch is
   $$
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{x\sqrt{x^2 - 1}} \quad \text{for } x > 1
   $$

2. **Quadrant II ($x < -1$, so $y \in (\pi/2, \pi)$):**
   In the second quadrant, both secant and tangent are negative:
   $$
   \sec y = x < 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \tan y = -\sqrt{\sec^2 y - 1} = -\sqrt{x^2 - 1} < 0
   $$
   Multiplying these two negative quantities together yields a *positive* product:
   $$
   \sec y \tan y = (x)\left(-\sqrt{x^2 - 1}\right) = -x\sqrt{x^2 - 1} > 0
   $$
   Because $x < 0$ in this quadrant, the positive quantity $-x$ is precisely $|x|$:
   $$
   -x = |x| > 0 \implies \sec y \tan y = |x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}
   $$
   Thus, the derivative on the left branch is
   $$
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{-x\sqrt{x^2 - 1}} = \frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}} \quad \text{for } x < -1
   $$

3. **Unification via Absolute Values:**
   Since $|x| = x$ when $x > 1$ and $|x| = -x$ when $x < -1$, both branches unite into a single formula valid for all $|x| > 1$:
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}}
   $$
   Applying the Chain Rule for any differentiable function $u = u(x)$ with $|u| > 1$ completes the proof:
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
   $$

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916726668.webp]]

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916747468.webp]]

### The Derivative of Arccosecant: $y = \csc^{-1} u$

Finally, the arccosecant function $y = \csc^{-1} x$ maps $(-\infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$ onto $[-\pi/2, 0) \cup (0, \pi/2]$ and is strictly decreasing on each of its two branches.

**Proof:**
Using the complementary angle identity from Section 6.8,
$$
\csc^{-1} x = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sec^{-1} x, \quad |x| \ge 1
$$
Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ for $|x| > 1$ immediately gives
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\csc^{-1} x) = 0 - \frac{d}{dx}(\sec^{-1} x) = -\frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}}
$$
Alternatively, differentiating $\csc y = x$ implicitly for $y \in (-\pi/2, 0) \cup (0, \pi/2)$ and noting that $\csc y$ and $\cot y$ always share the same sign on both intervals (both positive in Quadrant I, both negative in Quadrant IV) so that $\csc y \cot y = |x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1} > 0$, we obtain
$$
-\csc y \cot y \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\csc y \cot y} = -\frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}}
$$
Applying the Chain Rule for a differentiable function $u = u(x)$ with $|u| > 1$ yields
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\csc^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx} \quad \blacksquare
$$

## Integral Calculus: Inverse Trigonometric Antiderivatives

Whenever we discover a new family of derivatives, we gain a new set of integration formulas by running those derivatives in reverse. What makes the inverse trigonometric derivatives so valuable for integral calculus is that they allow us to integrate purely *algebraic* expressions involving sums and differences of squares—integrals like $\int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$ or $\int \frac{dx}{1 + x^2}$ that cannot be evaluated using polynomials, rational functions, or logarithms!

Notice also that because the derivatives of $\cos^{-1} u$, $\cot^{-1} u$, and $\csc^{-1} u$ differ from the derivatives of $\sin^{-1} u$, $\tan^{-1} u$, and $\sec^{-1} u$ only by a minus sign, we only need the three positive-sign inverse functions ($\sin^{-1}$, $\tan^{-1}$, and $\sec^{-1}$) to build our standard table of antiderivatives.

### Standard Integration Formulas (Table 6.6)

To make our integration formulas as flexible as possible, we generalize the constant $1$ in the denominator to any positive constant $a^2$ (with $a > 0$).

> [!theorem] Table 6.6: Integrals Evaluated with Inverse Trigonometric Functions
> The following formulas hold for any constant $a \neq 0$ (conventionally taken as $a > 0$):
> 
> 1. **Arcsine Form:**
>    $$
>    \int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C \quad (\text{valid for } u^2 < a^2)
>    $$
> 2. **Arctangent Form:**
>    $$
>    \int \frac{du}{a^2 + u^2} = \frac{1}{a} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C \quad (\text{valid for all } u)
>    $$
> 3. **Arcsecant Form:**
>    $$
>    \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right| + C \quad (\text{valid for } u^2 > a^2)
>    $$

### Rigorous Verification Proofs by Differentiation

Let us verify each of the three formulas in Table 6.6 by differentiating the right-hand side with respect to $u$ and confirming that it reproduces the integrand (assuming $a > 0$).

**Proof of the Arcsine Integral Formula:**
We differentiate the proposed antiderivative $\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C$ with respect to $u$. Applying the Chain Rule with inner argument $w = \frac{u}{a}$ and $\frac{dw}{du} = \frac{1}{a}$ for $u^2 < a^2$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{du}\left[\sin^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C\right] &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - \left(\frac{u}{a}\right)^2}} \cdot \frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - \frac{u^2}{a^2}}} \cdot \frac{1}{a} \\
&= \frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{a^2 - u^2}{a^2}}} \cdot \frac{1}{a} \\
&= \frac{1}{\frac{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}{\sqrt{a^2}}} \cdot \frac{1}{a}
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $a > 0$, we have $\sqrt{a^2} = a$, and the factors of $a$ cancel cleanly:
$$
\frac{1}{\frac{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}{a}} \cdot \frac{1}{a} = \frac{a}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} \cdot \frac{1}{a} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} \quad \blacksquare
$$

**Proof of the Arctangent Integral Formula:**
Differentiating the right-hand side of Formula 2 with respect to $u$ via the Chain Rule gives
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{du}\left[\frac{1}{a} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C\right] &= \frac{1}{a} \cdot \frac{1}{1 + \left(\frac{u}{a}\right)^2} \cdot \frac{d}{du}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{a} \cdot \frac{1}{1 + \frac{u^2}{a^2}} \cdot \frac{1}{a} \\
&= \frac{1}{a^2} \cdot \frac{1}{\frac{a^2 + u^2}{a^2}} \\
&= \frac{1}{a^2 + u^2} \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

**Proof of the Arcsecant Integral Formula:**
Now let us verify Formula 3, paying careful attention to how the absolute value $\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|$ behaves when $u$ is negative. Differentiating the right-hand side with respect to $u$:
$$
\frac{d}{du}\left[\frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right| + C\right] = \frac{1}{a} \frac{d}{du}\left[\sec^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|\right]
$$
Let $w = \frac{u}{a}$, so the argument of $\sec^{-1}$ is $|w| = \left|\frac{u}{a}\right| > 1$. Applying the Chain Rule to $\sec^{-1}|w|$ gives
$$
\frac{d}{du}\left[\sec^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|\right] = \frac{1}{\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|\sqrt{\left(\frac{u}{a}\right)^2 - 1}} \cdot \frac{d}{du}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|
$$
Recall that for any non-zero real variable $v \neq 0$, the derivative of $|v| = \sqrt{v^2}$ is $\frac{d}{dv}|v| = \frac{v}{|v|} = \operatorname{sgn}(v)$. Therefore, for $a > 0$,
$$
\frac{d}{du}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right| = \frac{u/a}{|u/a|} \cdot \frac{1}{a} = \frac{u}{|u|} \cdot \frac{1}{a}
$$
Substituting this back into our derivative expression yields
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{1}{a} \frac{d}{du}\left[\sec^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right|\right] &= \frac{1}{a} \cdot \frac{1}{\frac{|u|}{a} \frac{\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}{a}} \cdot \left(\frac{u}{|u|} \frac{1}{a}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{a} \cdot \frac{a^2}{|u|\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} \cdot \frac{u}{|u| \cdot a}
\end{aligned}
$$
Since $|u|^2 = u^2$, we have $\frac{u}{|u|^2} = \frac{u}{u^2} = \frac{1}{u}$, and the factors of $a$ cancel to leave
$$
\frac{1}{a} \cdot \frac{a^2}{\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} \cdot \frac{u}{u^2 \cdot a} = \frac{1}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} \quad \blacksquare
$$

> [!note] Dimensional Analysis and the Origin of the $\frac{1}{a}$ Factor
> Have you ever wondered why the arctangent and arcsecant integral formulas have a $\frac{1}{a}$ factor in front, whereas the arcsine integral formula does not? You can remember this effortlessly using **dimensional analysis**!
> 
> Imagine that both $u$ and $a$ represent physical lengths with dimension $[L]$. Then $du$ also has dimension $[L]$, while any inverse trigonometric function (which outputs a radian angle) is **dimensionless** ($[1]$):
> - **Arcsine Integral:** In $\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}$, the numerator $du$ has dimension $[L]$ and the denominator $\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}$ has dimension $\sqrt{[L]^2} = [L]$. Thus, the entire integral is already dimensionless:
>   $$
>   \left[\frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}\right] = \frac{L}{L} = 1
>   $$
>   Because the output $\sin^{-1}(u/a)$ is also dimensionless, no $\frac{1}{a}$ factor can appear!
> - **Arctangent Integral:** In $\int \frac{du}{a^2 + u^2}$, the numerator has dimension $[L]$ while the denominator $a^2 + u^2$ has dimension $[L]^2$:
>   $$
>   \left[\frac{du}{a^2 + u^2}\right] = \frac{L}{L^2} = L^{-1}
>   $$
>   Because $\tan^{-1}(u/a)$ is dimensionless, it *must* be multiplied by $\frac{1}{a}$ (dimension $L^{-1}$) to balance the dimensions of both sides!
> - **Arcsecant Integral:** In $\int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}$, the denominator is a product of $u$ (dimension $[L]$) and $\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}$ (dimension $[L]$), giving dimension $[L]^2$:
>   $$
>   \left[\frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}\right] = \frac{L}{L \cdot L} = L^{-1}
>   $$
>   Once again, the dimensional prefactor $\frac{1}{a}$ is required to match the $L^{-1}$ dimension of the integral.

## Systematic Integration Strategies and Algebraic Reductions

In practice, integrals rarely appear in the pristine forms of Table 6.6 right out of the box. However, we can systematically reduce a wide variety of algebraic and transcendental integrals to the three canonical templates of Table 6.6 using three core strategies: linear scaling, completing the square, and nonlinear substitution.

### Strategy 1: Linear Scaling and Substitution ($u = kx$)

When the squared variable term carries a non-unit coefficient—such as $a^2 - b^2 x^2$, $a^2 + b^2 x^2$, or $b^2 x^2 - a^2$ with $b > 0$—we group the coefficient inside the square as $(bx)^2$ and perform the linear substitution
$$
u = bx \implies du = b \, dx \implies dx = \frac{du}{b}
$$
This immediately reduces each scaled integral to its standard Table 6.6 template:

1. **Arcsine Scaling:**
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2 x^2}} = \frac{1}{b} \int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} = \frac{1}{b} \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C = \frac{1}{b} \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{bx}{a}\right) + C
   $$
2. **Arctangent Scaling:**
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{a^2 + b^2 x^2} = \frac{1}{b} \int \frac{du}{a^2 + u^2} = \frac{1}{ab} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C = \frac{1}{ab} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{bx}{a}\right) + C
   $$
3. **Arcsecant Scaling:**
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{x\sqrt{b^2 x^2 - a^2}} = \int \frac{b \, dx}{(bx)\sqrt{(bx)^2 - a^2}} = \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left|\frac{bx}{a}\right| + C
   $$
   (Notice that in the arcsecant scaling, the factor of $b$ from $du = b \, dx$ cancels the $b$ needed to turn the external $x$ in the denominator into $u = bx$, so no extra factor of $\frac{1}{b}$ appears outside!)

### Strategy 2: The Method of Completing the Square

What if the quadratic expression under the radical or in the denominator also contains a linear term $Bx$, such as $Ax^2 + Bx + C$? Whenever a quadratic polynomial contains both $x^2$ and $x$ terms, we can eliminate the linear term by **completing the square** and shifting the variable!

Specifically, every quadratic polynomial $Ax^2 + Bx + C$ ($A \neq 0$) can be rewritten in completed-square form by factoring out $A$ from the variable terms and adding and subtracting the square of half the linear coefficient:
$$
\begin{aligned}
Ax^2 + Bx + C &= A\left(x^2 + \frac{B}{A}x\right) + C \\
&= A\left(x^2 + \frac{B}{A}x + \frac{B^2}{4A^2}\right) + C - \frac{B^2}{4A} \\
&= A\left(x + \frac{B}{2A}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{4AC - B^2}{4A}\right)
\end{aligned}
$$
Notice the constant term $\frac{4AC - B^2}{4A}$: its numerator is the negative of the quadratic discriminant $\Delta = B^2 - 4AC$. Depending on the signs of $A$ and $\Delta$, completing the square leads directly to one of our three canonical inverse trigonometric integrals:

1. **Form I: Denominators with Radicals and Negative Leading Coefficients ($\sqrt{Bx - x^2}$ or $\sqrt{C + Bx - Ax^2}$):**
   When the $x^2$ term inside a square root is negative, completing the square produces a difference of squares $a^2 - (x - h)^2$. For instance, with $Bx - x^2$:
   $$
   Bx - x^2 = -\left(x^2 - Bx\right) = -\left(x^2 - Bx + \frac{B^2}{4}\right) + \frac{B^2}{4} = \left(\frac{B}{2}\right)^2 - \left(x - \frac{B}{2}\right)^2
   $$
   Setting $a = \frac{B}{2}$ and $u = x - \frac{B}{2}$ (so $du = dx$) transforms the integral directly into the Arcsine formula:
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{Bx - x^2}} = \int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C = \sin^{-1}\left(\frac{x - B/2}{B/2}\right) + C
   $$

2. **Form II: Irreducible Rational Quadratic Denominators ($Ax^2 + Bx + C$ with $\Delta = B^2 - 4AC < 0$):**
   When the discriminant $\Delta = B^2 - 4AC$ is strictly negative and $A > 0$, the quadratic $Ax^2 + Bx + C$ has no real roots—it is **irreducible** over $\mathbb{R}$ and strictly positive for all $x$. Completing the square expresses it as a sum of squares $u^2 + a^2$. Specifically, setting $u = \sqrt{A}\left(x + \frac{B}{2A}\right)$ and $a^2 = \frac{4AC - B^2}{4A} > 0$ reduces the integral directly to the Arctangent formula:
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{Ax^2 + Bx + C} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{A}} \int \frac{du}{u^2 + a^2} = \frac{1}{a\sqrt{A}} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C
   $$

3. **Form III: Radicals with Shifted External Linear Factors ($(x - h)\sqrt{(x - h)^2 - a^2}$):**
   When completing the square under a radical yields $(x - h)^2 - a^2$ and the matching shifted linear factor $(x - h)$ sits outside the radical in the denominator, setting $u = x - h$ and $du = dx$ transforms the integral directly into the Arcsecant formula:
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{(x - h)\sqrt{(x - h)^2 - a^2}} = \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left|\frac{x - h}{a}\right| + C
   $$

### Strategy 3: Nonlinear and Transcendental Substitutions

Many integrands that do not look like quadratic fractions at first glance actually conceal one of the Table 6.6 forms behind a nonlinear substitution $u = g(x)$:

1. **Exponential Substitutions:**
   Consider an integral of the form $\int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{e^{2x} - a^2}}$. Because $e^{2x} = (e^x)^2$, we substitute $u = e^x$. Then $du = e^x \, dx$, which means $dx = \frac{du}{e^x} = \frac{du}{u}$. Notice how expressing $dx$ as $\frac{du}{u}$ creates the external $u$ factor in the denominator needed for the canonical Arcsecant integral:
   $$
   \int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{e^{2x} - a^2}} = \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left|\frac{e^x}{a}\right| + C = \frac{1}{a} \sec^{-1}\left(\frac{e^x}{a}\right) + C
   $$
   (Here we can drop the absolute value bars for $a > 0$ because $e^x > 0$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.)

2. **Power Substitutions:**
   Consider an integral of the form $\int \frac{x^{k-1}}{a^2 + x^{2k}} \, dx$. Recognizing $x^{2k} = (x^k)^2$ in the denominator and its derivative pattern $x^{k-1} \, dx$ in the numerator, we substitute $u = x^k$, so $du = k x^{k-1} \, dx \implies x^{k-1} \, dx = \frac{1}{k} \, du$. This reduces the integral directly to the Arctangent formula:
   $$
   \int \frac{x^{k-1}}{a^2 + x^{2k}} \, dx = \frac{1}{k} \int \frac{du}{a^2 + u^2} = \frac{1}{ka} \tan^{-1}\left(\frac{x^k}{a}\right) + C
   $$

3. **Transcendental Integrands with Inverse Trigonometric Differentials:**
   Consider integrals of the form $\int \frac{e^{\tan^{-1} x}}{1 + x^2} \, dx$ or $\int \frac{\cos x}{a^2 + \sin^2 x} \, dx$. In the first case, the denominator $1 + x^2$ is part of the exact differential of $u = \tan^{-1} x$:
   $$
   u = \tan^{-1} x \implies du = \frac{dx}{1 + x^2} \implies \int \frac{e^{\tan^{-1} x}}{1 + x^2} \, dx = \int e^u \, du = e^{\tan^{-1} x} + C
   $$
   In the second case, setting $u = \sin x$ gives $du = \cos x \, dx$, turning $\int \frac{\cos x}{a^2 + \sin^2 x} \, dx$ into $\int \frac{du}{a^2 + u^2} = \frac{1}{a}\tan^{-1}\left(\frac{\sin x}{a}\right) + C$.

### Definite Integrals and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus

When evaluating a definite integral whose antiderivative $F(x)$ involves inverse trigonometric functions, the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus applies in the standard way across any closed interval $[p, q]$ on which the integrand $f(x)$ is continuous:
$$
\int_p^q f(x) \, dx = [F(x)]_p^q = F(q) - F(p)
$$
Furthermore, when we apply a substitution $u = g(x)$ to a definite integral, transforming the limits of integration via
$$
\int_p^q f(g(x)) g'(x) \, dx = \int_{g(p)}^{g(q)} f(u) \, du
$$
is especially convenient: the new bounds $u = g(p)$ and $u = g(q)$ typically land on standard special-triangle ratios (such as $0$, $\pm \frac{1}{2}$, $\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$, $\pm \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$, $\pm 1$, or $\pm \sqrt{3}$), allowing us to evaluate the inverse trigonometric antiderivative directly at the new limits in terms of special angles ($0, \frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{\pi}{4}, \frac{\pi}{3}, \frac{\pi}{2}$) without ever back-substituting to $x$.

---

# 6.10 Hyperbolic Functions

## Genesis, Motivation, and the Geometric Analogy

Why do we study a brand-new family of functions at the end of a chapter on logarithms and exponentials, and why do their names—*hyperbolic sine*, *hyperbolic cosine*, *hyperbolic tangent*—sound just like circular trigonometry? As we will see, these functions are simply special combinations of $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$ that arise naturally whenever gravity pulls on a flexible cable, a light wave passes through an absorbing medium, or we solve differential equations in physics and engineering. Better yet, they share an astonishing geometric and algebraic kinship with the familiar trigonometric functions.

### Historical Origin and Mathematical Discovery

In classical mechanics and differential geometry, several distinct mathematical problems converged in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, requiring functions beyond elementary polynomials, rational expressions, and circular trigonometry:

1. **The Hanging Chain (Catenary):**
   In 1638, Galileo Galilei conjectured in his *Two New Sciences* that a heavy, flexible chain suspended freely from two points hangs in the shape of a parabola. It is an easy visual mistake to make, because near the bottom vertex, the curve looks very much like a parabola. In 1690, Jakob Bernoulli posed the determination of the true physical curve as an open challenge to the mathematical world. Just a year later, in 1691, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, Christiaan Huygens, and Johann Bernoulli independently proved that Galileo's conjecture was false. By balancing the horizontal and vertical forces acting on an infinitesimal segment of the chain, they showed that the true equilibrium curve is a non-algebraic transcendental curve governed by the differential equation
   $$
   \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{s}{a}
   $$
   where $s$ is the arc length measured from the lowest point along the curve and $a$ is the ratio of horizontal tension to weight per unit length. The exact solution to this differential equation is the **catenary** curve (from the Latin *catena*, meaning "chain"):
   $$
   y = a \left(\frac{e^{x/a} + e^{-x/a}}{2}\right)
   $$
   This symmetric combination of growing and decaying exponentials led directly to the definition of the hyperbolic cosine.

2. **The Discovery of Hyperbolic Trigonometry:**
   While Johann Heinrich Lambert (1728–1777) and Vincenzo Riccati (1707–1775) investigated the geometry of conic sections, Riccati introduced standard abbreviations and studied formulas relating exponential combinations to the geometry of hyperbolas. Lambert published an extensive treatise in 1768 developing the systematic theory, introducing the modern terminology ($\sinh$, $\cosh$, $\tanh$), and demonstrating that these functions satisfy an entire web of identities mirroring circular trigonometry.

### Why Are Hyperbolic Functions Named After Trigonometric Functions?

The names *hyperbolic sine*, *hyperbolic cosine*, and *hyperbolic tangent* are far from arbitrary analogies. They stem from two deep structural correspondences between circular and hyperbolic geometry: **geometric area parametrization** and **complex analytic rotation**.

#### Geometric Parametrization: Circle vs. Equilateral Hyperbola

Let us start by revisiting the unit circle in the Euclidean plane:
$$
x^2 + y^2 = 1
$$
Suppose we draw a ray from the origin $(0,0)$ to a point $P(x, y)$ on the circle, forming a circular sector bounded by the positive $x$-axis, the ray, and the circular arc from $(1,0)$ to $P(x,y)$. Recall that a sector of angle $t$ radians in a circle of radius $r = 1$ has area $A = \frac{1}{2} r^2 t = \frac{1}{2}t$. Thus, the parameter $t$ can be interpreted geometrically not just as an angle, but as **twice the area of the circular sector**: $t = 2A$. In terms of this area parameter, the coordinates of $P$ on the unit circle are parametrized by
$$
x(t) = \cos t, \quad y(t) = \sin t
$$
where $t = 2A$.

Now, what happens if we replace the plus sign in the unit circle equation with a minus sign? We obtain the right branch ($x > 0$) of the equilateral (rectangular) unit hyperbola:
$$
x^2 - y^2 = 1
$$
Suppose we draw a ray from the origin $(0,0)$ to a point $P(x, y)$ on this hyperbola, and consider the **hyperbolic sector** bounded by the positive $x$-axis, the ray to $P(x, y)$, and the hyperbolic arc from $(1, 0)$ to $P(x, y)$. How are the coordinates of $P$ related to the area $A$ of this hyperbolic sector?

> [!theorem] Theorem: Hyperbolic Sector Area Parametrization
> For any $t > 0$, the point $P(\cosh t, \sinh t)$ lies on the right branch of the unit hyperbola $x^2 - y^2 = 1$, and the area $A$ of the hyperbolic sector bounded by the positive $x$-axis, the ray from $(0,0)$ to $P(\cosh t, \sinh t)$, and the hyperbolic arc from $(1,0)$ to $P$ satisfies
> $$
> A = \frac{t}{2} \iff t = 2A
> $$

**Proof:** Let $P = (\cosh t, \sinh t)$ be a point on the hyperbola $x^2 - y^2 = 1$ with $t > 0$. By inspecting the geometry in the first quadrant, we can see that the area $A$ of the hyperbolic sector is equal to the area of the right triangle with vertices $(0,0)$, $(x, 0)$, and $(x, y)$ minus the area under the hyperbolic curve $y = \sqrt{x^2 - 1}$ from $x = 1$ to $x = \cosh t$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
A &= \text{Area}(\text{Triangle}) - \int_{1}^{\cosh t} \sqrt{x^2 - 1} \, dx \\
&= \frac{1}{2} x y - \int_{1}^{\cosh t} \sqrt{x^2 - 1} \, dx \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \cosh t \sinh t - \int_{1}^{\cosh t} \sqrt{x^2 - 1} \, dx
\end{aligned}
$$
To evaluate the definite integral, let us make the hyperbolic substitution $x = \cosh u$, so that $dx = \sinh u \, du$. The lower limit $x = 1$ corresponds to $u = 0$, and the upper limit $x = \cosh t$ corresponds to $u = t$. Since $u \ge 0$, the fundamental identity $\cosh^2 u - \sinh^2 u = 1$ (proved in the next subsection) gives $\sqrt{\cosh^2 u - 1} = \sqrt{\sinh^2 u} = \sinh u$. Using the power-reduction identity $\sinh^2 u = \frac{\cosh 2u - 1}{2}$ and the double-argument identity $\sinh 2t = 2\sinh t \cosh t$, we compute:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int_{1}^{\cosh t} \sqrt{x^2 - 1} \, dx &= \int_{0}^{t} \sinh^2 u \, du \\
&= \int_{0}^{t} \left(\frac{\cosh 2u - 1}{2}\right) du \\
&= \left[ \frac{\sinh 2u}{4} - \frac{u}{2} \right]_{0}^{t} \\
&= \frac{\sinh 2t}{4} - \frac{t}{2} \\
&= \frac{2 \sinh t \cosh t}{4} - \frac{t}{2} \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \sinh t \cosh t - \frac{t}{2}
\end{aligned}
$$
Substituting this result back into our expression for the sector area $A$, the product terms $\frac{1}{2}\cosh t \sinh t$ cancel cleanly:
$$
\begin{aligned}
A &= \frac{1}{2} \cosh t \sinh t - \left( \frac{1}{2} \cosh t \sinh t - \frac{t}{2} \right) \\
&= \frac{t}{2}
\end{aligned}
$$
Solving for $t$ yields $t = 2A$. $\blacksquare$

In other words, just as $(\cos t, \sin t)$ parametrizes the unit circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$ with $t$ equal to twice the circular sector area, the coordinates
$$
x = \cosh t, \quad y = \sinh t
$$
parametrize the unit hyperbola $x^2 - y^2 = 1$ with $t$ equal to **twice the hyperbolic sector area**. This remarkable geometric equivalence is the true origin of the name **hyperbolic** functions.

#### The Complex Analytic Connection (Euler's Formula)

There is a second, equally illuminating reason why hyperbolic and circular functions behave like siblings. In complex analysis, Euler's formula $e^{iz} = \cos z + i\sin z$ allows us to express the circular cosine and sine directly in terms of complex exponentials:
$$
\cos z = \frac{e^{iz} + e^{-iz}}{2}, \quad \sin z = \frac{e^{iz} - e^{-iz}}{2i}
$$
What happens if we evaluate these circular formulas at a purely imaginary argument $z = ix$ (where $x \in \mathbb{R}$ and $i^2 = -1$)? Substituting $z = ix$ directly into both expressions gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\cos(ix) &= \frac{e^{i(ix)} + e^{-i(ix)}}{2} = \frac{e^{-x} + e^{x}}{2} = \cosh x \\
\sin(ix) &= \frac{e^{i(ix)} - e^{-i(ix)}}{2i} = \frac{e^{-x} - e^{x}}{2i} = i \left(\frac{e^{x} - e^{-x}}{2}\right) = i \sinh x
\end{aligned}
$$
These fundamental relations,
$$
\cosh x = \cos(ix), \quad \sinh x = -i \sin(ix)
$$
reveal that hyperbolic functions are literally the analytic continuation of circular trigonometric functions rotated by $90^\circ$ into the imaginary axis of the complex plane!

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on Osborn's Rule
> Because $\cosh x = \cos(ix)$ and $i\sinh x = \sin(ix)$, any circular trigonometric identity involving sums, differences, or products of sines and cosines converts into a valid identity for hyperbolic functions by making the replacement
> $$
> \cos \theta \mapsto \cosh u, \quad \sin \theta \mapsto \sinh u
> $$
> with one crucial twist known as **Osborn's Rule**: whenever two sine terms multiply together to form a product (such as $\sin^2 \theta$, $\sin A \sin B$, or $\tan^2 \theta = \frac{\sin^2 \theta}{\cos^2 \theta}$), the corresponding hyperbolic identity acquires a **sign change** ($+ \mapsto -$ or $- \mapsto +$) because $(i \sinh u)^2 = i^2 \sinh^2 u = -\sinh^2 u$.

### The Even-Odd Algebraic Decomposition of $e^x$

We can also arrive at $\cosh x$ and $\sinh x$ from a purely algebraic perspective, without invoking geometry or complex numbers at all. Ask yourself: can any function $f(x)$ defined on a symmetric domain (such as $(-\infty, \infty)$) be broken down into a symmetric (even) piece and an antisymmetric (odd) piece?

> [!theorem] Theorem: Existence and Uniqueness of the Even-Odd Decomposition
> Every function $f(x)$ whose domain is symmetric about the origin ($-x \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)$ whenever $x \in \operatorname{Dom}(f)$) can be decomposed **uniquely** into the sum of an even function $f_{\text{even}}(x)$ and an odd function $f_{\text{odd}}(x)$:
> $$
> f(x) = f_{\text{even}}(x) + f_{\text{odd}}(x) = \frac{f(x) + f(-x)}{2} + \frac{f(x) - f(-x)}{2}
> $$

**Proof:** We establish both existence and uniqueness of this decomposition step by step:
1. **Existence:** Define the two component functions $E(x) = \frac{f(x) + f(-x)}{2}$ and $O(x) = \frac{f(x) - f(-x)}{2}$.
   - Evaluating $E$ at $-x$ shows that $E(x)$ is even:
     $$
     E(-x) = \frac{f(-x) + f(-(-x))}{2} = \frac{f(-x) + f(x)}{2} = E(x)
     $$
   - Evaluating $O$ at $-x$ shows that $O(x)$ is odd:
     $$
     O(-x) = \frac{f(-x) - f(-(-x))}{2} = \frac{f(-x) - f(x)}{2} = -\frac{f(x) - f(-x)}{2} = -O(x)
     $$
   - Summing the two functions recovers $f(x)$ identically:
     $$
     E(x) + O(x) = \frac{f(x) + f(-x) + f(x) - f(-x)}{2} = \frac{2f(x)}{2} = f(x)
     $$
2. **Uniqueness:** Suppose $f(x)$ could be written as two such decompositions, $f(x) = E_1(x) + O_1(x) = E_2(x) + O_2(x)$, where $E_1, E_2$ are even and $O_1, O_2$ are odd. Rearranging terms to collect the even functions on the left and the odd functions on the right gives
   $$
   E_1(x) - E_2(x) = O_2(x) - O_1(x)
   $$
   Let us call this common function $g(x)$. Since the left-hand side is the difference of two even functions, $g(x)$ must be even:
   $$
   g(x) = E_1(x) - E_2(x) \implies g(-x) = g(x)
   $$
   At the same time, since the right-hand side is the difference of two odd functions, $g(x)$ must also be odd:
   $$
   g(x) = O_2(x) - O_1(x) \implies g(-x) = -g(x)
   $$
   A function that is simultaneously even and odd must satisfy $g(x) = -g(x)$, which implies
   $$
   2g(x) = 0 \implies g(x) = 0 \quad \text{for all } x
   $$
   Consequently, $E_1(x) - E_2(x) = 0 \implies E_1(x) = E_2(x)$ and $O_2(x) - O_1(x) = 0 \implies O_1(x) = O_2(x)$, proving uniqueness. $\blacksquare$

Now let us apply this canonical decomposition to the most important function in calculus, the natural exponential function $f(x) = e^x$:
$$
e^x = \underbrace{\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}}_{\text{even part: } \cosh x} + \underbrace{\frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}}_{\text{odd part: } \sinh x}
$$
The even part of $e^x$ is defined as the **hyperbolic cosine** of $x$ ($\cosh x$), and the odd part of $e^x$ is defined as the **hyperbolic sine** of $x$ ($\sinh x$). Notice that adding them gives $\cosh x + \sinh x = e^x$, while subtracting them gives $\cosh x - \sinh x = e^{-x}$.

## Definitions and Fundamental Algebraic Identities

Having seen how $\cosh x$ and $\sinh x$ emerge as the even and odd components of $e^x$, we can now define all six hyperbolic functions using the exact same ratios ($\tanh = \frac{\sinh}{\cosh}$, $\operatorname{sech} = \frac{1}{\cosh}$, etc.) that we use in circular trigonometry.

> [!abstract] Definition: The Six Basic Hyperbolic Functions
> For any real number $x$, the six hyperbolic functions are defined in terms of the exponential functions $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$ as follows:
> 
> 1. **Hyperbolic Cosine:**
>    $$
>    \cosh x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}
>    $$
> 2. **Hyperbolic Sine:**
>    $$
>    \sinh x = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}
>    $$
> 3. **Hyperbolic Tangent:**
>    $$
>    \tanh x = \frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x} = \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}}
>    $$
> 4. **Hyperbolic Cotangent:**
>    $$
>    \coth x = \frac{\cosh x}{\sinh x} = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{e^x - e^{-x}}, \quad x \neq 0
>    $$
> 5. **Hyperbolic Secant:**
>    $$
>    \operatorname{sech} x = \frac{1}{\cosh x} = \frac{2}{e^x + e^{-x}}
>    $$
> 6. **Hyperbolic Cosecant:**
>    $$
>    \operatorname{csch} x = \frac{1}{\sinh x} = \frac{2}{e^x - e^{-x}}, \quad x \neq 0
>    $$

Notice the domain restrictions right away: because $\cosh x = \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}$ is a sum of two strictly positive numbers, $\cosh x$ is never zero, so $\cosh x$, $\tanh x$, and $\operatorname{sech} x$ are defined for all real $x \in (-\infty, \infty)$. By contrast, $\sinh x = 0$ when $e^x = e^{-x}$, which occurs if and only if $x = 0$. Therefore, $\coth x$ and $\operatorname{csch} x$ are defined for all $x \neq 0$.

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on Pronunciation Conventions
> When speaking these functions aloud in mathematical practice:
> - $\cosh x$ is pronounced "kosh $x$" (rhyming with "gosh" or "gauche").
> - $\sinh x$ is pronounced "cinch $x$" (rhyming with "pinch") or "shine $x$".
> - $\tanh x$ is pronounced "tanch $x$" or "than $x$".
> - $\operatorname{sech} x$ is pronounced "sheck $x$" or "setch $x$".
> - $\operatorname{csch} x$ is pronounced "co-sheck $x$" or "co-setch $x$".

### Rigorous Proofs of the Hyperbolic Algebraic Identities

Because the six hyperbolic functions are built from $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$, every identity they satisfy can be proved directly from the laws of exponents. Let us summarize the core identities in a master theorem and then prove each one rigorously.

> [!theorem] Master Theorem: Hyperbolic Identities
> The hyperbolic functions satisfy the following algebraic and trigonometric-like identities for all real numbers where the functions are defined:
> 
> 1. **Fundamental Hyperbolic Identity:**
>    $$
>    \cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1
>    $$
> 2. **Derived Pythagorean Identities:**
>    $$
>    1 - \tanh^2 x = \operatorname{sech}^2 x
>    $$
>    $$
>    \coth^2 x - 1 = \operatorname{csch}^2 x, \quad x \neq 0
>    $$
> 3. **Addition and Subtraction Formulas:**
>    $$
>    \sinh(x \pm y) = \sinh x \cosh y \pm \cosh x \sinh y
>    $$
>    $$
>    \cosh(x \pm y) = \cosh x \cosh y \pm \sinh x \sinh y
>    $$
>    $$
>    \tanh(x \pm y) = \frac{\tanh x \pm \tanh y}{1 \pm \tanh x \tanh y}
>    $$
> 4. **Double-Argument Formulas:**
>    $$
>    \sinh(2x) = 2 \sinh x \cosh x
>    $$
>    $$
>    \cosh(2x) = \cosh^2 x + \sinh^2 x = 2\cosh^2 x - 1 = 1 + 2\sinh^2 x
>    $$
>    $$
>    \tanh(2x) = \frac{2 \tanh x}{1 + \tanh^2 x}
>    $$
> 5. **Power-Reduction (Half-Argument) Formulas:**
>    $$
>    \cosh^2 x = \frac{\cosh 2x + 1}{2}
>    $$
>    $$
>    \sinh^2 x = \frac{\cosh 2x - 1}{2}
>    $$

Let us walk through the proofs of each of these five groups of identities.

#### Proof of Identity 1: $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$

**Proof:** Substituting the exponential definitions of $\cosh x$ and $\sinh x$ and expanding the squares, we obtain:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x &= \left(\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}\right)^2 - \left(\frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}\right)^2 \\
&= \frac{e^{2x} + 2e^x e^{-x} + e^{-2x}}{4} - \frac{e^{2x} - 2e^x e^{-x} + e^{-2x}}{4} \\
&= \frac{(e^{2x} + 2 + e^{-2x}) - (e^{2x} - 2 + e^{-2x})}{4} \\
&= \frac{e^{2x} + 2 + e^{-2x} - e^{2x} + 2 - e^{-2x}}{4} \\
&= \frac{4}{4} = 1
\end{aligned}
$$
Alternatively, we can factor the difference of squares as $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = (\cosh x + \sinh x)(\cosh x - \sinh x) = e^x \cdot e^{-x} = 1$. $\blacksquare$

#### Proof of Identity 2: Derived Pythagorean Identities

**Proof:** Both identities follow immediately by dividing the fundamental identity $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$ by either $\cosh^2 x$ or $\sinh^2 x$:
1. Dividing both sides of $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$ by $\cosh^2 x$ (which is valid for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$ because $\cosh x \ge 1 > 0$):
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{\cosh^2 x}{\cosh^2 x} - \frac{\sinh^2 x}{\cosh^2 x} &= \frac{1}{\cosh^2 x} \\
   1 - \left(\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x}\right)^2 &= \left(\frac{1}{\cosh x}\right)^2 \\
   1 - \tanh^2 x &= \operatorname{sech}^2 x
   \end{aligned}
   $$
2. For $x \neq 0$, we have $\sinh x \neq 0$. Dividing both sides of $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$ by $\sinh^2 x$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{\cosh^2 x}{\sinh^2 x} - \frac{\sinh^2 x}{\sinh^2 x} &= \frac{1}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   \left(\frac{\cosh x}{\sinh x}\right)^2 - 1 &= \left(\frac{1}{\sinh x}\right)^2 \\
   \coth^2 x - 1 &= \operatorname{csch}^2 x
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   This completes the proof of both derived Pythagorean identities. $\blacksquare$

#### Proof of Identity 3: Addition and Subtraction Formulas

**Proof:** We verify the addition formulas for $\sinh$ and $\cosh$ by expanding their right-hand sides in terms of exponentials:
1. Expanding the right-hand side of $\sinh x \cosh y + \cosh x \sinh y$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \sinh x \cosh y + \cosh x \sinh y &= \left(\frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{e^y + e^{-y}}{2}\right) + \left(\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{e^y - e^{-y}}{2}\right) \\
   &= \frac{e^{x+y} + e^{x-y} - e^{-x+y} - e^{-x-y}}{4} + \frac{e^{x+y} - e^{x-y} + e^{-x+y} - e^{-x-y}}{4} \\
   &= \frac{2e^{x+y} - 2e^{-(x+y)}}{4} \\
   &= \frac{e^{x+y} - e^{-(x+y)}}{2} = \sinh(x + y)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Replacing $y$ with $-y$ and using the parity properties $\sinh(-y) = -\sinh y$ and $\cosh(-y) = \cosh y$ immediately yields the subtraction formula:
   $$
   \sinh(x - y) = \sinh x \cosh y - \cosh x \sinh y
   $$
2. Similarly, expanding the right-hand side of $\cosh x \cosh y + \sinh x \sinh y$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \cosh x \cosh y + \sinh x \sinh y &= \left(\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{e^y + e^{-y}}{2}\right) + \left(\frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{e^y - e^{-y}}{2}\right) \\
   &= \frac{e^{x+y} + e^{x-y} + e^{-x+y} + e^{-x-y}}{4} + \frac{e^{x+y} - e^{x-y} - e^{-x+y} + e^{-x-y}}{4} \\
   &= \frac{2e^{x+y} + 2e^{-(x+y)}}{4} \\
   &= \frac{e^{x+y} + e^{-(x+y)}}{2} = \cosh(x + y)
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Replacing $y$ with $-y$ gives the subtraction formula:
   $$
   \cosh(x - y) = \cosh x \cosh y - \sinh x \sinh y
   $$
   *(Notice how in hyperbolic cosine, the sign between the products matches the sign inside the argument—$\cosh(x \pm y) = \cosh x \cosh y \pm \sinh x \sinh y$—in contrast to circular cosine $\cos(x \pm y) = \cos x \cos y \mp \sin x \sin y$, where the sign flips, exactly as predicted by Osborn's Rule!)*
3. Finally, for the hyperbolic tangent addition and subtraction formula, we take the ratio of $\sinh(x \pm y)$ to $\cosh(x \pm y)$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \tanh(x \pm y) &= \frac{\sinh(x \pm y)}{\cosh(x \pm y)} \\
   &= \frac{\sinh x \cosh y \pm \cosh x \sinh y}{\cosh x \cosh y \pm \sinh x \sinh y}
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Dividing both the numerator and denominator by $\cosh x \cosh y$ produces:
   $$
   \tanh(x \pm y) = \frac{\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x} \pm \frac{\sinh y}{\cosh y}}{1 \pm \left(\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x}\right)\left(\frac{\sinh y}{\cosh y}\right)} = \frac{\tanh x \pm \tanh y}{1 \pm \tanh x \tanh y} \quad \blacksquare
   $$

#### Proof of Identity 4: Double-Argument Formulas

**Proof:** Setting $y = x$ in the addition formulas from Identity 3 yields all three double-argument formulas:
1. For $\sinh(2x)$:
   $$
   \sinh(2x) = \sinh(x + x) = \sinh x \cosh x + \cosh x \sinh x = 2 \sinh x \cosh x
   $$
2. For $\cosh(2x)$:
   $$
   \cosh(2x) = \cosh(x + x) = \cosh^2 x + \sinh^2 x
   $$
   We can express $\cosh(2x)$ purely in terms of $\cosh x$ or purely in terms of $\sinh x$ using $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$:
   - Substituting $\sinh^2 x = \cosh^2 x - 1$ gives
     $$
     \cosh 2x = \cosh^2 x + (\cosh^2 x - 1) = 2\cosh^2 x - 1
     $$
   - Substituting $\cosh^2 x = 1 + \sinh^2 x$ gives
     $$
     \cosh 2x = (1 + \sinh^2 x) + \sinh^2 x = 1 + 2\sinh^2 x
     $$
3. Setting $y = x$ in the tangent addition formula gives:
   $$
   \tanh(2x) = \frac{\tanh x + \tanh x}{1 + \tanh x \tanh x} = \frac{2\tanh x}{1 + \tanh^2 x} \quad \blacksquare
   $$

#### Proof of Identity 5: Power-Reduction Formulas

**Proof:** We solve the two alternative forms of the double-argument formula for $\cosh 2x$ directly for $\cosh^2 x$ and $\sinh^2 x$:
1. From $\cosh 2x = 2\cosh^2 x - 1$:
   $$
   2\cosh^2 x = \cosh 2x + 1 \implies \cosh^2 x = \frac{\cosh 2x + 1}{2}
   $$
2. From $\cosh 2x = 1 + 2\sinh^2 x$:
   $$
   2\sinh^2 x = \cosh 2x - 1 \implies \sinh^2 x = \frac{\cosh 2x - 1}{2} \quad \blacksquare
   $$

## Geometric Profiles, Parity, and Asymptotic Behaviors

Unlike circular trigonometric functions, which oscillate periodically forever, hyperbolic functions are non-periodic (for real arguments) and exhibit exponential growth or asymptotic stabilization as $x \to \pm\infty$. Each of the six hyperbolic functions has a distinct symmetry and asymptotic profile governed by its constituent exponential terms $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$.

> [!summary] Summary of Domain, Range, and Parity
> | Function | Domain | Range | Parity | Zeroes | Asymptotes |
> | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
> | $y = \sinh x$ | $(-\infty, \infty)$ | $(-\infty, \infty)$ | Odd | $x = 0$ | None |
> | $y = \cosh x$ | $(-\infty, \infty)$ | $[1, \infty)$ | Even | None | Asymptotic to $\frac{1}{2}e^{\|x\|}$ as $\|x\| \to \infty$ |
> | $y = \tanh x$ | $(-\infty, \infty)$ | $(-1, 1)$ | Odd | $x = 0$ | Horizontal: $y = 1$ ($x \to \infty$), $y = -1$ ($x \to -\infty$) |
> | $y = \coth x$ | $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}$ | $(-\infty, -1) \cup (1, \infty)$ | Odd | None | Horizontal: $y = \pm 1$; Vertical: $x = 0$ |
> | $y = \operatorname{sech} x$ | $(-\infty, \infty)$ | $(0, 1]$ | Even | None | Horizontal: $y = 0$ ($x \to \pm \infty$) |
> | $y = \operatorname{csch} x$ | $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}$ | $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}$ | Odd | None | Horizontal: $y = 0$; Vertical: $x = 0$ |

### Analytical Properties and Limiting Behavior

Let us examine the graph and analytical behavior of each of the six functions in detail:

1. **Hyperbolic Sine ($y = \sinh x$):**
   - **Parity:** Odd function ($\sinh(-x) = -\sinh x$), reflecting rotational symmetry of $180^\circ$ through the origin.
   - **Sign:** For $x > 0$, $e^x > e^{-x}$ so $\sinh x > 0$; for $x < 0$, $e^x < e^{-x}$ so $\sinh x < 0$; and $\sinh(0) = 0$.
   - **Monotonicity:** Strictly increasing across all of $\mathbb{R}$ because its derivative is $\frac{d}{dx}\sinh x = \cosh x \ge 1 > 0$.
   - **Concavity:** Since $\frac{d^2}{dx^2}\sinh x = \sinh x$, the graph is concave down on $(-\infty, 0)$ and concave up on $(0, \infty)$, with an inflection point at the origin $(0,0)$ where the tangent line has slope $\cosh(0) = 1$.
   - **End-Behavior Limits:** As $x \to \infty$, $e^{-x} \to 0$ so $\sinh x \approx \frac{1}{2}e^x \to \infty$; as $x \to -\infty$, $e^x \to 0$ so $\sinh x \approx -\frac{1}{2}e^{-x} \to -\infty$:
     $$
     \lim_{x \to \infty} \sinh x = \infty, \quad \lim_{x \to -\infty} \sinh x = -\infty
     $$

2. **Hyperbolic Cosine ($y = \cosh x$):**
   - **Parity:** Even function ($\cosh(-x) = \cosh x$), symmetric with respect to the $y$-axis.
   - **Global Minimum:** At $x = 0$, $\cosh(0) = \frac{1+1}{2} = 1$. Since $\cosh^2 x = 1 + \sinh^2 x \ge 1$ and $\cosh x > 0$, we have $\cosh x \ge 1$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
   - **Monotonicity:** Strictly decreasing on $(-\infty, 0]$ (where $\sinh x \le 0$) and strictly increasing on $[0, \infty)$ (where $\sinh x \ge 0$).
   - **Concavity:** Since $\frac{d^2}{dx^2}\cosh x = \cosh x \ge 1 > 0$, the catenary curve is strictly concave upward everywhere.
   - **Exponential Dominance:** As $x \to \infty$, $e^{-x} \to 0$, so $\cosh x$ is asymptotic to the growing exponential curve $\frac{1}{2}e^x$. As $x \to -\infty$, $e^x \to 0$, so $\cosh x$ is asymptotic to the decaying exponential curve $\frac{1}{2}e^{-x}$.

3. **Hyperbolic Tangent ($y = \tanh x$):**
   - **Parity:** Odd function ($\tanh(-x) = -\tanh x$), passing through $(0,0)$.
   - **Monotonicity:** Strictly increasing across $\mathbb{R}$ because $\frac{d}{dx}\tanh x = \operatorname{sech}^2 x > 0$.
   - **Asymptotic Limits:** To find the horizontal asymptotes as $x \to \pm\infty$, we factor out the dominant exponential term from numerator and denominator:
     $$
     \lim_{x \to \infty} \tanh x = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 - e^{-2x}}{1 + e^{-2x}} = \frac{1 - 0}{1 + 0} = 1
     $$
     $$
     \lim_{x \to -\infty} \tanh x = \lim_{x \to -\infty} \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{e^x + e^{-x}} = \lim_{x \to -\infty} \frac{e^{2x} - 1}{e^{2x} + 1} = \frac{0 - 1}{0 + 1} = -1
     $$
   - **Boundedness:** Because $\tanh x$ is strictly increasing between its horizontal asymptotes $y = -1$ and $y = 1$, its output values are strictly bounded: $-1 < \tanh x < 1$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$. (This smooth S-shaped "sigmoid" profile makes $\tanh x$ a famous activation function in neural networks and statistical mechanics!)

4. **Hyperbolic Cotangent ($y = \coth x$):**
   - **Domain & Parity:** Odd function, reciprocal of $\tanh x$, undefined at $x = 0$.
   - **Vertical Asymptote:** One-sided infinite limits at $x = 0$:
     $$
     \lim_{x \to 0^+} \coth x = +\infty, \quad \lim_{x \to 0^-} \coth x = -\infty
     $$
   - **Horizontal Asymptotes:** $\lim_{x \to \infty} \coth x = 1$ and $\lim_{x \to -\infty} \coth x = -1$.
   - **Range:** $(-\infty, -1) \cup (1, \infty)$; values in the closed interval $[-1, 1]$ are never attained.

5. **Hyperbolic Secant ($y = \operatorname{sech} x$):**
   - **Range & Parity:** Reciprocal of $\cosh x$; an even, bell-shaped function that is strictly positive everywhere ($0 < \operatorname{sech} x \le 1$) and achieves its absolute maximum of $1$ at $x = 0$.
   - **Horizontal Asymptote:** Asymptotic to the $x$-axis in both directions: $\lim_{x \to \pm \infty} \operatorname{sech} x = 0$.

6. **Hyperbolic Cosecant ($y = \operatorname{csch} x$):**
   - **Domain & Parity:** Reciprocal of $\sinh x$, odd function undefined at $x = 0$.
   - **Vertical Asymptote:** At $x = 0$,
     $$
     \lim_{x \to 0^+} \operatorname{csch} x = +\infty, \quad \lim_{x \to 0^-} \operatorname{csch} x = -\infty
     $$
   - **Horizontal Asymptote:** Asymptotic to the $x$-axis in both directions: $\lim_{x \to \pm \infty} \operatorname{csch} x = 0$.

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916818389.webp]]

## Differential and Integral Calculus of Hyperbolic Functions

Because the building blocks $e^x$ and $e^{-x}$ are infinitely differentiable everywhere, all six hyperbolic functions are smooth (infinitely differentiable) on their respective domains. Even better, their derivatives and integrals are remarkably simple to compute and remember.

### Derivatives of Hyperbolic Functions

> [!summary] Table 6.9: Derivatives of Hyperbolic Functions
> Let $u = u(x)$ be a differentiable function of $x$. The derivatives of the six hyperbolic functions are:
> 
> 1. **Hyperbolic Sine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh u) = \cosh u \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 2. **Hyperbolic Cosine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh u) = \sinh u \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 3. **Hyperbolic Tangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\tanh u) = \operatorname{sech}^2 u \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 4. **Hyperbolic Cotangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\coth u) = -\operatorname{csch}^2 u \frac{du}{dx}, \quad u \neq 0
>    $$
> 5. **Hyperbolic Secant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{sech} u) = -\operatorname{sech} u \tanh u \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 6. **Hyperbolic Cosecant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch} u) = -\operatorname{csch} u \coth u \frac{du}{dx}, \quad u \neq 0
>    $$

> [!warning] Notational and Sign Caution
> Pay special attention to the signs in Table 6.9, especially the derivative of $\cosh u$:
> $$
> \frac{d}{dx}(\cos u) = -\sin u \frac{du}{dx}, \quad \text{BUT} \quad \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh u) = +\sinh u \frac{du}{dx}
> $$
> Unlike circular trigonometry, differentiating $\cosh u$ produces a **positive** $\sinh u$! In fact, $\sinh x$ and $\cosh x$ are simply each other's derivatives. In hyperbolic calculus, the three primary functions ($\sinh u$, $\cosh u$, $\tanh u$) all have **positive** derivatives, whereas the three reciprocal/co-functions ($\coth u$, $\operatorname{sech} u$, $\operatorname{csch} u$) all have **negative** derivatives.

#### Proofs of the Hyperbolic Derivative Formulas

**Proof:** Let us prove each formula for $u = x$; multiplying by $\frac{du}{dx}$ via the Chain Rule immediately gives the general form in Table 6.9.

1. **Derivative of $\sinh x$:**
   Differentiating the exponential definition of $\sinh x$ term by term gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh x) &= \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2}\right) \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) - \frac{d}{dx}(e^{-x})\right] \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[e^x - (-e^{-x})\right] \\
   &= \frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2} = \cosh x
   \end{aligned}
   $$

2. **Derivative of $\cosh x$:**
   Similarly, differentiating the exponential definition of $\cosh x$ gives:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh x) &= \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{e^x + e^{-x}}{2}\right) \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{d}{dx}(e^x) + \frac{d}{dx}(e^{-x})\right] \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[e^x + (-e^{-x})\right] \\
   &= \frac{e^x - e^{-x}}{2} = \sinh x
   \end{aligned}
   $$

3. **Derivative of $\tanh x$:**
   Applying the Quotient Rule to $\tanh x = \frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x}$ and using the derivatives of $\sinh x$ and $\cosh x$ just proved:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\tanh x) &= \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x}\right) \\
   &= \frac{\cosh x \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh x) - \sinh x \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh x)}{\cosh^2 x} \\
   &= \frac{\cosh x (\cosh x) - \sinh x (\sinh x)}{\cosh^2 x} \\
   &= \frac{\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x}{\cosh^2 x}
   \end{aligned}
   $$
   Applying the fundamental identity $\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x = 1$ in the numerator yields:
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\tanh x) = \frac{1}{\cosh^2 x} = \operatorname{sech}^2 x
   $$

4. **Derivative of $\coth x$:**
   Applying the Quotient Rule to $\coth x = \frac{\cosh x}{\sinh x}$ for $x \neq 0$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\coth x) &= \frac{\sinh x \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh x) - \cosh x \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh x)}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   &= \frac{\sinh x (\sinh x) - \cosh x (\cosh x)}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   &= \frac{\sinh^2 x - \cosh^2 x}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   &= \frac{-(\cosh^2 x - \sinh^2 x)}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   &= -\frac{1}{\sinh^2 x} = -\operatorname{csch}^2 x
   \end{aligned}
   $$

5. **Derivative of $\operatorname{sech} x$:**
   Using the Chain/Reciprocal Rule on $\operatorname{sech} x = (\cosh x)^{-1}$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{sech} x) &= -(\cosh x)^{-2} \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh x) \\
   &= -\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh^2 x} \\
   &= -\left(\frac{1}{\cosh x}\right)\left(\frac{\sinh x}{\cosh x}\right) \\
   &= -\operatorname{sech} x \tanh x
   \end{aligned}
   $$

6. **Derivative of $\operatorname{csch} x$:**
   Using the Chain/Reciprocal Rule on $\operatorname{csch} x = (\sinh x)^{-1}$ for $x \neq 0$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch} x) &= -(\sinh x)^{-2} \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh x) \\
   &= -\frac{\cosh x}{\sinh^2 x} \\
   &= -\left(\frac{1}{\sinh x}\right)\left(\frac{\cosh x}{\sinh x}\right) \\
   &= -\operatorname{csch} x \coth x \quad \blacksquare
   \end{aligned}
   $$

### Integral Formulas for Hyperbolic Functions

By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, reversing each differentiation rule in Table 6.9 yields a companion indefinite integral formula.

> [!summary] Table 6.10: Integral Formulas for Hyperbolic Functions
> 1. $$
>    \int \sinh u \, du = \cosh u + C
>    $$
> 2. $$
>    \int \cosh u \, du = \sinh u + C
>    $$
> 3. $$
>    \int \operatorname{sech}^2 u \, du = \tanh u + C
>    $$
> 4. $$
>    \int \operatorname{csch}^2 u \, du = -\coth u + C
>    $$
> 5. $$
>    \int \operatorname{sech} u \tanh u \, du = -\operatorname{sech} u + C
>    $$
> 6. $$
>    \int \operatorname{csch} u \coth u \, du = -\operatorname{csch} u + C
>    $$

#### Integrals of $\tanh u$ and $\coth u$ via Logarithmic Substitution

What if we need to integrate $\tanh u$ or $\coth u$ itself? Just as we integrate $\tan u = \frac{\sin u}{\cos u}$ by substituting $w = \cos u$, we can integrate $\tanh u$ and $\coth u$ by writing them as ratios of $\sinh u$ and $\cosh u$:

1. **Antiderivative of $\tanh u$:**
   Writing $\tanh u = \frac{\sinh u}{\cosh u}$, we have
   $$
   \int \tanh u \, du = \int \frac{\sinh u}{\cosh u} \, du
   $$
   Let $w = \cosh u$, so that $dw = \sinh u \, du$. Because $\cosh u \ge 1 > 0$ for all real $u$, we can drop the absolute value bars on $|w| = w$:
   $$
   \int \frac{dw}{w} = \ln|w| + C = \ln(\cosh u) + C
   $$
2. **Antiderivative of $\coth u$:**
   Writing $\coth u = \frac{\cosh u}{\sinh u}$ for $u \neq 0$, we have
   $$
   \int \coth u \, du = \int \frac{\cosh u}{\sinh u} \, du
   $$
   Let $w = \sinh u$, so that $dw = \cosh u \, du$. Here $\sinh u$ can be positive or negative, so we retain the absolute value bars:
   $$
   \int \frac{dw}{w} = \ln|w| + C = \ln|\sinh u| + C
   $$

## Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

Because hyperbolic functions arise naturally in physical systems and integration problems, we frequently need to invert them. Even more importantly for integral calculus, the derivatives of inverse hyperbolic functions turn out to be simple algebraic fractions involving square roots like $\sqrt{1 + x^2}$ and $\sqrt{x^2 - 1}$, giving us a powerful new way to evaluate integrals of algebraic functions.

### Invertibility, Monotonicity, and Domain Restrictions

Recall from Section 6.1 that a function possesses a well-defined, single-valued inverse if and only if it is one-to-one (injective) on its domain. Let us check which of the six hyperbolic functions are already one-to-one, and which ones require a domain restriction (a principal branch):

1. **Hyperbolic Sine ($\sinh x$):**
   Because its derivative is strictly positive everywhere,
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh x) = \cosh x \ge 1 > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
   $$
   $\sinh x$ is strictly increasing on $(-\infty, \infty)$. Therefore, it is globally one-to-one without any domain restriction, and its inverse is defined for all real numbers:
   $$
   y = \sinh^{-1} x \iff x = \sinh y, \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\sinh^{-1}) = (-\infty, \infty), \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\sinh^{-1}) = (-\infty, \infty)
   $$

2. **Hyperbolic Cosine ($\cosh x$):**
   Because $\cosh x$ is an even function ($\cosh(-x) = \cosh x$), every value $x > 1$ in its range is hit twice (once for $y > 0$ and once for $y < 0$), failing the horizontal line test across $\mathbb{R}$. However, for $x > 0$, its derivative satisfies
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh x) = \sinh x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x > 0
   $$
   Restricting the domain of $\cosh$ to the non-negative real numbers $[0, \infty)$ makes it strictly increasing from $1$ to $\infty$. This restricted **principal branch** defines the inverse hyperbolic cosine:
   $$
   y = \cosh^{-1} x \iff x = \cosh y \quad (y \ge 0), \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\cosh^{-1}) = [1, \infty), \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\cosh^{-1}) = [0, \infty)
   $$

3. **Hyperbolic Tangent ($\tanh x$):**
   Since
   $$
   \frac{d}{dx}(\tanh x) = \operatorname{sech}^2 x > 0 \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
   $$
   $\tanh x$ is strictly increasing throughout $(-\infty, \infty)$ with range $(-1, 1)$. Thus it is globally one-to-one, and its inverse is:
   $$
   y = \tanh^{-1} x \iff x = \tanh y, \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\tanh^{-1}) = (-1, 1), \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\tanh^{-1}) = (-\infty, \infty)
   $$

4. **Hyperbolic Cotangent ($\coth x$):**
   For $x \neq 0$, $\frac{d}{dx}(\coth x) = -\operatorname{csch}^2 x < 0$. Moreover, $\coth x < -1$ on $(-\infty, 0)$ and $\coth x > 1$ on $(0, \infty)$, so the outputs on the two branches never overlap. Thus $\coth x$ is globally one-to-one on $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}$ with range $(-\infty, -1) \cup (1, \infty)$, and its inverse is:
   $$
   y = \coth^{-1} x \iff x = \coth y, \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\coth^{-1}) = |x| > 1, \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\coth^{-1}) = \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}
   $$

5. **Hyperbolic Secant ($\operatorname{sech} x$):**
   Like $\cosh x$, the bell-shaped function $\operatorname{sech} x$ is even and therefore requires restriction to the non-negative principal domain $[0, \infty)$, where it is strictly decreasing from $1$ down toward $0$. Its inverse is:
   $$
   y = \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x \iff x = \operatorname{sech} y \quad (y \ge 0), \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\operatorname{sech}^{-1}) = (0, 1], \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\operatorname{sech}^{-1}) = [0, \infty)
   $$

6. **Hyperbolic Cosecant ($\operatorname{csch} x$):**
   Since $\operatorname{csch} x < 0$ for $x < 0$, $\operatorname{csch} x > 0$ for $x > 0$, and $\frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch} x) = -\operatorname{csch} x \coth x < 0$ on each branch, $\operatorname{csch} x$ is globally one-to-one on $\mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}$:
   $$
   y = \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x \iff x = \operatorname{csch} y, \quad \operatorname{Dom}(\operatorname{csch}^{-1}) = \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}, \quad \operatorname{Ran}(\operatorname{csch}^{-1}) = \mathbb{R} \setminus \{0\}
   $$

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916860591.webp]]
![[06. Transcendental Functions-1790916883785.webp]]

### Reciprocal Identities for Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

Because $\operatorname{sech} y$, $\operatorname{csch} y$, and $\coth y$ are the reciprocals of $\cosh y$, $\sinh y$, and $\tanh y$, their inverse functions satisfy three clean reciprocal identities.

> [!summary] Table 6.11: Identities for Inverse Hyperbolic Functions
> 1. $$
>    \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x = \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right), \quad 0 < x \le 1
>    $$
> 2. $$
>    \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x = \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right), \quad x \neq 0
>    $$
> 3. $$
>    \coth^{-1} x = \tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right), \quad |x| > 1
>    $$

**Proof:** Each identity follows directly from the definition of the inverse functions and their range restrictions:
1. **Proof of $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x = \cosh^{-1}(1/x)$:**
   Let $y = \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$ for $0 < x \le 1$. By definition of the principal branch of $\operatorname{sech}^{-1}$, this is equivalent to
   $$
   x = \operatorname{sech} y \quad \text{with } y \ge 0
   $$
   Taking the reciprocal of both sides gives
   $$
   \frac{1}{x} = \frac{1}{\operatorname{sech} y} = \cosh y \quad \text{with } y \ge 0
   $$
   Since $0 < x \le 1$, we have $\frac{1}{x} \ge 1$. Because $y \ge 0$ lies in the principal range of $\cosh^{-1}$, applying the definition of $\cosh^{-1}$ to $\cosh y = \frac{1}{x}$ yields
   $$
   y = \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
   $$
   Therefore, $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x = \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$.

2. **Proof of $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x = \sinh^{-1}(1/x)$:**
   Let $y = \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$ for $x \neq 0$. Since neither $\operatorname{csch}^{-1}$ nor $\sinh^{-1}$ has a branch restriction, we have the direct equivalence:
   $$
   x = \operatorname{csch} y \iff \frac{1}{x} = \sinh y \iff y = \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
   $$

3. **Proof of $\coth^{-1} x = \tanh^{-1}(1/x)$:**
   Let $y = \coth^{-1} x$ for $|x| > 1$. Taking reciprocals maps $|x| > 1$ to $0 < |1/x| < 1$, which lies inside the domain $(-1, 1)$ of $\tanh^{-1}$:
   $$
   x = \coth y \iff \frac{1}{x} = \tanh y \iff y = \tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \quad \blacksquare
   $$

### Explicit Logarithmic Representations of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

Here is one of the most striking facts about inverse hyperbolic functions: because the hyperbolic functions are defined in terms of $e^y$ and $e^{-y}$, the inverse equation $x = f(y)$ always reduces to a quadratic equation in the variable $w = e^y$. Solving that quadratic equation for $e^y$ and taking the natural logarithm means that **every inverse hyperbolic function can be written explicitly in closed form using natural logarithms and algebraic square roots!**

> [!theorem] Master Theorem: Logarithmic Expressions of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions
