> The six inverse hyperbolic functions have the following exact logarithmic representations on their domains:
> 
> 1. **Inverse Hyperbolic Sine:**
>    $$
>    \sinh^{-1} x = \ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}\right), \quad -\infty < x < \infty
>    $$
> 2. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cosine:**
>    $$
>    \cosh^{-1} x = \ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}\right), \quad x \ge 1
>    $$
> 3. **Inverse Hyperbolic Tangent:**
>    $$
>    \tanh^{-1} x = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{1 + x}{1 - x}\right), \quad |x| < 1
>    $$
> 4. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cotangent:**
>    $$
>    \coth^{-1} x = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{x + 1}{x - 1}\right), \quad |x| > 1
>    $$
> 5. **Inverse Hyperbolic Secant:**
>    $$
>    \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x = \ln\left(\frac{1 + \sqrt{1 - x^2}}{x}\right), \quad 0 < x \le 1
>    $$
> 6. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cosecant:**
>    $$
>    \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x = \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \frac{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}{|x|}\right), \quad x \neq 0
>    $$

Let us prove each of these six logarithmic formulas carefully, paying close attention to why one root of the quadratic equation is kept and the other is rejected.

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\sinh^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Let $y = \sinh^{-1} x$ for any $x \in \mathbb{R}$. By definition,
$$
x = \sinh y = \frac{e^y - e^{-y}}{2}
$$
Multiplying both sides by $2$ gives $2x = e^y - e^{-y}$. Now, to clear the negative exponent $e^{-y}$, we multiply both sides by $e^y$ (which is valid since $e^y > 0$ for all real $y$) and rearrange into standard quadratic form:
$$
2x e^y = (e^y)^2 - 1 \iff (e^y)^2 - 2x(e^y) - 1 = 0
$$
Viewing this as a quadratic equation $w^2 - 2xw - 1 = 0$ in the unknown $w = e^y$, we apply the quadratic formula:
$$
e^y = w = \frac{-(-2x) \pm \sqrt{(-2x)^2 - 4(1)(-1)}}{2(1)} = \frac{2x \pm \sqrt{4x^2 + 4}}{2} = x \pm \sqrt{x^2 + 1}
$$
Which sign should we choose? Notice that because $x^2 + 1 > x^2$, taking square roots gives $\sqrt{x^2 + 1} > \sqrt{x^2} = |x| \ge x$. Consequently,
$$
x - \sqrt{x^2 + 1} < 0
$$
However, $e^y$ is strictly positive for every real number $y$! Thus, the minus root $x - \sqrt{x^2 + 1}$ is negative (extraneous) and must be rejected, leaving the unique positive root:
$$
e^y = x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides yields
$$
y = \sinh^{-1} x = \ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}\right) \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\cosh^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Let $y = \cosh^{-1} x$ for $x \ge 1$, which by definition means $x = \cosh y$ together with the principal-branch restriction $y \ge 0$:
$$
x = \frac{e^y + e^{-y}}{2} \implies 2x = e^y + e^{-y}
$$
Multiplying through by $e^y$ and rearranging gives a quadratic equation in $w = e^y$:
$$
(e^y)^2 - 2x(e^y) + 1 = 0
$$
Applying the quadratic formula to solve for $e^y$ gives:
$$
e^y = \frac{2x \pm \sqrt{4x^2 - 4}}{2} = x \pm \sqrt{x^2 - 1}
$$
Unlike the $\sinh^{-1} x$ case, here **both** roots $x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}$ and $x - \sqrt{x^2 - 1}$ are positive! Indeed, multiplying them together shows that they are reciprocals of one another:
$$
\left(x - \sqrt{x^2 - 1}\right)\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}\right) = x^2 - (x^2 - 1) = 1 \implies x - \sqrt{x^2 - 1} = \frac{1}{x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}}
$$
Why, then, do we choose the plus sign? Recall that the principal branch of $\cosh^{-1} x$ requires $y \ge 0$, which forces $e^y \ge e^0 = 1$. For $x > 1$, we have $x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1} > 1$, whereas its reciprocal $x - \sqrt{x^2 - 1} < 1$ corresponds to the rejected negative branch $y \le 0$. Thus, to ensure $y \ge 0$ ($e^y \ge 1$), we must select the plus sign:
$$
e^y = x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides gives:
$$
\cosh^{-1} x = \ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 - 1}\right), \quad x \ge 1 \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\tanh^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Let $y = \tanh^{-1} x$ for $|x| < 1$. By definition,
$$
x = \tanh y = \frac{e^y - e^{-y}}{e^y + e^{-y}}
$$
Multiplying the numerator and denominator on the right-hand side by $e^y$ eliminates the negative exponents:
$$
x = \frac{e^{2y} - 1}{e^{2y} + 1}
$$
Now we clear the denominator and solve algebraically for $e^{2y}$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
x(e^{2y} + 1) &= e^{2y} - 1 \\
x e^{2y} + x &= e^{2y} - 1 \\
1 + x &= e^{2y} - x e^{2y} \\
1 + x &= e^{2y}(1 - x)
\end{aligned}
$$
Because $|x| < 1$, both $1 + x > 0$ and $1 - x > 0$. Dividing by $1 - x$ gives:
$$
e^{2y} = \frac{1 + x}{1 - x}
$$
Taking the natural logarithm of both sides and dividing by $2$ completes the proof:
$$
2y = \ln\left(\frac{1 + x}{1 - x}\right) \implies y = \tanh^{-1} x = \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{1 + x}{1 - x}\right) \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\coth^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Rather than solving from scratch, we can combine the reciprocal identity $\coth^{-1} x = \tanh^{-1}(1/x)$ from Table 6.11 with the logarithmic formula for $\tanh^{-1}$ just proved. For $|x| > 1$, we have $|1/x| < 1$, so:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\coth^{-1} x &= \tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{1 + 1/x}{1 - 1/x}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{\frac{x+1}{x}}{\frac{x-1}{x}}\right) \\
&= \frac{1}{2} \ln\left(\frac{x + 1}{x - 1}\right) \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Using the reciprocal identity $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x = \cosh^{-1}(1/x)$ from Table 6.11 together with the logarithmic formula for $\cosh^{-1}$, we note that for $0 < x \le 1$, we have $\frac{1}{x} \ge 1$ and $\sqrt{x^2} = x$. Thus:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x &= \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)^2 - 1}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \sqrt{\frac{1 - x^2}{x^2}}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \frac{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}{x}\right) \quad (\text{since } x > 0) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1 + \sqrt{1 - x^2}}{x}\right) \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

#### Proof of the Logarithmic Formula for $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$

**Proof:** Using the reciprocal identity $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x = \sinh^{-1}(1/x)$ from Table 6.11 and the logarithmic formula for $\sinh^{-1}$, we note that for any $x \neq 0$ (which may be positive or negative!), $\sqrt{x^2} = |x|$. Therefore:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x &= \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)^2 + 1}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \sqrt{\frac{1 + x^2}{x^2}}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{1}{x} + \frac{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}{|x|}\right) \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

## Differential Calculus of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

How do we differentiate the six inverse hyperbolic functions? We have two equally rewarding paths available: we can use **implicit differentiation** on the defining equation $x = f(y)$ combined with the Pythagorean hyperbolic identities, or we can **differentiate the explicit logarithmic formulas** directly! Let us summarize all six derivative rules in Table 6.12 and then prove them using both methods.

> [!summary] Table 6.12: Derivatives of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions
> Let $u = u(x)$ be a differentiable function of $x$. The derivatives of the six inverse hyperbolic functions are:
> 
> 1. **Inverse Hyperbolic Sine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\sinh^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + u^2}} \frac{du}{dx}
>    $$
> 2. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cosine:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\cosh^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{u^2 - 1}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad u > 1
>    $$
> 3. **Inverse Hyperbolic Tangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\tanh^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{1 - u^2} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| < 1
>    $$
> 4. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cotangent:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\coth^{-1} u) = \frac{1}{1 - u^2} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad |u| > 1
>    $$
> 5. **Inverse Hyperbolic Secant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{sech}^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{u\sqrt{1 - u^2}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad 0 < u < 1
>    $$
> 6. **Inverse Hyperbolic Cosecant:**
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch}^{-1} u) = -\frac{1}{|u|\sqrt{1 + u^2}} \frac{du}{dx}, \quad u \neq 0
>    $$

### Step-by-Step Proofs via Implicit Differentiation

Let us prove each formula for $u = x$; the Chain Rule then supplies the factor $\frac{du}{dx}$.

#### Proof 1: Derivative of $y = \sinh^{-1} x$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \sinh^{-1} x \iff \sinh y = x$. Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ via the Chain Rule gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\sinh y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\cosh y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\cosh y}
\end{aligned}
$$
From the fundamental identity $\cosh^2 y - \sinh^2 y = 1$, we have $\cosh y = \pm \sqrt{1 + \sinh^2 y}$. Since $\cosh y > 0$ for all real $y$, we must choose the positive square root:
$$
\cosh y = \sqrt{1 + \sinh^2 y} = \sqrt{1 + x^2}
$$
Substituting this back into $\frac{dy}{dx}$ gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\sinh^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}} \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof 2: Derivative of $y = \cosh^{-1} x$ for $x > 1$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \cosh^{-1} x \iff \cosh y = x$ with $y > 0$ (for $x > 1$; note that at the endpoint $x = 1$, $y = 0$ and the graph has a vertical tangent). Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\cosh y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\sinh y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sinh y}
\end{aligned}
$$
From $\cosh^2 y - \sinh^2 y = 1$, we have $\sinh^2 y = \cosh^2 y - 1 = x^2 - 1$. Because $y > 0$ on the principal branch, $\sinh y$ is strictly positive, so we select the positive root:
$$
\sinh y = \sqrt{x^2 - 1}
$$
Therefore,
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\cosh^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x^2 - 1}}, \quad x > 1 \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof 3: Derivative of $y = \tanh^{-1} x$ for $|x| < 1$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \tanh^{-1} x \iff \tanh y = x$. Differentiating both sides with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\tanh y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
\operatorname{sech}^2 y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\operatorname{sech}^2 y}
\end{aligned}
$$
Using the derived Pythagorean identity $\operatorname{sech}^2 y = 1 - \tanh^2 y = 1 - x^2$, we obtain:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\tanh^{-1} x) = \frac{1}{1 - x^2}, \quad |x| < 1 \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof 4: Derivative of $y = \coth^{-1} x$ for $|x| > 1$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \coth^{-1} x \iff \coth y = x$. Differentiating implicitly with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\coth y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
-\operatorname{csch}^2 y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\operatorname{csch}^2 y}
\end{aligned}
$$
Using the derived Pythagorean identity $\operatorname{csch}^2 y = \coth^2 y - 1 = x^2 - 1$, we find:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\coth^{-1} x) = -\frac{1}{x^2 - 1} = \frac{1}{1 - x^2}, \quad |x| > 1 \quad \blacksquare
$$

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on $\tanh^{-1}$ vs. $\coth^{-1}$ Derivatives
> Look closely at Formulas 3 and 4 in Table 6.12: $\frac{d}{dx}(\tanh^{-1} x)$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(\coth^{-1} x)$ produce the **exact same algebraic expression** $\frac{1}{1 - x^2}$!
> How is that possible when $\tanh^{-1} x$ and $\coth^{-1} x$ are different functions? The key is that their domains are **mutually exclusive** (disjoint):
> - On the interior interval $|x| < 1$, $\frac{1}{1 - x^2} > 0$ is the derivative of $\tanh^{-1} x$ (and $\coth^{-1} x$ is not even defined there).
> - On the exterior intervals $|x| > 1$, $\frac{1}{1 - x^2} < 0$ is the derivative of $\coth^{-1} x$ (and $\tanh^{-1} x$ is not defined there).
> Consequently, when integrating $\frac{1}{1 - x^2}$, the domain of $x$ dictates whether the antiderivative is $\tanh^{-1} x$ (when $|x| < 1$) or $\coth^{-1} x$ (when $|x| > 1$).

#### Proof 5: Derivative of $y = \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$ for $0 < x < 1$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \operatorname{sech}^{-1} x \iff \operatorname{sech} y = x$ with $y > 0$ (for $0 < x < 1$). Differentiating implicitly with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{sech} y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
-\operatorname{sech} y \tanh y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\operatorname{sech} y \tanh y}
\end{aligned}
$$
We already know $\operatorname{sech} y = x$. Moreover, because $y > 0$ on the principal branch, $\tanh y > 0$, so the identity $\tanh^2 y = 1 - \operatorname{sech}^2 y$ yields the positive square root:
$$
\tanh y = \sqrt{1 - \operatorname{sech}^2 y} = \sqrt{1 - x^2}
$$
Substituting these back into the derivative formula gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x) = -\frac{1}{x \sqrt{1 - x^2}}, \quad 0 < x < 1 \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Proof 6: Derivative of $y = \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$ for $x \neq 0$

**Proof:** By definition, $y = \operatorname{csch}^{-1} x \iff \operatorname{csch} y = x$. Differentiating implicitly with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch} y) &= \frac{d}{dx}(x) \\
-\operatorname{csch} y \coth y \frac{dy}{dx} &= 1 \implies \frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{1}{\operatorname{csch} y \coth y}
\end{aligned}
$$
Here $\operatorname{csch} y = x$. From the identity $\coth^2 y = 1 + \operatorname{csch}^2 y = 1 + x^2$, we have $|\coth y| = \sqrt{1 + x^2}$. Now let us carefully determine the sign of the product $\operatorname{csch} y \coth y$:
Notice that $\operatorname{csch} y = \frac{1}{\sinh y}$ and $\coth y = \frac{\cosh y}{\sinh y}$. Because $\cosh y > 0$ for all real $y$, both $\operatorname{csch} y$ and $\coth y$ have the exact same sign as $\sinh y$ (positive when $x > 0$, negative when $x < 0$). Therefore, their product $\operatorname{csch} y \coth y$ is **always positive**, regardless of whether $x > 0$ or $x < 0$! That means we must write $x \cdot \operatorname{sgn}(x)\sqrt{1 + x^2} = |x|\sqrt{1 + x^2}$:
$$
\operatorname{csch} y \coth y = |x|\sqrt{1 + x^2}
$$
Substituting this into the derivative yields:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}(\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x) = -\frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{1 + x^2}}, \quad x \neq 0 \quad \blacksquare
$$

### Alternative Proofs by Differentiating the Logarithmic Forms

To see how nicely our logarithmic formulas from the Master Theorem mesh with Table 6.12, let us verify the derivatives of $\sinh^{-1} x$ and $\tanh^{-1} x$ by differentiating their logarithmic representations directly:

1. **Verification of $\frac{d}{dx}(\sinh^{-1} x)$:**
   Applying the Chain Rule to $\sinh^{-1} x = \ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}\right)$:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}\left[\ln\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}\right)\right] &= \frac{1}{x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}} \frac{d}{dx}\left(x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}\right) \\
   &= \frac{1}{x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}}\left(1 + \frac{2x}{2\sqrt{x^2 + 1}}\right) \\
   &= \frac{1}{x + \sqrt{x^2 + 1}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{x^2 + 1} + x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 1}}\right) \\
   &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{x^2 + 1}} \quad \blacksquare
   \end{aligned}
   $$

2. **Verification of $\frac{d}{dx}(\tanh^{-1} x)$:**
   Using the quotient rule of logarithms to expand $\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1 + x}{1 - x}\right)$ before differentiating:
   $$
   \begin{aligned}
   \frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1 + x}{1 - x}\right)\right] &= \frac{1}{2} \frac{d}{dx}\left[\ln(1 + x) - \ln(1 - x)\right] \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{1 + x} - \frac{-1}{1 - x}\right] \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{(1 - x) + (1 + x)}{(1 + x)(1 - x)}\right] \\
   &= \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{2}{1 - x^2}\right] = \frac{1}{1 - x^2} \quad \blacksquare
   \end{aligned}
   $$

## Integrals Leading to Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

The chief analytical payoff of inverse hyperbolic functions in elementary calculus is their ability to integrate algebraic expressions containing quadratic radicals ($\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}$, $\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}$, $\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}$) and rational quadratic denominators ($a^2 - u^2$). By reversing the differentiation rules of Table 6.12 and scaling by $a > 0$, we obtain Table 6.13.

> [!summary] Table 6.13: Integrals Leading to Inverse Hyperbolic Functions
> Let $a > 0$. The following indefinite integrals evaluate to inverse hyperbolic functions (or their equivalent logarithmic forms):
> 
> 1. $$
>    \int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}} = \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C = \ln\left(u + \sqrt{a^2 + u^2}\right) + C'
>    $$
> 2. $$
>    \int \frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}} = \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C = \ln\left(u + \sqrt{u^2 - a^2}\right) + C', \quad u > a > 0
>    $$
> 3. $$
>    \int \frac{du}{a^2 - u^2} = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{a} \tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C & \text{if } u^2 < a^2 \\ \frac{1}{a} \coth^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C & \text{if } u^2 > a^2 \end{cases} = \frac{1}{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a + u}{a - u}\right| + C
>    $$
> 4. $$
>    \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}} = -\frac{1}{a} \operatorname{sech}^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C, \quad 0 < u < a
>    $$
> 5. $$
>    \int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}} = -\frac{1}{a} \operatorname{csch}^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right| + C, \quad u \neq 0
>    $$

### Step-by-Step Derivations of the Integral Formulas

Let us derive each of the five formulas in Table 6.13 by factoring out $a > 0$ and substituting $w = \frac{u}{a}$.

#### Derivation 1: $\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}}$

**Proof:** Factoring $a > 0$ out of the radical in the denominator gives:
$$
\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2\left(1 + (u/a)^2\right)}} = \int \frac{du}{a\sqrt{1 + (u/a)^2}}
$$
Now let $w = \frac{u}{a}$, so that $dw = \frac{1}{a} \, du$, or $du = a \, dw$. The factors of $a$ cancel cleanly:
$$
\int \frac{a \, dw}{a\sqrt{1 + w^2}} = \int \frac{dw}{\sqrt{1 + w^2}} = \sinh^{-1}(w) + C = \sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C
$$
We can also rewrite this antiderivative in logarithmic form using the Master Theorem formula for $\sinh^{-1}$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\sinh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) &= \ln\left(\frac{u}{a} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{u}{a}\right)^2 + 1}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{u + \sqrt{u^2 + a^2}}{a}\right) \\
&= \ln\left(u + \sqrt{u^2 + a^2}\right) - \ln a
\end{aligned}
$$
Absorbing the constant $-\ln a$ into an updated constant of integration $C' = C - \ln a$ yields:
$$
\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}} = \ln\left(u + \sqrt{u^2 + a^2}\right) + C' \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Derivation 2: $\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}$ for $u > a > 0$

**Proof:** Factoring $a > 0$ out of the radical gives:
$$
\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2\left((u/a)^2 - 1\right)}} = \int \frac{du}{a\sqrt{(u/a)^2 - 1}}
$$
Substituting $w = \frac{u}{a}$ and $dw = \frac{1}{a} \, du$ (with $w > 1$ since $u > a > 0$) yields:
$$
\int \frac{dw}{\sqrt{w^2 - 1}} = \cosh^{-1}(w) + C = \cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C
$$
Expressed in logarithmic form by absorbing $-\ln a$ into $C' = C - \ln a$, this is:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\cosh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C &= \ln\left(\frac{u}{a} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{u}{a}\right)^2 - 1}\right) + C \\
&= \ln\left(\frac{u + \sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}{a}\right) + C \\
&= \ln\left(u + \sqrt{u^2 - a^2}\right) + C' \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$

#### Derivation 3: $\int \frac{du}{a^2 - u^2}$ and Unification via Partial Fractions

**Proof:** Factoring $a^2$ from the denominator gives:
$$
\int \frac{du}{a^2\left(1 - (u/a)^2\right)}
$$
Substituting $w = \frac{u}{a}$, so $du = a \, dw$, one factor of $a$ cancels and one remains out front:
$$
\frac{1}{a^2} \int \frac{a \, dw}{1 - w^2} = \frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{1 - w^2}
$$
As we noted in our discussion of Table 6.12, the antiderivative of $\frac{1}{1 - w^2}$ splits into two cases depending on whether $|w| < 1$ or $|w| > 1$:
1. **Case 1 ($|u| < a \iff |w| < 1$):**
   $$
   \frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{1 - w^2} = \frac{1}{a} \tanh^{-1}(w) + C = \frac{1}{a} \tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C
   $$
2. **Case 2 ($|u| > a \iff |w| > 1$):**
   $$
   \frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{1 - w^2} = \frac{1}{a} \coth^{-1}(w) + C = \frac{1}{a} \coth^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C
   $$

Can we unify both cases into a single formula valid for all $u \neq \pm a$? Yes—by decomposing the rational integrand into partial fractions:
$$
\frac{1}{a^2 - u^2} = \frac{1}{(a - u)(a + u)} = \frac{1}{2a}\left[\frac{1}{a + u} + \frac{1}{a - u}\right]
$$
Integrating term by term directly yields:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\int \frac{du}{a^2 - u^2} &= \frac{1}{2a} \int \frac{du}{a + u} + \frac{1}{2a} \int \frac{du}{a - u} \\
&= \frac{1}{2a}\ln|a + u| - \frac{1}{2a}\ln|a - u| + C \\
&= \frac{1}{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a + u}{a - u}\right| + C \quad \blacksquare
\end{aligned}
$$
Notice how the absolute value inside the logarithm automatically handles both cases:
- When $|u| < a$, $\frac{a+u}{a-u} > 0$, so $\frac{1}{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a + u}{a - u}\right| = \frac{1}{2a}\ln\left(\frac{1 + u/a}{1 - u/a}\right) = \frac{1}{a}\tanh^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right)$.
- When $|u| > a$, $\frac{a+u}{a-u} < 0$, so $\frac{1}{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a + u}{a - u}\right| = \frac{1}{2a}\ln\left(\frac{u + a}{u - a}\right) = \frac{1}{2a}\ln\left(\frac{u/a + 1}{u/a - 1}\right) = \frac{1}{a}\coth^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right)$.
Thus, the single logarithmic expression $\frac{1}{2a}\ln\left|\frac{a+u}{a-u}\right| + C$ unifies both the $\tanh^{-1}$ and $\coth^{-1}$ antiderivatives.

#### Derivation 4: $\int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}$ for $0 < u < a$

**Proof:** Factoring $a > 0$ from the radical in the denominator gives:
$$
\int \frac{du}{u \cdot a\sqrt{1 - (u/a)^2}}
$$
Let $w = \frac{u}{a}$, so that $u = aw$ and $du = a \, dw$ (with $0 < w < 1$):
$$
\int \frac{a \, dw}{aw \cdot a \sqrt{1 - w^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{w\sqrt{1 - w^2}}
$$
From Table 6.12, $\frac{d}{dw}\left(\operatorname{sech}^{-1} w\right) = -\frac{1}{w\sqrt{1 - w^2}}$. Therefore:
$$
\frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{w\sqrt{1 - w^2}} = -\frac{1}{a}\operatorname{sech}^{-1}(w) + C = -\frac{1}{a}\operatorname{sech}^{-1}\left(\frac{u}{a}\right) + C \quad \blacksquare
$$

#### Derivation 5: $\int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}}$ for $u \neq 0$

**Proof:** Factoring $a > 0$ from the radical gives:
$$
\int \frac{du}{u \cdot a\sqrt{1 + (u/a)^2}}
$$
Let $w = \frac{u}{a}$, so $u = aw$ and $du = a \, dw$:
$$
\int \frac{a \, dw}{aw \cdot a \sqrt{1 + w^2}} = \frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{w\sqrt{1 + w^2}}
$$
From Table 6.12, $\frac{d}{dw}\left(\operatorname{csch}^{-1}|w|\right) = -\frac{1}{w\sqrt{1 + w^2}}$ for both $w > 0$ and $w < 0$ (since when $w < 0$, $|w| = -w$, and by the Chain Rule $\frac{d}{dw}\operatorname{csch}^{-1}(-w) = -\frac{1}{|-w|\sqrt{1+w^2}}(-1) = \frac{1}{-w\sqrt{1+w^2}} = -\frac{1}{w\sqrt{1+w^2}}$). Therefore:
$$
\frac{1}{a} \int \frac{dw}{w\sqrt{1 + w^2}} = -\frac{1}{a}\operatorname{csch}^{-1}|w| + C = -\frac{1}{a}\operatorname{csch}^{-1}\left|\frac{u}{a}\right| + C \quad \blacksquare
$$

---

# 6.11 First Order Differential Equations

Up to this point in calculus, most equations we have solved for an unknown quantity have been algebraic—where the unknown is a number—or direct integration problems of the form $\frac{dy}{dx} = g(x)$, where the right-hand side depends only on the independent variable $x$. Yet in physics, chemistry, biology, and engineering, the rate at which a quantity $y$ changes often depends not just on time or position $x$, but on the current state $y$ itself (think of radioactive decay, cooling bodies, or compound interest). When an equation relates an unknown function $y(x)$ to its own derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$, we enter the realm of differential equations.

## Mathematical Foundations of First-Order Equations

### Formal Definitions: Equation, Solution, and Initial Value Problem

What exactly makes a differential equation "first-order," and what does it mean to solve one? Let us begin by establishing the formal vocabulary.

> [!abstract] Definition: First-Order Differential Equation
> A **first-order differential equation** is a mathematical relation of the form:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)
> $$
> in which $f(x, y)$ is a real-valued function of two independent variables defined on an open, connected region $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2$ of the $xy$-plane.
> 
> Alternatively, the equation may be expressed in prime notation:
> $$
> y' = f(x, y)
> $$
> or in symmetrical differential form:
> $$
> M(x, y) \, dx + N(x, y) \, dy = 0
> $$

The equation is called *first-order* because it involves only the first derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and no higher-order derivatives such as $\frac{d^2y}{dx^2}$. Unlike an algebraic equation whose solution is a number, a solution to a differential equation is an entire differentiable function defined on an interval.

> [!abstract] Definition: Solution on an Interval
> A **solution** of the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ on an interval $I \subseteq \mathbb{R}$ (which may be finite, semi-infinite, or the entire real line $(-\infty, \infty)$) is a differentiable function $y = \phi(x)$ satisfying:
> 1. $(x, \phi(x)) \in D$ for all $x \in I$.
> 2. The derivative $\frac{d}{dx}\phi(x)$ exists at every point $x \in I$.
> 3. The identity:
>    $$
>    \frac{d}{dx}\phi(x) = f(x, \phi(x))
>    $$
>    holds identically for all $x \in I$.

In plain language, a function $y = \phi(x)$ is a solution on $I$ if its graph stays inside the allowed domain $D$ where $f(x, y)$ makes sense, it is differentiable everywhere on $I$, and plugging $\phi(x)$ and its derivative $\phi'(x)$ into the equation reduces both sides to the exact same function of $x$.

Depending on the algebraic complexity of the equation, we may not always be able to isolate $y$ by itself on the left-hand side. Because of this, solutions are classified based on how the dependent variable is expressed:
- **Explicit Solution:** The dependent variable is solved directly as an isolated formula of the independent variable: $y = \phi(x)$.
- **Implicit Solution:** A relation $F(x, y) = 0$ is an implicit solution on $I$ if it defines one or more explicit solutions $y = \phi(x)$ on $I$ through the Implicit Function Theorem.

Because integrating a first derivative introduces an arbitrary constant of integration $C$, a first-order differential equation typically possesses an infinite family of solutions. In real-world applications, however, we usually want to predict the specific behavior of a system that starts from a known state at a known instant. How do we pin down a single trajectory from an infinite family? We supply a starting point.

> [!abstract] Definition: Initial Value Problem (IVP)
> An **initial value problem (IVP)** consists of a first-order differential equation together with a prescribed point through which the solution curve must pass:
> $$
> \begin{cases}
> \dfrac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y) \\
> y(x_0) = y_0
> \end{cases}
> $$
> where $(x_0, y_0) \in D$. The requirement $y(x_0) = y_0$ is called the **initial condition**.

### Geometric Interpretation: Direction Fields and Integral Curves

Why does an initial condition $y(x_0) = y_0$ single out one specific curve? We can see why immediately by translating the calculus statement $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ into the geometry of the Euclidean plane.

Recall that the derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ represents the slope of the tangent line to the curve $y(x)$. Therefore, at every point $(x, y)$ in the domain $D$, the scalar value $f(x, y)$ prescribes the exact slope that any solution curve passing through that point must have. If we imagine placing a tiny line segment of slope $f(x, y)$ at each point $(x, y) \in D$, we obtain a visual map of tangent directions called a **direction field** (or **slope field**).

From this perspective, a solution $y = \phi(x)$ corresponds geometrically to a smooth curve—called an **integral curve**—that threads its way through the plane so that its tangent line at every point $(x, \phi(x))$ aligns with the local direction field slope $f(x, \phi(x))$.

The general solution of a first-order differential equation represents a one-parameter family of integral curves parameterized by an arbitrary constant of integration $C$. Imposing the initial condition $y(x_0) = y_0$ geometrically isolates the unique integral curve from this family that passes through the specific coordinate point $(x_0, y_0)$.

### The Verification Protocol for Candidate Solutions

Before learning how to find solutions from scratch, how do we verify analytically whether a proposed candidate function $y = \phi(x)$ actually solves a given initial value problem? We follow a strict two-part verification protocol:

1. **Initial Condition Verification:** Evaluate the candidate function $\phi(x)$ at the starting value $x = x_0$ to confirm that the curve passes through the prescribed point:
   $$
   \phi(x_0) = y_0
   $$
2. **Differential Equation Verification:** Check that $\phi(x)$ satisfies the differential equation across the interval $I$ by computing both sides independently:
   - Differentiate $\phi(x)$ with respect to $x$ to compute the left-hand side: $\text{LHS} = \frac{d}{dx}\phi(x)$.
   - Substitute $y = \phi(x)$ into the formula $f(x, y)$ to compute the right-hand side: $\text{RHS} = f(x, \phi(x))$.
   - Demonstrate through algebraic or trigonometric simplification that $\text{LHS} \equiv \text{RHS}$ as an identity for all $x \in I$.

## Separable First-Order Differential Equations

### Theoretical Criteria for Separability

When we confront a general first-order equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$, we cannot simply integrate both sides with respect to $x$ because the right-hand side contains the unknown function $y(x)$ tangled up with $x$. However, what if the right-hand side factors cleanly into a piece involving only $x$ and a piece involving only $y$? That special structure gives rise to the simplest class of nonlinear differential equations we can solve analytically.

> [!abstract] Definition: Separable Equation
> A first-order differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ is **separable** (or has **separable variables**) if the bivariate function $f(x, y)$ can be factored into the product of a function of $x$ alone and a function of $y$ alone:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x) h(y)
> $$
> where $g$ is continuous on an interval $I_x$ and $h$ is continuous on an interval $I_y$.

For instance, $\frac{dy}{dx} = x^2 y^3$ is separable with $g(x) = x^2$ and $h(y) = y^3$, whereas $\frac{dy}{dx} = x + y$ is *not* separable because the sum $x + y$ cannot be factored into a product $g(x)h(y)$.

### Rigorous Mathematical Derivation of the Separation Technique

In practice, we solve a separable equation by moving all $y$ terms (along with $dy$) to the left and all $x$ terms (along with $dx$) to the right, writing $\frac{1}{h(y)} \, dy = g(x) \, dx$, and integrating both sides. Why are we allowed to treat the derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ as if it were a fraction and integrate one side with respect to $y$ and the other with respect to $x$?

Far from being a mere notational trick, this heuristic procedure of "multiplying by $dx$ and dividing by $h(y)$" is rigorously justified by the Chain Rule and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus.

Let $y = \phi(x)$ be a differentiable solution on an interval $I$ such that $h(\phi(x)) \neq 0$ for all $x \in I$. We proceed in four rigorous steps:

1. **Reciprocal Division:**
   Because $h(\phi(x)) \neq 0$ on $I$, we can legally divide both sides of $\frac{dy}{dx} = g(x) h(y)$ by $h(y)$ to gather all $y$-dependence on the left:
   $$
   \frac{1}{h(y)} \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x)
   $$
2. **Definite Integration with Respect to $x$:**
   Since the two sides are identical functions of $x$ on $I$, their definite integrals with respect to the independent variable $x$ over any subinterval $[x_0, x] \subseteq I$ must be equal:
   $$
   \int_{x_0}^x \frac{1}{h(\phi(t))} \frac{d\phi}{dt} \, dt = \int_{x_0}^x g(t) \, dt
   $$
3. **Change of Variables (Substitution Rule):**
   Now look closely at the left-hand integral. By the Chain Rule (the Substitution Rule for definite integrals), we can make the substitution $u = \phi(t)$, which carries the differential $du = \phi'(t) \, dt = \frac{dy}{dt} \, dt$. Updating the limits of integration—when $t = x_0$, $u = \phi(x_0) = y_0$, and when $t = x$, $u = \phi(x) = y$—transforms the left-hand integral into an integral with respect to $u$:
   $$
   \int_{y_0}^y \frac{1}{h(u)} \, du = \int_{x_0}^x g(t) \, dt
   $$
4. **Indefinite Integral Formulation:**
   Expressed in terms of indefinite integrals, if $H(y)$ is any antiderivative of $\frac{1}{h(y)}$ (so that $H'(y) = \frac{1}{h(y)}$) and $G(x)$ is any antiderivative of $g(x)$ (so that $G'(x) = g(x)$), then our separated equation takes the familiar operational form:
   $$
   \int \frac{1}{h(y)} \, dy = \int g(x) \, dx
   $$
   Evaluating both antiderivatives yields the implicit algebraic relation:
   $$
   H(y) = G(x) + C
   $$
   where $C$ is an arbitrary single constant of integration absorbing the constants from both indefinite integrals.

### Equilibrium and Singular Solutions

Look back at Step 1 of our derivation: we divided both sides by $h(y)$ under the explicit assumption that $h(y) \neq 0$. What happens at values of $y$ where $h(y)$ *does* equal zero? Overlooking those values is one of the most common pitfalls in solving separable equations.

> [!warning] Notational Caution: Division by Zero and Lost Solutions
> Dividing by $h(y)$ is valid strictly on intervals where $h(y) \neq 0$. If there exist real numbers $c_k$ such that:
> $$
> h(c_k) = 0
> $$
> then the constant function:
> $$
> y(x) \equiv c_k
> $$
> has derivative $\frac{dy}{dx} = 0$. Substituting $y = c_k$ into the differential equation yields:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad g(x) h(c_k) = g(x) \cdot 0 = 0
> $$
> Thus, every real root $c_k$ of $h(y) = 0$ provides a constant solution, termed an **equilibrium solution** (or stationary solution).
> 
> When separating variables, these equilibrium solutions may not appear in the family $H(y) = G(x) + C$ for any finite value of $C$. Such solutions must be cataloged separately to avoid losing valid integral curves.

In other words, before dividing by $h(y)$, always pause to check whether $h(y) = 0$ has any real roots. Each root $y = c_k$ represents a horizontal line in the direction field where the slope is identically zero, meaning a system that starts at $y_0 = c_k$ stays at $c_k$ forever.

## First-Order Linear Differential Equations

### Definition and the Canonical Standard Form

Not every first-order differential equation is separable. For example, consider $\frac{dy}{dx} + y = e^x$: here the right-hand side is $e^x - y$, which cannot be factored into $g(x)h(y)$. Fortunately, this equation belongs to a second major class of solvable differential equations—**first-order linear equations**—which are arguably the most important differential equations in all of applied mathematics and physics.

> [!abstract] Definition: First-Order Linear Differential Equation
> A first-order differential equation is **linear** if it can be expressed as a linear polynomial in the dependent variable $y$ and its first derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$:
> $$
> A(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + B(x) y = C(x)
> $$
> On any interval where the leading coefficient $A(x) \neq 0$, division by $A(x)$ produces the **standard form**:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = Q(x)
> $$
> where $P(x) = \frac{B(x)}{A(x)}$ and $Q(x) = \frac{C(x)}{A(x)}$ are continuous functions on the interval of interest.

Notice why the equation is called *linear*: the unknown function $y$ and its derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ appear only to the first power and are never multiplied together or placed inside nonlinear functions like $y^2$, $\sin y$, or $e^y$. The coefficient functions $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$, by contrast, can be as nonlinear in $x$ as we please!

When preparing a linear differential equation for solution, three structural features of the standard form $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)$ dictate our entire strategy:

1. **Unity Leading Coefficient:** The derivative term $\frac{dy}{dx}$ must have a coefficient of identically $1$. If any variable or numeric factor $A(x)$ multiplies $\frac{dy}{dx}$, the entire equation must be divided by $A(x)$ before attempting to identify $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$.
2. **Algebraic Sign Convention:** The canonical sign between $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and $P(x)y$ is positive ($+$). If the equation features a negative sign, such as $\frac{dy}{dx} - \frac{3}{x}y = x$, the negative sign is bound directly to $P(x)$, so that $P(x) = -\frac{3}{x}$. Forgetting this negative sign will invert the integrating factor and ruin the solution.
3. **Classification of the Non-homogeneous Term:**
   - If $Q(x) \equiv 0$ on $I$, the equation:
     $$
     \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = 0
     $$
     is called **homogeneous**. Notice that a homogeneous first-order linear equation is immediately separable: $\frac{dy}{y} = -P(x) \, dx$.
   - If $Q(x) \not\equiv 0$, the equation is called **non-homogeneous** (or inhomogeneous), and $Q(x)$ acts as an external "driving" or "forcing" term.

### The Theory of the Integrating Factor

Why can't we directly integrate the standard form $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = Q(x)$? The obstacle is that the left-hand side consists of two separate terms involving $y$: its derivative $\frac{dy}{dx}$ and the multiple $P(x)y$.

However, look at the structure of the sum $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y$. Doesn't it vaguely resemble the result of differentiating a product using the Product Rule, $\frac{d}{dx}[v(x)y] = v(x)\frac{dy}{dx} + \frac{dv}{dx}y$? This observation sparks a brilliant idea: what if we multiply the entire differential equation through by an auxiliary function $v(x)$—called an **integrating factor**—specifically chosen so that the left-hand side:
$$
v(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) v(x) y
$$
collapses into the exact derivative $\frac{d}{dx}[v(x)y]$ of a single product with respect to $x$?

### Rigorous Step-by-Step Derivation of the Integrating Factor $v(x)$

Let us find out what function $v(x)$ accomplishes this magic. We seek a strictly positive, differentiable function $v(x) > 0$ such that multiplying the left-hand side of our standard equation by $v(x)$ matches the derivative of the product $v(x)y$:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}\left[v(x) y\right] = v(x)\left(\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y\right)
$$
Expanding the left-hand side using the Leibniz Product Rule gives:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}\left[v(x) y\right] = v(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + y \frac{dv}{dx}
$$
Equating this expansion with the right-hand side, we require:
$$
v(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + y \frac{dv}{dx} = v(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) v(x) y
$$
Subtracting the identical term $v(x) \frac{dy}{dx}$ from both sides leaves a remarkably clean requirement:
$$
y \frac{dv}{dx} = P(x) v(x) y
$$
Since this identity must hold for arbitrary solution functions $y(x)$, we divide both sides by $y$ to obtain the fundamental differential equation governing the integrating factor $v(x)$:
$$
\frac{dv}{dx} = P(x) v
$$
Look at what we have arrived at: this is a first-order *separable* differential equation for $v(x)$! Separating the variables $v$ and $x$ yields:
$$
\frac{1}{v} \, dv = P(x) \, dx
$$
Integrating both sides with respect to their variables gives:
$$
\int \frac{1}{v} \, dv = \int P(x) \, dx
$$
Because we sought a strictly positive function $v(x) > 0$ on the interval $I$, we have $|v| = v$, so evaluating the left-hand integral gives:
$$
\ln v = \int P(x) \, dx + C_1
$$
Finally, exponentiating both sides to solve explicitly for $v(x)$ produces:
$$
v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx + C_1} = e^{C_1} \cdot e^{\int P(x) \, dx} = A e^{\int P(x) \, dx}
$$
where $A = e^{C_1} > 0$ is an arbitrary positive constant.

> [!note] Theoretical Remark on the Arbitrary Constant in $v(x)$
> Do we need to carry the arbitrary constant $A$ (or $C_1$) when computing an integrating factor? No! We do not require the most general family of integrating factors; any single non-zero function that satisfies the product rule transformation condition will suffice (indeed, any multiplicative constant $A$ would simply cancel out from both sides of $v(x)\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)v(x)y = v(x)Q(x)$). Setting $A = 1$ (equivalent to choosing the arbitrary constant of integration $C_1 = 0$) produces the simplest canonical integrating factor:
> $$
> v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx}
> $$
> where $\int P(x) \, dx$ denotes any convenient antiderivative of $P(x)$.

### Derivation of the General Solution Formula

Now that we know how to construct $v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx}$, let us see how multiplying the differential equation by $v(x)$ unlocks the general solution $y(x)$ in just a few lines.

Starting with the standard form $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)$, we multiply every term on both sides by $v(x)$:
$$
v(x) \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) v(x) y = v(x) Q(x)
$$
By the exact way we designed $v(x)$, the entire left-hand side collapses into the derivative of the product $v(x) y$:
$$
\frac{d}{dx}\left[v(x) y\right] = v(x) Q(x)
$$
Now the derivative on the left is a single total derivative with respect to $x$, and the right-hand side is a known function of $x$ alone! Integrating both sides with respect to $x$ gives:
$$
\int \frac{d}{dx}\left[v(x) y\right] \, dx = \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx
$$
By the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, the left-hand side simplifies directly to $v(x)y$:
$$
v(x) y = \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + C
$$
Because $v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx} > 0$ for all real arguments, $v(x)$ is never zero. We can therefore divide both sides by $v(x)$ to isolate the explicit solution $y(x)$:
$$
y = \frac{1}{v(x)} \left[ \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + C \right]
$$
or, written in compact indefinite-integral notation:
$$
y = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx
$$
where the constant of integration $C$ is understood to be included within the indefinite integral $\int v(x) Q(x) \, dx$.

### The Master Theorem

Let us summarize this complete result in a formal theorem.

> [!theorem] Theorem 4: Solution of Linear First Order Differential Equations
> Let $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$ be continuous functions on an open interval $I$. Then the general solution of the linear first-order differential equation:
> $$
> \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = Q(x)
> $$
> is given by:
> $$
> y = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx
> $$
> where the integrating factor $v(x)$ is:
> $$
> v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx}
> $$
> In the construction of $v(x)$, any antiderivative of $P(x)$ may be chosen (the integration constant may be taken as zero).

**Proof:** As established in the preceding derivation, since $P(x)$ is continuous on the open interval $I$, it possesses a differentiable antiderivative $R(x) = \int P(x) \, dx$ on $I$ by the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus. Defining $v(x) = e^{R(x)} > 0$, the Chain Rule gives $v'(x) = R'(x)e^{R(x)} = P(x)v(x)$. Multiplying the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = Q(x)$ by the non-zero function $v(x)$ produces the equivalent equation $\frac{d}{dx}[v(x)y] = v(x)Q(x)$. Since $v(x)Q(x)$ is continuous on $I$, integrating both sides with respect to $x$ and dividing by $v(x) \neq 0$ yields all differentiable solutions $y(x) = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx$ on $I$. $\blacksquare$

When using Theorem 4, pay special attention to how the integration constant $C$ interacts with the reciprocal factor $\frac{1}{v(x)}$.

> [!warning] Notational Caution: The Placement of the Constant $C$
> The constant of integration $C$ arises from evaluating the outer integral $\int v(x) Q(x) \, dx$. Because the entire integrated quantity is divided by $v(x)$, the constant $C$ is also divided by $v(x)$:
> $$
> y(x) = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + \frac{C}{v(x)}
> $$
> Writing $y = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + C$ (placing $C$ outside the bracket) is a severe mathematical error that destroys the validity of the solution unless $v(x)$ is constant.

### Structural Decomposition: Homogeneous and Particular Components

If we examine the expanded formula $y(x) = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + \frac{C}{v(x)}$ more closely, we uncover a deep structural property shared by all linear differential equations (and linear algebraic systems $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$). The general solution of a linear first-order differential equation decomposes cleanly into the sum of two distinct parts:
$$
y(x) = y_p(x) + y_h(x)
$$
1. **The Homogeneous Solution ($y_h(x)$):**
   $$
   y_h(x) = \frac{C}{v(x)} = C e^{-\int P(x) \, dx}
   $$
   Notice that if we set the driving term $Q(x) = 0$, the integral term vanishes and $y(x)$ reduces purely to $y_h(x)$. Thus, $y_h(x)$ is the general one-parameter solution of the associated homogeneous equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = 0$, representing the system's internal, unforced response.
2. **The Particular Solution ($y_p(x)$):**
   $$
   y_p(x) = \frac{1}{v(x)} \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx
   $$
   This term (evaluated with $C = 0$) is a single specific solution of the complete non-homogeneous equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = Q(x)$, capturing the system's response to the external forcing function $Q(x)$.

Why does this decomposition work? By the principle of linear superposition, the difference between any two solutions of the non-homogeneous equation satisfies the homogeneous equation $\frac{dy}{dx} + P(x)y = 0$. Consequently, adding the full one-parameter family of homogeneous solutions $y_h(x)$ to any single particular solution $y_p(x)$ generates every possible solution of the differential equation.

### The Canonical 4-Step Solution Algorithm

Rather than memorizing the final integral formula as a black box, it is far safer and more illuminating in practice to execute the integrating-factor method step by step.

> [!summary] Summary of the 4-Step Algorithm for Linear First-Order Equations
> To solve any first-order linear differential equation systematically:
> 
> 1. **Put into Standard Form:**
>    Manipulate the equation algebraically into:
>    $$
>    \frac{dy}{dx} + P(x) y = Q(x)
>    $$
>    ensuring the coefficient of $\frac{dy}{dx}$ is $1$, and identifying $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$ precisely (including any negative signs).
> 
> 2. **Calculate an Antiderivative of $P(x)$:**
>    Evaluate:
>    $$
>    \int P(x) \, dx
>    $$
>    choosing the simplest antiderivative by setting the integration constant to zero.
> 
> 3. **Construct the Integrating Factor $v(x)$:**
>    Compute:
>    $$
>    v(x) = e^{\int P(x) \, dx}
>    $$
>    simplifying the exponential expression using logarithmic identities wherever applicable (e.g., $e^{\ln |g(x)|} = |g(x)|$, $e^{n \ln x} = e^{\ln(x^n)} = x^n$).
> 
> 4. **Evaluate the General Solution Integral:**
>    Multiply the standard form by $v(x)$ to collapse the left-hand side into $\frac{d}{dx}[v(x)y] = v(x)Q(x)$, integrate both sides, and divide by $v(x)$:
>    $$
>    y = \frac{1}{v(x)} \left[ \int v(x) Q(x) \, dx + C \right]
>    $$
>    ensuring the constant $C$ is introduced *before* dividing through by $v(x)$. If an initial condition $y(x_0) = y_0$ is given, substitute $x_0$ and $y_0$ to determine the unique scalar value of $C$.

---

# 6.12 Euler's Numerical Method; Slope Fields

## Qualitative and Numerical Foundations of Differential Equations

### The Necessity of Numerical and Qualitative Analysis

Up to this point, whenever we encountered a differential equation, our goal was to find an exact, closed-form analytical formula for the solution of the initial value problem:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y), \quad y(x_0) = y_0
$$
Why can't we just do that for every differential equation we meet? In reality, finding an explicit formula using elementary functions—polynomials, rational functions, trigonometric functions, exponentials, and logarithms—is frequently impossible. Even seemingly innocent first-order differential equations, such as $y' = x^2 + y^2$ or $y' = e^{-x^2}$, cannot be integrated in terms of elementary transcendental functions.

What do we do when a closed-form formula is unavailable, overly complicated for practical evaluation, or unnecessary for engineering tolerances? Instead of giving up, we attack the problem along two complementary paths:
1. **Qualitative Analysis:** We determine the geometric behavior, asymptotic trends, equilibrium states, and stability properties of solution curves directly from the differential equation itself, without ever solving it analytically (for instance, by inspecting its slope field).
2. **Quantitative Numerical Analysis:** We construct a discrete sequence of approximate numerical values that track the unknown exact solution curve across an interval of interest, keeping the approximation error within controllable bounds.

### Formal Definitions: Numerical Solution and Numerical Method

Before we build our first algorithm, let's clarify what it actually means to "solve" a differential equation numerically. Instead of obtaining a continuous formula $y(x)$, we generate a table of coordinates that closely shadows the true curve.

> [!abstract] Definition: Numerical Solution
> Given an initial value problem $y' = f(x, y)$ with $y(x_0) = y_0$ on an interval $[x_0, b]$, a **numerical solution** is a finite sequence or table of ordered pairs:
> $$
> \{(x_0, y_0), (x_1, y_1), (x_2, y_2), \dots, (x_n, y_n)\}
> $$
> where each $x_k \in [x_0, b]$ represents a discrete value of the independent variable, and each $y_k \approx y(x_k)$ is an approximation to the true value of the exact solution $y(x)$ evaluated at $x = x_k$.

How do we generate those approximate values $y_k$? We need a step-by-step recipe that uses the rate of change $f(x, y)$ to march forward from our starting point.

> [!abstract] Definition: Numerical Method
> A **numerical method** is a deterministic mathematical algorithm or iterative recurrence relation that systematically computes the sequence of approximate values $\{y_k\}$ from the differential equation $y' = f(x, y)$, the initial condition $(x_0, y_0)$, and a chosen step size $dx$.

In other words, a numerical method is the computational engine, and the numerical solution is the table of points it produces. Numerical methods form the computational foundation of modern science and engineering, and nearly all advanced single-step and multi-step algorithms—such as the Runge-Kutta family and predictor-corrector schemes—trace their theoretical origins back to the foundational method introduced by Leonhard Euler in 1768.

## Slope Fields (Direction Fields)

### Geometric Definition and Construction of Direction Fields

How can we visualize the solutions to a differential equation before we ever try to solve it? Notice that every first-order differential equation:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)
$$
carries built-in geometric information. At any point $(x, y)$ in the domain of $f$, the scalar value $f(x, y)$ tells us the exact instantaneous rate of change—that is, the slope of the tangent line—of the solution curve passing through that point. Even if we do not know the equation of the curve $y(x)$, we know the exact slope it must have at every single point in the plane!

> [!abstract] Definition: Slope Field (Direction Field)
> Let $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2$ be the domain of the real-valued function $f(x, y)$. The **slope field** (or **direction field**) associated with the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ is the geometric assignment of a short line segment of slope:
> $$
> m = f(x, y)
> $$
> centered at each coordinate point $(x, y) \in D$.

Each of these short segments—often called a *lineal element* or *tangent element*—is tangent to the unique solution curve passing through its center. Consequently, any integral curve $y = \phi(x)$ of the differential equation must be everywhere tangent to the lineal elements of the slope field through which it passes.

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1791111708134.webp]]

### Graphical Portrayal: Vector Renditions versus Scalar Slopes

When you look at computer-generated slope fields (such as those depicted in Figure 6.41 above), you will often see the lineal elements drawn with tiny arrowheads (vector glyphs) to make the visual flow across the grid easier to follow. However, you should be careful not to read too much into those arrowheads.

> [!warning] Notational Caution: Slopes Have No Direction
> Although computer plotting packages frequently draw direction field segments with arrows or directed vectors, this visual convention must not be interpreted as an indication that slopes possess physical direction or vector polarity.
> 
> Mathematically:
> - A slope $m = f(x, y)$ is a pure scalar quantity representing the tangent gradient $\frac{dy}{dx} = \tan \theta$.
> - A slope segment defines an unoriented line of inclination $\theta$, not a directed vector with a designated sense of traversal (forward versus backward).
> - In an autonomous or non-autonomous system, time or the independent variable $x$ may increase ($dx > 0$), but the slope itself remains an undirected geometric tangent.

### Qualitative Analysis of Integral Curves

Think of a slope field as a visual map of iron filings aligned with a magnetic field, or streamlines in a flowing river. By inspecting a slope field, we can visualize the entire one-parameter family of solution curves without performing a single symbolic integration:
1. **Tracing Solution Trajectories:** If we start at a prescribed initial point $(x_0, y_0)$ and follow the tangent segments continuously—always moving parallel to the local lineal elements—we trace out the global trajectory of the particular solution curve passing through $(x_0, y_0)$.
2. **Equilibria and Nullclines:**
   - Curves in the $xy$-plane along which $f(x, y) = 0$ are called **nullclines** (or directional zero-isoclines). Along a nullcline, all slope segments are strictly horizontal ($m = 0$), marking potential local extrema or flat equilibrium states.
   - More generally, if $f(x, y) = c$ for any constant $c$, the locus of points satisfying this relation is called an **isocline** of constant slope $c$.
3. **Asymptotic Convergence and Funneling:** Slope fields immediately reveal long-term qualitative behavior, showing us whether neighboring solution curves funnel together toward a common limiting path (attractors), peel apart and diverge toward infinity (instability), or oscillate periodically.

## Tangent Line Linearization and Euler's Stepping Mechanism

### The Principle of Local Linearization

Now let's turn our geometric intuition from slope fields into a quantitative calculator. Instead of sketching a curve by eye, how can we compute numerical coordinates along the trajectory? The analytic foundation of Euler's method is a familiar friend from differential calculus: local approximation of a differentiable function by its tangent line (linearization).

Suppose $y = y(x)$ is the exact, unknown solution to the initial value problem:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y), \quad y(x_0) = y_0
$$
Since $y(x)$ is differentiable at $x_0$, zooming in closely around $(x_0, y_0)$ makes the curve look nearly indistinguishable from its tangent line. Recall that the linearization $L(x)$ of $y(x)$ at the known initial point $(x_0, y_0)$ is given by the first-order Taylor polynomial:
$$
L(x) = y(x_0) + \left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{(x_0, y_0)} (x - x_0)
$$
Even though we don't know the formula for $y(x)$, we know both pieces of data needed to build this tangent line! Substituting the initial condition $y(x_0) = y_0$ and the slope from the differential equation $\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{(x_0, y_0)} = f(x_0, y_0)$, we get:
$$
L(x) = y_0 + f(x_0, y_0)(x - x_0)
$$

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1791111784677.webp]]

By the fundamental property of linear approximation, this tangent line $L(x)$ provides an accurate approximation to the true solution $y(x)$ within a sufficiently small open interval centered at $x_0$:
$$
y(x) \approx L(x) \quad \text{for } |x - x_0| \ll 1
$$

### Discrete Propagation: Stepping by Increment $dx$

If a single tangent line only stays close to the curve near $x_0$, how do we approximate the solution far away from $x_0$? Euler's brilliant idea was to march forward in small discrete steps of length $dx$ (often denoted as step size $h = \Delta x$), recalculating the slope at the end of every step. Let's trace the first few steps of this journey:

1. **First Advance:**
   We step forward from $x_0$ by a small increment $dx$ to define the next independent coordinate:
   $$
   x_1 = x_0 + dx
   $$
   To estimate the solution's height at $x_1$, we evaluate the tangent line $L(x)$ at $x = x_1$:
   $$
   y_1 = L(x_1) = y_0 + f(x_0, y_0)(x_1 - x_0) = y_0 + f(x_0, y_0) \, dx
   $$
   Notice an important subtlety here: while the initial point $(x_0, y_0)$ lies *exactly* on the true solution curve $y = y(x)$, our newly calculated point $(x_1, y_1)$ generally does not lie on the curve. Instead, it sits on the tangent line to the curve at $(x_0, y_0)$. However, as long as the increment $dx$ is small, $(x_1, y_1)$ lies very close to the exact solution point $(x_1, y(x_1))$.

2. **Second Advance:**
   Now we stand at the approximate point $(x_1, y_1)$ and want to take a second step. Ideally, we would use the slope of the true solution curve at $x_1$, which is $f(x_1, y(x_1))$. Why can't we use that? Because $y(x_1)$ is unknown, and our point $(x_1, y_1)$ has drifted slightly off the true curve! What is the next best thing? We ask the slope field what the slope is at our current location $(x_1, y_1)$:
   $$
   m_1 = f(x_1, y_1)
   $$
   Using this approximate slope, we advance by another step $dx$ to $x_2 = x_1 + dx$ and compute the second ordinate:
   $$
   y_2 = y_1 + f(x_1, y_1) \, dx
   $$

3. **Successive Stepping:**
   Continuing in this exact same fashion, we use the slope $f(x_2, y_2)$ at $(x_2, y_2)$ to step to $x_3 = x_2 + dx$, yielding our third approximation:
   $$
   y_3 = y_2 + f(x_2, y_2) \, dx
   $$

![[06. Transcendental Functions-1791111804301.webp]]

### Euler's Polygonal Path

What does this stepping process look like geometrically? As you can see in Figure 6.43, Euler's method constructs a continuous, piecewise-linear curve—called an **Euler polygonal path**—formed by connecting the calculated vertices:
$$
(x_0, y_0), \, (x_1, y_1), \, (x_2, y_2), \, \dots, \, (x_n, y_n)
$$
with straight line segments. On each subinterval $[x_{k-1}, x_k]$, the slope of the polygonal segment is held constant at the value $f(x_{k-1}, y_{k-1})$.

Because each step after the first launches from an already approximate point $(x_{k-1}, y_{k-1})$ using a slightly perturbed slope $f(x_{k-1}, y_{k-1})$, you can see in Figure 6.43 that the gap between the polygonal path and the true integral curve tends to accumulate as we take more and more steps.

## The Formal Euler Method Algorithm

### Formulation for Uniform Partitions

Let's package this stepping procedure into a clean, systematic algorithm. Suppose $[x_0, b]$ is the computational interval on which we want to approximate the solution to the initial value problem:
$$
\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y), \quad y(x_0) = y_0
$$
To construct an $n$-step approximation across $[x_0, b]$, we first divide the interval into $n$ subintervals of equal width:
$$
dx = \frac{b - x_0}{n}
$$
This generates a uniform grid of discrete mesh points (nodes) along the $x$-axis via the recurrence:
$$
\begin{aligned}
x_1 &= x_0 + dx \\
x_2 &= x_1 + dx \\
&\;\;\vdots \\
x_n &= x_{n-1} + dx
\end{aligned}
$$
Equivalently, we can write any node directly in closed non-recursive form:
$$
x_k = x_0 + k \, dx \quad \text{for } k = 0, 1, 2, \dots, n
$$

At each node, the corresponding sequence of approximate $y$-values $\{y_k\}$ is generated iteratively via the canonical Euler equations:
$$
\begin{aligned}
y_1 &= y_0 + f(x_0, y_0) \, dx \\
y_2 &= y_1 + f(x_1, y_1) \, dx \\
&\;\;\vdots \\
y_n &= y_{n-1} + f(x_{n-1}, y_{n-1}) \, dx
\end{aligned}
$$

> [!abstract] Definition: General Euler Recurrence Relation
> For any step index $k \ge 0$, the $(k+1)$-th approximation in Euler's method is given by the difference equation:
> $$
> y_{k+1} = y_k + f(x_k, y_k) \, dx
> $$
> with the mesh coordinate updated by:
> $$
> x_{k+1} = x_k + dx
> $$

In words, the new value $y_{k+1}$ is always the old value $y_k$ plus the local rate of change $f(x_k, y_k)$ multiplied by the step size $dx$.

### Finite Difference Interpretation

Beyond tangent-line geometry, there is a second, purely algebraic way to see where Euler's formula comes from: the limit definition of the derivative.

Recall that at $x = x_k$, the derivative is defined as the limit of a difference quotient:
$$
\left.\frac{dy}{dx}\right|_{x = x_k} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{y(x_k + \Delta x) - y(x_k)}{\Delta x}
$$
What happens if we stop taking the limit and instead choose a small, fixed, non-zero increment $\Delta x = dx$? Replacing the continuous derivative with this **forward finite difference quotient** gives the approximation:
$$
\frac{y(x_k + dx) - y(x_k)}{dx} \approx y'(x_k) = f(x_k, y(x_k))
$$
Now, simply multiply both sides by $dx$ and add $y(x_k)$ to isolate the forward value $y(x_k + dx)$:
$$
y(x_k + dx) \approx y(x_k) + f(x_k, y(x_k)) \, dx
$$
When we replace the exact function values $y(x_k)$ and $y(x_k + dx)$ with their discrete computational approximations $y_k$ and $y_{k+1}$, we immediately recover Euler's stepping formula:
$$
y_{k+1} = y_k + f(x_k, y_k) \, dx
$$

## Mathematical Error Analysis and Convergence

### Local Truncation Error (LTE)

Whenever we use an approximation, a natural question arises: *how far off are we, and how fast does the error shrink when we make the step size $dx$ smaller?* To answer this, numerical analysts distinguish between the error made in a single step and the total error accumulated over many steps.

The error introduced during a single isolated step of the algorithm—assuming for the moment that our step starts from an *exact* point on the true solution curve—is called the **local truncation error**. Why the word "truncation"? Because linearizing a function amounts to truncating its infinite Taylor series after the first-order term!

> [!theorem] Local Truncation Error of Euler's Method
> Let $y(x)$ be twice continuously differentiable on $[x_0, b]$. If $y_k = y(x_k)$ is exact, the local truncation error $e_{\text{local}} = y(x_k + dx) - y_{k+1}$ incurred in advancing by a single step $dx$ satisfies:
> $$
> e_{\text{local}} = \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2
> $$
> for some $\xi_k \in (x_k, x_k + dx)$. Consequently:
> $$
> e_{\text{local}} = \mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)
> $$

**Proof:**
Suppose $y(x)$ possesses continuous derivatives through order $2$ on the subinterval $[x_k, x_k + dx]$. By Taylor's Theorem with the Lagrange form of the remainder, we can expand the exact solution $y(x_k + dx)$ around $x_k$:
$$
y(x_k + dx) = y(x_k) + y'(x_k) \, dx + \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2
$$
for some unknown intermediate point $\xi_k \in (x_k, x_k + dx)$. Since $y(x)$ solves the differential equation $y' = f(x, y)$, we can substitute $y'(x_k) = f(x_k, y(x_k))$ into this expansion:
$$
y(x_k + dx) = y(x_k) + f(x_k, y(x_k)) \, dx + \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2
$$
Now compare this exact formula with the numerical value $y_{k+1}$ predicted by one step of Euler's method starting from the exact point $y_k = y(x_k)$:
$$
y_{k+1} = y(x_k) + f(x_k, y(x_k)) \, dx
$$
Subtracting Euler's prediction $y_{k+1}$ from the true value $y(x_k + dx)$, the constant and linear terms cancel completely:
$$
\begin{aligned}
e_{\text{local}} &= y(x_k + dx) - y_{k+1} \\
&= \left[ y(x_k) + f(x_k, y(x_k)) \, dx + \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2 \right] - \left[ y(x_k) + f(x_k, y(x_k)) \, dx \right] \\
&= \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2
\end{aligned}
$$
Because this remainder term is proportional to the square of the step size, $(dx)^2$, the local truncation error of Euler's method is second-order: $e_{\text{local}} = \mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)$. $\blacksquare$

### Global Truncation Error (GTE) and Cumulative Error Propagation

If the error in a single step is proportional to $(dx)^2$, does that mean our final answer at $x = b$ is also accurate to order $(dx)^2$? Unfortunately, no! In practical computation, we don't just take one step—we take $n$ steps across the entire interval $[x_0, b]$, and the small errors from each step pile up. This total accumulated discrepancy at the end of the interval is the **global truncation error**.

> [!abstract] Definition: Global Truncation Error
> The **global truncation error** (or cumulative error) at the target terminal coordinate $x_n = b$ is the absolute difference between the exact analytical solution value and the numerical approximation obtained after $n$ steps:
> $$
> E_{\text{global}} = |y(b) - y_n|
> $$

Let's see why piling up $n$ steps reduces our accuracy by one full power of $dx$. To cross the fixed interval $[x_0, b]$ using steps of size $dx$, the total number of steps we must take is:
$$
n = \frac{b - x_0}{dx}
$$
Notice that $n$ is inversely proportional to $dx$: if we make $dx$ ten times smaller, we have to take ten times as many steps. Assuming that the local errors accumulate roughly additively over these $n$ steps (and that error amplification is bounded via the Lipschitz continuity of $f$), we can bound the global error by summing the local errors:
$$
E_{\text{global}} \approx \sum_{k=1}^n e_{\text{local}} \le n \cdot \max_k |e_{\text{local}}|
$$
Substituting $n = \frac{b - x_0}{dx}$ and the local error bound $|e_{\text{local}}| \le C (dx)^2$, one factor of $dx$ cancels out:
$$
E_{\text{global}} \le \left(\frac{b - x_0}{dx}\right) \cdot C (dx)^2 = C (b - x_0) \, dx
$$
Therefore, the global truncation error scales linearly with $dx$:
$$
E_{\text{global}} = \mathcal{O}(dx)
$$

> [!summary] Master Classification: Order of Euler's Method
> Euler's method is a **first-order numerical method**.
> - The local truncation error (per step) is $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)$.
> - The global (cumulative) truncation error across a fixed interval is $\mathcal{O}(dx)$.

### The Step-Halving Rule of First-Order Methods

What does first-order convergence ($E_{\text{global}} = \mathcal{O}(dx)$) mean for us in practice when we run Euler's method on a computer? It gives us a simple, predictable rule of thumb called the **step-halving rule**:
- Because the global error is directly proportional to $dx$, cutting the step size in half ($dx \to \frac{dx}{2}$) doubles the required number of steps ($n \to 2n$) and cuts the final global error approximately in half:
  $$
  E_{\text{global}}\left(\frac{dx}{2}\right) \approx \frac{1}{2} E_{\text{global}}(dx)
  $$
- For instance, in standard test problems where reducing the step size from $dx = 0.1$ ($10$ steps) to $dx = 0.05$ ($20$ steps) is performed, the terminal relative error drops roughly in half—from approximately $5.6\%$ down to $2.9\%$. To gain just one extra decimal place of accuracy (reducing error by a factor of $10$), you must do $10$ times as much computational work!

### Geometric Curvature and Systematic Error Bias

Can we predict ahead of time whether Euler's approximation $y_n$ will be *too high* or *too low* compared to the true answer $y(b)$? Look again at our exact formula for the local truncation error:
$$
e_{\text{local}} = y(x_k + dx) - y_{k+1} = \frac{1}{2} y''(\xi_k) (dx)^2
$$
Because $(dx)^2$ is always positive, the sign of the error is completely controlled by the second derivative $y''$—that is, the **concavity** of the true solution curve!

1. **Concave Up Solutions ($y''(x) > 0$):**
   When a curve is concave upward, it bends upward away from its tangent lines, meaning every tangent line lies strictly *below* the curve:
   $$
   L(x) \le y(x)
   $$
   Consequently, $e_{\text{local}} > 0$, and Euler's method systematically **underestimates** the true solution values ($y_k < y(x_k)$).
2. **Concave Down Solutions ($y''(x) < 0$):**
   When a curve is concave downward, it bends downward beneath its tangent lines, meaning every tangent line lies strictly *above* the curve:
   $$
   L(x) \ge y(x)
   $$
   Consequently, $e_{\text{local}} < 0$, and Euler's method systematically **overestimates** the true solution values ($y_k > y(x_k)$).
3. **Points of Inflection ($y''(x) = 0$):**
   At an inflection point where concavity changes sign, the tangent line crosses through the curve, and the local systematic bias switches between underestimation and overestimation.

### Truncation Error versus Floating-Point Round-Off Error

Mathematically, since $E_{\text{truncation}} = \mathcal{O}(dx)$, we have the theoretical limit:
$$
\lim_{dx \to 0} E_{\text{truncation}}(dx) = 0
$$
So why don't we just set $dx = 10^{-20}$ on our computer and get an essentially exact answer? Because that limit assumes we are doing arithmetic with infinitely many decimal places! Real digital computers store numbers with finite precision (typically 64-bit IEEE 754 floating-point format, which holds about $15$ to $17$ significant decimal digits).

> [!warning] Error Trade-Off and the Buildup of Round-Off Errors
> In any real computer calculation, the total error is the sum of two competing error sources:
> $$
> E_{\text{total}} = E_{\text{truncation}} + E_{\text{round-off}}
> $$
> 
> 1. **Discretization / Truncation Error ($E_{\text{truncation}} \propto dx$):**
>    Comes from approximating a curved trajectory by a finite string of straight tangent segments. It shrinks linearly as $dx$ becomes smaller.
> 2. **Machine Round-Off Error ($E_{\text{round-off}} \propto \frac{\epsilon_{\text{mach}}}{dx}$):**
>    Every floating-point arithmetic operation introduces a tiny rounding error bounded by machine epsilon $\epsilon_{\text{mach}} \approx 10^{-16}$. Because crossing the interval $[x_0, b]$ requires $n = \frac{b - x_0}{dx}$ steps, the accumulated round-off error grows with the number of steps:
>    $$
>    E_{\text{round-off}} \approx n \cdot \epsilon_{\text{mach}} = \frac{b - x_0}{dx} \cdot \epsilon_{\text{mach}}
>    $$
>    As $dx \to 0$, the step count $n \to \infty$, and the accumulated round-off error blows up without bound!

### The Optimal Step Size Phenomenon

Because truncation error decreases with $dx$ while round-off error increases with $1/dx$, a tug-of-war ensues between the two errors. Their sum $E_{\text{total}}$ reaches a minimum at a critical threshold called the **optimal step size** $dx_{\text{optimal}}$.

Shrinking $dx$ below $dx_{\text{optimal}}$ actually makes your numerical answer *worse*, not better: round-off noise overwhelms the calculation, causing the numerical solution to degrade and destabilize, while also consuming vastly more computing time and memory.

## Synthesis and Comparative Perspective

### Euler's Method as the Foundational Prototype

Because Euler's method converges slowly ($\mathcal{O}(dx)$) and requires tiny step sizes that invite round-off error and long runtimes, it is rarely used for heavy-duty scientific computing on its own. Why, then, do we study it so carefully? Because it is the conceptual prototype for the entire field of computational differential equations!

How do modern numerical methods achieve high precision without shrinking $dx$ to impractical extremes? Instead of relying solely on the slope at the very beginning of the step, they sample the slope field at multiple trial points across each subinterval $[x_k, x_k + dx]$ to obtain a much more representative average slope:
1. **Improved Euler Method (Heun's Predictor-Corrector Method):**
   First uses a standard Euler step to *predict* a provisional endpoint $y_{k+1}^*$, and then *corrects* the step by averaging the initial slope $f(x_k, y_k)$ with the predicted end slope $f(x_{k+1}, y_{k+1}^*)$. This symmetric averaging cancels out the leading error term, yielding **second-order** global accuracy:
   $$
   E_{\text{global}} = \mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)
   $$
2. **Classical Fourth-Order Runge-Kutta Method (RK4):**
   Computes a weighted average (analogous to Simpson's rule in numerical integration) of four trial slopes sampled at the start, midpoint, and end of the subinterval $[x_k, x_k + dx]$. This achieves **fourth-order** global accuracy:
   $$
   E_{\text{global}} = \mathcal{O}\left((dx)^4\right)
   $$
   Under RK4, merely halving the step size ($dx \to dx/2$) slashes the global truncation error by a dramatic factor of $2^4 = 16$!

### Comparative Summary of Numerical ODE Methods

| Method | Order of Method | Local Truncation Error (LTE) | Global Truncation Error (GTE) | Slope Evaluations per Step | Geometric / Mathematical Mechanism |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Euler's Method** | First-Order | $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)$ | $\mathcal{O}(dx)$ | $1$ | Tangent line linearization at step origin $(x_k, y_k)$ |
| **Improved Euler (Heun)** | Second-Order | $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^3\right)$ | $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^2\right)$ | $2$ | Trapezoidal average of initial and predicted end slopes |
| **Runge-Kutta 4 (RK4)** | Fourth-Order | $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^5\right)$ | $\mathcal{O}\left((dx)^4\right)$ | $4$ | Simpson's rule weighted average of four subinterval slopes |

---

## 6.1 Inverse Functions and Their Derivatives

### One-to-One Functions

- Define a one-to-one (injective) function on a domain $D$ in both its direct and contrapositive forms, and explain why this condition is necessary for reversing a function.

**The Horizontal Line Test**
- State the Horizontal Line Test and explain geometrically why a horizontal line intersecting a graph more than once violates the definition of a one-to-one function.

### Inverses

**Formal Definition and Domain–Range Relations**
- Define the inverse function $f^{-1}$ of a one-to-one function $f$, and state how the domain and range of $f^{-1}$ relate to the domain and range of $f$.
- Explain the notational distinction between the inverse function $f^{-1}(x)$ and the algebraic reciprocal $(f(x))^{-1}$.

**Composition and Identity Characterization**
- State the Inverse Composition Rule characterizing when two functions $f$ and $g$ form an inverse pair.

**Existence Criteria (Connection to Monotonicity and the MVT)**
- Explain why strictly increasing or strictly decreasing functions on an interval are always one-to-one.
- State the derivative criteria (from Corollary 3 of the Mean Value Theorem) that guarantee a differentiable function $f$ has an inverse on an interval $I$.

### Finding and Graphing Inverses

**Geometric Symmetry Across the Line $y = x$**
- State the geometric relationship between the graphs of $y = f(x)$ and $y = f^{-1}(x)$, and justify it using coordinate pairs.

**Algebraic Procedure to Express $f^{-1}$ as a Function of $x$**
- Outline the two-step algebraic procedure for finding an explicit formula for $y = f^{-1}(x)$ from $y = f(x)$ and relate each step to the graph.

**Domain Restrictions**
- Explain how domain restrictions allow non-one-to-one functions to be inverted, and illustrate this process using $f(x) = x^2$.

### Derivatives of Inverses of Differentiable Functions

**Reciprocal Slopes of Reflected Tangents**
- Derive the slope of a nonvertical, nonhorizontal line $y = mx + b$ after reflection across the line $y = x$, and use this geometric property to relate the tangent slopes of $f$ at $(a, f(a))$ and $f^{-1}$ at $(f(a), a)$.

**The Derivative Rule for Inverses**
- State Theorem 1 (The Derivative Rule for Inverses), including all hypotheses on $f$ and $f'$, and write the derivative formula in Leibniz, prime, and operator notations.
- Prove Theorem 1 in two main stages: first establish the existence and continuity of $f^{-1}$ on $f(I)$, and then prove differentiability at $b = f(a)$ using the limit definition of the derivative.

**Differential and Rate-of-Change Interpretation**
- Interpret the reciprocal relationship between the derivatives of $f$ and $f^{-1}$ using differentials $dx$ and $dy$ and relative rates of change.

**Chain Rule Connection**
- Assuming both $f$ and $f^{-1}$ are differentiable, derive the formula for $(f^{-1})'(f(x))$ by differentiating the composition identity $f^{-1}(f(x)) = x$.

### 6.1 Exercises: Theory and Applications (Selected Problems 45–52)

**Problem 45: Invertibility of Negated Functions**
- If $f(x)$ is one-to-one, can anything be said about $g(x) = -f(x)$? Give both an algebraic proof and a geometric explanation for your answer.

**Problem 46: Invertibility of Reciprocal Functions**
- If $f(x)$ is one-to-one and $f(x)$ is never $0$, can anything be said about $h(x) = 1/f(x)$? Give reasons and a formal proof for your answer.

**Problem 47: Composition of One-to-One Functions**
- Suppose that the range of $g$ lies in the domain of $f$ so that the composite $f \circ g$ is defined. If $f$ and $g$ are one-to-one, can anything be said about $f \circ g$? Give reasons and a proof for your answer.

**Problem 48: One-to-One Property of Inner Functions in a Composite**
- If a composite $f \circ g$ is one-to-one, must $g$ be one-to-one? Give reasons and a proof for your answer.

**Problem 49: Geometric Area Integral Identity for Inverses**
- Suppose $f(x)$ is positive, continuous, and increasing over the interval $[a, b]$. By interpreting the graph of $f$, show that $\int_a^b f(x) \, dx + \int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} f^{-1}(y) \, dy = b f(b) - a f(a)$, and also verify the identity analytically assuming $f$ is continuously differentiable.

**Problem 50: Invertibility of Linear Fractional (Möbius) Transformations**
- Determine conditions on the constants $a, b, c,$ and $d$ so that the rational function $f(x) = \frac{ax + b}{cx + d}$ has an inverse, and find an explicit formula for $f^{-1}$ when those conditions hold.

**Problem 51: Chain Rule Connection and Differentiability of Inverses**
- If we write $g(x)$ for $f^{-1}(x)$, the derivative formula can be written as $g'(f(a)) = \frac{1}{f'(a)}$, or $g'(f(a)) \cdot f'(a) = 1$. Replacing $a$ with $x$ yields $g'(f(x)) \cdot f'(x) = 1$. Assume that $f$ and $g$ are differentiable functions that are inverses of one another, so that $(g \circ f)(x) = x$. Differentiate both sides of this equation with respect to $x$, using the Chain Rule to express $(g \circ f)'(x)$ as a product of derivatives of $g$ and $f$. What do you find? Explain why this is not a proof of Theorem 1.

**Problem 52: Equivalence of the Washer and Shell Methods for Inverses**
- Let $f$ be differentiable on the interval $a \le x \le b$, with $a > 0$, and suppose that $f$ has a differentiable inverse, $f^{-1}$. Revolve about the $y$-axis the region bounded by the graph of $f$ and the lines $x = a$ and $y = f(b)$ to generate a solid. Then the values of the integrals given by the washer and shell methods for the volume have identical values: $\int_{f(a)}^{f(b)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy = \int_a^b 2\pi x (f(b) - f(x)) \, dx$. To prove this equality, define $W(t) = \int_{f(a)}^{f(t)} \pi \left( (f^{-1}(y))^2 - a^2 \right) dy$ and $S(t) = \int_a^t 2\pi x (f(t) - f(x)) \, dx$. Show that the functions $W$ and $S$ agree at a point of $[a, b]$ and have identical derivatives on $[a, b]$, thereby establishing $W(t) = S(t)$ for all $t \in [a, b]$, and in particular $W(b) = S(b)$.
