**[Differentiability and the Derivative of $e^x$]**
- Justify why $y = e^x$ is differentiable on $(-\infty, \infty)$ before computing its derivative.
- Derive the derivative formula for $\frac{d}{dx} e^x$ via implicit differentiation, and state how $y = e^x$ is uniquely characterized by the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = y$.

**[Chain Rule Generalization and Rate-of-Change Interpretation]**
- State the general Chain Rule formula and differential form for the derivative of $e^u$ where $u = u(x)$.
- Compare the absolute rate of change and the relative (percentage) rate of change of $y = e^x$.

**[Linearization of $e^x$ at $x = 0$]**
- Derive the linear approximation of $e^x$ centered at $x = 0$, and use concavity to prove the global inequality comparing $e^x$ and its tangent line across $\mathbb{R}$.

### Theoretical Foundations: Algebraic vs. Transcendental Classifications

**[Algebraic vs. Transcendental Numbers]**
- Define algebraic and transcendental numbers, provide examples of rational and irrational algebraic numbers, and summarize the historical milestones regarding the transcendence of $e$ and $\pi$.

**[Algebraic vs. Transcendental Functions]**
- Define algebraic and transcendental functions, demonstrate why $y = \frac{1}{\sqrt{x+1}}$ is algebraic, and list the primary families of elementary transcendental functions.

### Integration Involving the Exponential Function

**[The Indefinite and Definite Integral of $e^u$]**
- State the indefinite integral formula for $\int e^u \, du$ and the corresponding substitution formula for definite integrals $\int_a^b e^{g(x)} g'(x) \, dx$.

**[Separable Differential Equations and Initial Value Problems]**
- Define a separable first-order differential equation in both derivative and differential forms.
- Outline the five-step method for solving an initial value problem of the form $e^y \frac{dy}{dx} = g(x)$ with $y(x_0) = y_0$, including how to determine the domain of the explicit solution and verify the result.

### Advanced Theoretical Results and Geometric Dualities

**[Complementary Area Duality for Inverse Functions]**
- State and prove the integral area duality identity relating $\int_1^a \ln x \, dx$ and $\int_0^{\ln a} e^y \, dy$ for $a > 1$ using a geometric partitioning argument.

**[The Geometric, Logarithmic, and Arithmetic Mean Inequality]**
- Use the strict concavity of $y = e^x$ on $[\ln a, \ln b]$ ($0 < a < b$) together with midpoint tangent and secant trapezoidal bounds to state and prove the inequality comparing the geometric mean, logarithmic mean, and arithmetic mean of $a$ and $b$.
- Define the logarithmic mean $L(a, b)$ and describe its physical significance in engineering transport phenomena.
- Explain geometrically why the area of a trapezoid equals the length of its midpoint cross-section multiplied by its width.

## 6.4 $a^x$ and $\log_a x$

### The General Exponential Function $a^x$

**Definition of Arbitrary Real Powers**

- State the formal definition of the general exponential function $a^x$ for any base $a > 0$ and real exponent $x$, specify its domain and range, and describe the special case $a = 1$.
- Explain the distinction between the status of this identity when $x$ is rational versus when $x$ is irrational.

**Laws of Exponents for General Bases**
- State and prove the four algebraic laws of exponents (product, reciprocal, quotient, and power of a power) for a positive base $a > 0$ and arbitrary real exponents $x, y$.

### The Power Rule in Final Form

**Generalizing the Power Identity for Logarithms**
- State and prove the power rule for the natural logarithm $\ln(x^n)$ for any $x > 0$ and any real number $n \in \mathbb{R}$.

**Derivation of the Derivative of $x^n$**
- Derive the derivative of $x^n$ with respect to $x$ for $x > 0$ and any real exponent $n \in \mathbb{R}$.

**The Power Rule (Final Form)**
- State the final form of the Power Rule for a positive differentiable function $u(x)$ and an arbitrary real exponent $n$ in both derivative and differential forms.
- Outline the four progressive stages of generality through which the Power Rule is developed across calculus.

### Differentiation and Analysis of $a^x$

**Derivative of $a^x$ and $a^u$**
- Derive the derivative of $a^x$ for a positive constant $a > 0$, and state the general Chain Rule formula for $a^u$ in both derivative and differential forms.
- Explain from the derivative formula why the base $e$ is considered the natural base in calculus.

**Monotonicity, Concavity, and Asymptotic Behavior of $y = a^x$**
- Analyze the curve $y = a^x$ across the cases $a > 1$, $0 < a < 1$, and $a = 1$ by determining its first derivative and monotonicity, its second derivative and concavity, and its horizontal asymptotes as $x \to \pm\infty$.

### Differentiation of Variable-Base, Variable-Exponent Functions

**Method 1: Converting to Base $e$**
- Derive the derivative of a variable-base, variable-exponent function $y = f(x)^{g(x)}$ ($f(x) > 0$) by rewriting the expression in base $e$.

**Method 2: Logarithmic Differentiation**
- Derive the derivative of $y = f(x)^{g(x)}$ ($f(x) > 0$) via logarithmic differentiation.
- Explain how the resulting derivative decomposes into two additive terms corresponding to the Power Rule and the Exponential Rule.

### Integration Involving $a^u$

**The Indefinite and Definite Integral of $a^u$**
- Derive the indefinite integral formula for $\int a^u \, du$, state the restriction on the base $a$, and state the corresponding definite integral formula under a substitution $u = g(x)$.

### Logarithmic Functions with Base $a$

**Invertibility and Definition of $\log_a x$**
- Justify why $f(x) = a^x$ ($a > 0, a \neq 1$) is invertible, state the formal definition of the base-$a$ logarithm $\log_a x$, and specify its domain and range.

**Fundamental Inverse Identities and Graphs**
- State the two fundamental inverse cancellation identities relating $a^x$ and $\log_a x$, and describe how the graph, intercept, and asymptote of $y = \log_a x$ relate to those of $y = a^x$.

**Evaluation of $\log_a x$ and the Change of Base Formula**
- State and prove the Change of Base formula expressing $\log_a x$ in terms of natural logarithms.
- State the general Change of Base formula between two arbitrary bases $a$ and $b$, and deduce the reciprocal identity relating $\log_a b$ and $\log_b a$.

**Arithmetic Properties of Base $a$ Logarithms**

### Calculus of Base $a$ Logarithms

**Differentiation of $\log_a u$**
- Derive the derivative of $\log_a u$ for a positive differentiable function $u(x)$, and state the elementary case $u = x$ as well as the differential form.

**Integration Involving $\log_a x$**

- State the definition of earthquake magnitude $R$ on the Richter scale, defining each variable in the formula.

## 6.6 L'Hôpital's Rule

### Historical Background and Origins

- Describe the historical origins of l'Hôpital's Rule, including the respective roles of Johann Bernoulli and Guillaume de l'Hôpital, their 1694 agreement, the 1696 calculus textbook, and the 1721 priority dispute.

### Indeterminate Quotients

**The Nature of Indeterminate Forms**

- Define an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$ for a quotient $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$, and explain why the Limit Quotient Rule cannot be applied.
- Explain what the term *indeterminate* means in terms of the competing limiting behaviors of the numerator and denominator.

**The Foundation of Differential Calculus as an Indeterminate Form**

- Explain why the limit definition of the derivative $f'(a)$ is universally an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$.

**Geometric and Linearization Interpretation**

- Use local linear approximations of $f(x)$ and $g(x)$ near $x = a$ to provide a geometric justification for why $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ is governed by the ratio of derivatives at $x = a$.

### L'Hôpital's Rule (First Form)

**Pointwise Derivative Formulation**

- State and prove Theorem 2: L'Hôpital's Rule (First Form), specifying all required pointwise hypotheses at $x = a$.
- Contrast the expression differentiated in l'Hôpital's Rule with the derivative of a quotient $\frac{d}{dx}\left[\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right]$.

**Theoretical Limitations of the First Form**

- Identify the three primary theoretical limitations of the First Form of l'Hôpital's Rule that motivate a stronger formulation.

### Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem

- State and prove Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem on $[a, b]$, including the additional hypothesis required to express the conclusion as a ratio of derivatives and why that hypothesis prevents division by zero.
- Interpret Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem geometrically in terms of a parametric curve in the plane.

### L'Hôpital's Rule (Stronger Form)

**Formulation for the Indeterminate Form $\frac{0}{0}$**

- State Theorem 3: L'Hôpital's Rule (Stronger Form) for indeterminate forms of type $\frac{0}{0}$, specifying all continuity and differentiability hypotheses on an open interval $I$ containing $a$.
- Explain why continuity at $x = a$ must be explicitly stated when differentiability at $x = a$ is relaxed, how introductory textbooks simplify this hypothesis, and how continuous extensions handle functions defined only on $I \setminus \{a\}$.
- Prove Theorem 3 for both finite and infinite limits by establishing the right-hand and left-hand limits.

**Repeated Application (Iteration) of the Rule**

- Explain how and under what conditions the Stronger Form of l'Hôpital's Rule can be iterated when the first derivative ratio remains indeterminate, and state when the iteration must stop.

### The Indeterminate Form $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$

**Theoretical Extension to Infinite Quotients**

- Define an indeterminate form of type $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$.
- State and prove L'Hôpital's Rule for indeterminate forms of type $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$.

### Limits at Infinity ($x \to \pm\infty$)

- State and prove L'Hôpital's Rule for limits at infinity ($x \to \pm\infty$) for both $\frac{0}{0}$ and $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ forms.

### Other Indeterminate Forms

**Indeterminate Products ($0 \cdot \infty$)**

- Define an indeterminate form of type $0 \cdot \infty$ and explain the competing factors in the product.
- Describe the two algebraic ways to convert an indeterminate product $f(x) \cdot g(x)$ into an indeterminate quotient, and explain the criterion for choosing which factor to invert.

**Indeterminate Differences ($\infty - \infty$)**

- Define an indeterminate form of type $\infty - \infty$, and contrast it with determinate infinite sums and differences.
- Describe the three standard algebraic strategies for converting an $\infty - \infty$ difference into a quotient or product suitable for l'Hôpital's Rule.

**Indeterminate Powers ($1^\infty$, $0^0$, $\infty^0$)**

- Define the three indeterminate power forms $1^\infty$, $0^0$, and $\infty^0$ for $\lim_{x \to a} [f(x)]^{g(x)}$, and explain the competing pulls of the base and exponent in each case.
- Identify which power forms involving $0$ and $\infty$ are determinate rather than indeterminate, and state their limiting values.

**The Method of Logarithmic Transformation**

- Explain how to evaluate an indeterminate power $\lim_{x \to a} [f(x)]^{g(x)}$ using a logarithmic transformation, and identify the indeterminate form produced for $\ln y$ in each of the three cases $1^\infty$, $0^0$, and $\infty^0$.
- State and prove the Logarithmic Limit Inversion Theorem for both finite and infinite limits of $\ln f(x)$.

### Flowchart and Decision Strategy for L'Hôpital's Rule

- Outline the complete step-by-step decision flowchart for evaluating $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ using l'Hôpital's Rule.

### Analytical Cautions, Pitfalls, and Failure Modes

**Over-Differentiation Beyond the Indeterminate Form**

- Explain the error of over-differentiation in l'Hôpital's Rule and state the rule for when to stop differentiating.

**Circularity and Non-Improving Repetition**

- Explain how circularity (infinite loops) can occur when applying l'Hôpital's Rule, and demonstrate how to resolve both trigonometric loops (such as $\lim_{x \to (\pi/2)^-} \frac{\sec x}{\tan x}$) and algebraic radical loops (such as $\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 1}}$).

**Failure of $\lim \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}$ to Exist (Converse Falsity)**

- State the logical direction of l'Hôpital's Rule as a conditional implication, and explain what can and cannot be concluded when $\lim_{x \to a} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}$ fails to exist due to oscillation.
- Explain why the Cauchy Mean Value Theorem relation $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \frac{f'(c_x)}{g'(c_x)}$ does not make the converse of l'Hôpital's Rule true.

**Canonical Counterexamples to the Converse**

- **Example (Oscillatory $\frac{0}{0}$ Form at a Finite Point):** Evaluate $\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ for $f(x) = x^2 \sin\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$ and $g(x) = x$, and explain why l'Hôpital's Rule cannot be applied.
- **Example (Oscillatory $\frac{\infty}{\infty}$ Form at Infinity):** Evaluate $\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}$ for $f(x) = x + \sin x$ and $g(x) = x$, and show that the limit of the derivative ratio does not exist.

### Parametric Curves and the Geometry of Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem

**Definition of a Parametric Curve in the Plane**

- Define a parametric curve in $\mathbb{R}^2$ and express it as a vector-valued position function $\mathbf{r}(t)$.

**Continuity of a Parametric Curve**

- Define continuity of a parametric plane curve $\mathbf{r}(t) = (g(t), f(t))$ at $t_0 \in [a, b]$ in terms of the Euclidean metric and in $(\epsilon, \delta)$-form.
- State and prove both directions of the Componentwise Continuity Theorem for a parametric curve on $[a, b]$, and explain how the proof generalizes to $\mathbb{R}^n$.

**Differentiability of a Parametric Curve**

- Define differentiability of a parametric curve $\mathbf{r}(t) = (g(t), f(t))$ at $t_0 \in (a, b)$ and define the velocity (tangent) vector $\mathbf{r}'(t_0)$.
- State and prove both directions of the Componentwise Differentiability Theorem for a parametric curve on $(a, b)$.

**Geometric Synthesis with Cauchy's Mean Value Theorem**

- Synthesize the secant chord slope and instantaneous tangent slope (both via the parametric chain rule and via differentials) of a plane curve $\mathbf{r}(t) = (g(t), f(t))$ to interpret Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem geometrically.
- Show how the classical Mean Value Theorem for $y = f(x)$ arises as a special case of Cauchy's Extended Mean Value Theorem.

## 6.7 Relative Rates of Growth

### Intuition and Asymptotic Hierarchies

**[Asymptotic Domination at Infinity]**
- Explain the general setup for comparing the long-term behavior of positive functions as $x \to \infty$ and identify which major function family dominates polynomials, power functions, and rational functions.

**[Geometric Scale and Astronomical Growth Rates]**
- Describe the centimeter-scale blackboard thought experiment comparing the vertical growth of $y = e^x$ at $x = 1, 6, 10, 24, 43\text{ cm}$ against the horizontal distance required for $y = \ln x$ to reach the same height, and explain how reflection across the line $y = x$ connects their behaviors.

### Mathematical Definition of Growth Rates

- Define what it means for a positive function $f(x)$ to grow faster than $g(x)$, grow slower than $g(x)$, or grow at the same rate as $g(x)$ as $x \to \infty$.
- Explain why scaling a function by a positive constant does not alter its asymptotic growth rate, illustrating with $f(x) = 2x$ and $g(x) = x$.

### The Fundamental Growth Hierarchy

**[Exponentials Outgrow Polynomial Powers]**
- State and prove the theorem comparing the growth rate of an exponential function $a^x$ ($a > 1$) to any positive integer power $x^n$ as $x \to \infty$.

**[Exponentials with Distinct Bases]**
- State and prove the theorem comparing the growth rates of two exponential functions $a^x$ and $b^x$ with distinct positive bases $a > b > 0$ as $x \to \infty$.

**[Polynomial Powers Outgrow Logarithms]**
- State and prove the theorem comparing the growth rate of the natural logarithm $\ln x$ to any positive real power $x^p$ ($p > 0$) as $x \to \infty$, and explain its implication for arbitrarily small fractional powers of $x$.

**[Equivalence of All Logarithmic Bases]**
- State and prove the Logarithmic Base Invariance Theorem comparing the growth rates of $\log_a x$ and $\log_b x$ for any bases $a, b > 1$ as $x \to \infty$.

**[Transitivity of Growth Rates]**
- State and prove the Transitivity of Growth Rate Equivalence theorem for three positive functions $f(x)$, $g(x)$, and $h(x)$ as $x \to \infty$.
- Explain the practical utility of transitivity when comparing two complicated functions against a standard benchmark function.
- Show that $f(x) = \sqrt{x^2 + 5}$ and $h(x) = (2\sqrt{x} - 1)^2$ grow at the same rate as $x \to \infty$ by comparing both to a common benchmark function.

### Asymptotic Notation: Little-oh and Big-oh

**[Origins and Mathematical Significance]**
- Identify the mathematicians who introduced the Landau symbols ($o$ and $O$), the historical period of their introduction, and their original mathematical purpose.

**[Little-oh Notation]**
- Define the asymptotic relation $f(x) = o(g(x))$ as $x \to \infty$ and state its meaning in terms of relative growth rates.

**[Big-oh Notation]**
- Define the asymptotic relation $f(x) = O(g(x))$ as $x \to \infty$ and explain its geometric interpretation for sufficiently large $x$.

**[Theoretical Relationships Between Orders of Growth]**
- Prove that $f = o(g)$ implies $f = O(g)$ as $x \to \infty$, and provide a counterexample demonstrating that the converse is false.
- Explain why two functions $f$ and $g$ that grow at the same rate as $x \to \infty$ satisfy both $f = O(g)$ and $g = O(f)$.

### Algorithmic Complexity: Sequential vs. Binary Search

**[Computational Step Counting as Asymptotic Growth]**
- Explain how algorithm efficiency is quantified in terms of input size $n$ as $n \to \infty$.

**[Sequential Search]**
- Describe the procedure of a sequential search on an ordered list of $n$ items, state its worst-case step count and big-oh complexity, and determine the maximum number of comparisons required for a list of $n = 26,000$ entries.

**[Binary Search]**
- Describe the bisection procedure of a binary search on an ordered list of $n$ items.

**[Mathematical Derivation of Binary Search Complexity]**
- Derive the worst-case bound in terms of the ceiling function for the maximum number of bisections $m$ required by binary search on an ordered list of length $n$, and state the big-oh time complexity of both sequential search and binary search.

**[Asymptotic Comparison of Efficiencies]**
- Compute the maximum number of comparisons required by binary search for an ordered list of $n = 26,000$ entries, and prove asymptotically that binary search is of smaller order than sequential search as $n \to \infty$.

**[Circular Radians and Arc Length Equivalence]**

### Right-Triangle Trigonometry and Algebraic Inversion

**The Derivative of Arccosecant: $y = \csc^{-1} u$**

**Standard Integration Formulas (Table 6.6)**
- State the three standard indefinite integral formulas evaluated with $\sin^{-1}$, $\tan^{-1}$, and $\sec^{-1}$ for a constant $a > 0$, along with the domain condition on $u$ for each formula.

### Systematic Integration Strategies and Algebraic Reductions

**Strategy 1: Linear Scaling and Substitution ($u = kx$)**
- Derive the scaled integration formulas for $\int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2 x^2}}$, $\int \frac{dx}{a^2 + b^2 x^2}$, and $\int \frac{dx}{x\sqrt{b^2 x^2 - a^2}}$ ($a, b > 0$), and explain why the external factor of $b$ cancels in the arcsecant case.

**Strategy 2: The Method of Completing the Square**
- Derive the general completed-square identity for a quadratic polynomial $Ax^2 + Bx + C$ ($A \neq 0$).
- Classify and reduce the three canonical quadratic integral forms—Form I involving $\sqrt{Bx - x^2}$, Form II involving an irreducible quadratic denominator $Ax^2 + Bx + C$ (stating the discriminant condition), and Form III involving $(x - h)\sqrt{(x - h)^2 - a^2}$—to the three standard inverse trigonometric integrals.

**Strategy 3: Nonlinear and Transcendental Substitutions**
- Determine the appropriate nonlinear substitutions and evaluate the canonical integral forms $\int \frac{dx}{\sqrt{e^{2x} - a^2}}$, $\int \frac{x^{k-1}}{a^2 + x^{2k}} \, dx$, $\int \frac{e^{\tan^{-1} x}}{1 + x^2} \, dx$, and $\int \frac{\cos x}{a^2 + \sin^2 x} \, dx$.

**Definite Integrals and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus**
- Explain how the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus and the change-of-limits rule for $u$-substitution streamline the evaluation of definite integrals involving inverse trigonometric antiderivatives.

## 6.10 Hyperbolic Functions

### Genesis, Motivation, and the Geometric Analogy

**Historical Origin and Mathematical Discovery**
- Describe Galileo's conjecture regarding a freely suspended heavy chain, identify who disproved it, and state the governing differential equation and equilibrium curve of the catenary.
- Summarize the historical contributions of Vincenzo Riccati and Johann Heinrich Lambert to the development of hyperbolic trigonometry.

**Why Are Hyperbolic Functions Named After Trigonometric Functions?**
- Explain how the unit circle is parametrized in terms of circular sector area.
- State and prove the theorem relating the hyperbolic parametrization of the right branch of the unit equilateral hyperbola to the area of the corresponding hyperbolic sector.
- Evaluate the complex circular functions $\cos z$ and $\sin z$ at an imaginary argument $z = ix$ to derive the exact analytic relations between circular and hyperbolic functions.
- State Osborn's Rule for converting circular trigonometric identities into hyperbolic identities, and explain the algebraic reason for the sign change.

**The Even-Odd Algebraic Decomposition of $e^x$**
- State and prove the theorem on the existence and uniqueness of decomposing a function on a symmetric domain into the sum of an even function and an odd function.
- Apply the even-odd decomposition to $e^x$ to motivate the definitions of $\cosh x$ and $\sinh x$.

### Definitions and Fundamental Algebraic Identities

- Define all six basic hyperbolic functions ($\cosh x$, $\sinh x$, $\tanh x$, $\coth x$, $\operatorname{sech} x$, and $\operatorname{csch} x$) in terms of exponential functions, specifying their domains and standard pronunciation conventions.

**Rigorous Proofs of the Hyperbolic Algebraic Identities**
- State and prove the fundamental hyperbolic identity relating $\cosh^2 x$ and $\sinh^2 x$.
- State and prove the two derived Pythagorean identities relating $\tanh x$ to $\operatorname{sech} x$ and $\coth x$ to $\operatorname{csch} x$.
- State and prove the addition and subtraction formulas for $\sinh(x \pm y)$, $\cosh(x \pm y)$, and $\tanh(x \pm y)$, and contrast their sign patterns with circular trigonometry.
- State and prove the double-argument formulas for $\sinh(2x)$ (one form), $\cosh(2x)$ (three forms), and $\tanh(2x)$.
- State and prove the power-reduction (half-argument) formulas for $\cosh^2 x$ and $\sinh^2 x$.

### Geometric Profiles, Parity, and Asymptotic Behaviors

- Construct the summary table specifying the domain, range, parity, zeroes, and vertical or horizontal asymptotes for all six hyperbolic functions.

**Analytical Properties and Limiting Behavior**
- Analyze the parity, sign, monotonicity, concavity, extrema, inflection points, and end-behavior limits for $y = \sinh x$, $y = \cosh x$, $y = \tanh x$, $y = \coth x$, $y = \operatorname{sech} x$, and $y = \operatorname{csch} x$, and sketch their graphs.

### Differential and Integral Calculus of Hyperbolic Functions

**Derivatives of Hyperbolic Functions**
- State the differentiation formulas for all six hyperbolic functions ($\sinh u$, $\cosh u$, $\tanh u$, $\coth u$, $\operatorname{sech} u$, and $\operatorname{csch} u$) along with their domain conditions.
- Compare the signs of the derivatives of the six hyperbolic functions with those of the six circular trigonometric functions.
- Prove each of the six hyperbolic differentiation formulas from first definitions and algebraic identities.

**Integral Formulas for Hyperbolic Functions**
- State the six basic indefinite integral formulas corresponding to the derivatives of the six hyperbolic functions.
- Derive the indefinite integrals $\int \tanh u \, du$ and $\int \coth u \, du$.

### Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

**Invertibility, Monotonicity, and Domain Restrictions**
- Justify the invertibility of $\sinh x$, $\tanh x$, $\coth x$, and $\operatorname{csch} x$ on their natural domains, explain why $\cosh x$ and $\operatorname{sech} x$ require principal domain restrictions, and state the domain and range of all six inverse hyperbolic functions alongside their graphs.

**Reciprocal Identities for Inverse Hyperbolic Functions**
- State and prove the three reciprocal identities relating $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$, $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$, and $\coth^{-1} x$ to $\cosh^{-1}(1/x)$, $\sinh^{-1}(1/x)$, and $\tanh^{-1}(1/x)$, including all domain conditions.

**Explicit Logarithmic Representations of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions**
- State the explicit logarithmic formulas and domain conditions for all six inverse hyperbolic functions ($\sinh^{-1} x$, $\cosh^{-1} x$, $\tanh^{-1} x$, $\coth^{-1} x$, $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$, and $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$).
- Prove the explicit logarithmic formula for $\sinh^{-1} x$, justifying why only one root of the intermediate equation is valid.
- Prove the explicit logarithmic formula for $\cosh^{-1} x$, justifying the choice of sign using the principal branch restriction.
- Prove the explicit logarithmic formula for $\tanh^{-1} x$.
- Derive the explicit logarithmic formulas for $\coth^{-1} x$, $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$, and $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$.

### Differential Calculus of Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

- State the differentiation formulas and domain conditions for all six inverse hyperbolic functions ($\sinh^{-1} u$, $\cosh^{-1} u$, $\tanh^{-1} u$, $\coth^{-1} u$, $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} u$, and $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} u$).

**Step-by-Step Proofs via Implicit Differentiation**
- Prove the derivative formulas for $\sinh^{-1} x$, $\cosh^{-1} x$, $\tanh^{-1} x$, $\coth^{-1} x$, $\operatorname{sech}^{-1} x$, and $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$ via implicit differentiation, justifying the sign of every radical and explaining the appearance of $|x|$ in the derivative of $\operatorname{csch}^{-1} x$.
- Compare the algebraic forms of $\frac{d}{dx}(\tanh^{-1} x)$ and $\frac{d}{dx}(\coth^{-1} x)$, and explain how their domains distinguish them during integration.

**Alternative Proofs by Differentiating the Logarithmic Forms**
- Verify the derivative formulas for $\sinh^{-1} x$ and $\tanh^{-1} x$ by directly differentiating their explicit logarithmic representations.

### Integrals Leading to Inverse Hyperbolic Functions

- State all five standard indefinite integral formulas that evaluate to inverse hyperbolic functions (and their equivalent logarithmic forms), including all parameter and domain conditions for $a > 0$.

**Step-by-Step Derivations of the Integral Formulas**
- Derive the indefinite integral formulas for $\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}}$ and $\int \frac{du}{\sqrt{u^2 - a^2}}$ in both inverse hyperbolic and logarithmic forms.
- Derive the indefinite integral formula for $\int \frac{du}{a^2 - u^2}$ in terms of $\tanh^{-1}(u/a)$ and $\coth^{-1}(u/a)$ according to the domain of $u$, and prove the unified logarithmic formula via partial fractions.
- Derive the indefinite integral formulas for $\int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 - u^2}}$ and $\int \frac{du}{u\sqrt{a^2 + u^2}}$.

## 6.11 First Order Differential Equations

### Mathematical Foundations of First-Order Equations

**Formal Definitions: Equation, Solution, and Initial Value Problem**
- Define a first-order differential equation in derivative notation, prime notation, and symmetrical differential form.
- Define a solution $y = \phi(x)$ of a first-order differential equation on an interval $I \subseteq \mathbb{R}$, stating all three required conditions.
- Distinguish between an explicit solution and an implicit solution on an interval $I$.
- Define an initial value problem (IVP) and the initial condition.

**Geometric Interpretation: Direction Fields and Integral Curves**
- Explain the geometric meaning of a direction field (slope field) and an integral curve for a first-order differential equation.
- Explain what the general solution represents geometrically and how imposing an initial condition isolates a specific trajectory.

**The Verification Protocol for Candidate Solutions**
- State the two-part analytical protocol for verifying that a candidate function $y = \phi(x)$ solves a given initial value problem on an interval $I$.

### Separable First-Order Differential Equations

**Theoretical Criteria for Separability**
- Define what it means for a first-order differential equation to be separable, including the continuity hypotheses on its factor functions.

**Rigorous Mathematical Derivation of the Separation Technique**
- Derive and justify the method of separation of variables rigorously from the Chain Rule and the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus, progressing from reciprocal division through definite integration and change of variables to the indefinite integral relation.

**Equilibrium and Singular Solutions**
- Explain how equilibrium (stationary) constant solutions arise in a separable differential equation and why dividing by the $y$-dependent factor can cause these valid solutions to be lost from the general family.

### First-Order Linear Differential Equations

**Definition and the Canonical Standard Form**
- Define a general first-order linear differential equation and state how to reduce it to canonical standard form, specifying the coefficient functions $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$.
- State the three structural rules governing the canonical standard form regarding the leading coefficient, the algebraic sign convention for $P(x)$, and the classification into homogeneous versus non-homogeneous equations.

**The Theory of the Integrating Factor**
- Explain the motivating purpose of introducing an auxiliary multiplying function (integrating factor) $v(x)$ when solving a first-order linear differential equation in standard form.

**Rigorous Step-by-Step Derivation of the Integrating Factor $v(x)$**
- State the product-rule condition that the integrating factor $v(x)$ must satisfy, derive the resulting separable differential equation for $v(x)$, and solve it to obtain the general family of integrating factors.
- Explain why we may set the integration constant to zero and select a single canonical integrating factor $v(x)$ rather than keeping the arbitrary constant.

**Derivation of the General Solution Formula**
- Using the canonical integrating factor $v(x)$, derive the explicit general solution formula for a first-order linear differential equation in standard form.

**The Master Theorem**
- State and prove Theorem 4 (Solution of Linear First Order Differential Equations), including the continuity hypotheses on $P(x)$ and $Q(x)$.
- Explain how the constant of integration $C$ interacts with the reciprocal factor $\frac{1}{v(x)}$ in the general solution, and identify the common algebraic error to avoid when placing $C$.

**Structural Decomposition: Homogeneous and Particular Components**
- Decompose the general solution of a first-order linear differential equation into its homogeneous component $y_h(x)$ and particular component $y_p(x)$, and explain how the principle of linear superposition relates them.

**The Canonical 4-Step Solution Algorithm**
- State the 4-step operational algorithm for solving a first-order linear differential equation from initial inspection to final evaluation of the solution and initial condition.

## 6.12 Euler's Numerical Method; Slope Fields

### Qualitative and Numerical Foundations of Differential Equations

**The Necessity of Numerical and Qualitative Analysis**
- Explain why closed-form analytical solutions to first-order initial value problems $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$, $y(x_0) = y_0$ are frequently unavailable, and provide concrete examples of non-elementary first-order differential equations.
- Compare the objectives and methods of qualitative analysis versus quantitative numerical analysis for differential equations.

**Formal Definitions: Numerical Solution and Numerical Method**
- Define a numerical solution to an initial value problem on a closed interval $[x_0, b]$.
- Define a numerical method and distinguish it from the numerical solution it produces.

### Slope Fields (Direction Fields)

**Geometric Definition and Construction of Direction Fields**
- Define the slope field (direction field) and a lineal (tangent) element associated with the differential equation $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ on a domain $D \subseteq \mathbb{R}^2$.
- Explain the geometric relationship between the lineal elements of a slope field and the integral curves of the differential equation.

**Graphical Portrayal: Vector Renditions versus Scalar Slopes**
- Explain why slope segments in a direction field possess no intrinsic direction or vector polarity even when computer plotting software renders them with arrowheads.

**Qualitative Analysis of Integral Curves**
- Describe how to trace particular solution trajectories through a slope field starting from an initial condition $(x_0, y_0)$.
- Define nullclines and general isoclines of a first-order differential equation, and explain their geometric meaning.
- Identify the long-term qualitative behaviors of solution families that can be deduced directly from inspecting a slope field.

### Tangent Line Linearization and Euler's Stepping Mechanism

**The Principle of Local Linearization**
- Derive the local tangent line linearization $L(x)$ of an unknown solution $y(x)$ to $\frac{dy}{dx} = f(x, y)$ at the initial point $(x_0, y_0)$.

**Discrete Propagation: Stepping by Increment $dx$**
- Formulate the first step of Euler's method to obtain $(x_1, y_1)$ and explain geometrically why $(x_1, y_1)$ generally lies off the true solution curve.
- Explain how the slope is chosen for the second and subsequent steps once the approximated points have drifted off the exact solution curve.

**Euler's Polygonal Path**
- Describe the geometric structure of Euler's polygonal path and explain why discrepancy from the true integral curve accumulates over successive steps.

### The Formal Euler Method Algorithm

**Formulation for Uniform Partitions**
- State the formula for the uniform step size $dx$ and both the recursive and closed-form expressions for the mesh nodes $x_k$ on $[x_0, b]$.
- State the general Euler recurrence relation for advancing from $(x_k, y_k)$ to $(x_{k+1}, y_{k+1})$.

**Finite Difference Interpretation**
- Derive Euler's stepping recurrence directly from the limit definition of the derivative using a forward finite difference quotient.

### Mathematical Error Analysis and Convergence

**Local Truncation Error (LTE)**
- Define the local truncation error $e_{\text{local}}$ of a single step in Euler's method.
- State and prove the theorem giving the exact expression and asymptotic order of the local truncation error for Euler's method.

**Global Truncation Error (GTE) and Cumulative Error Propagation**
- Define the global truncation error $E_{\text{global}}$ at the terminal endpoint $x_n = b$.
- Derive the asymptotic order of the global truncation error across $[x_0, b]$ from the single-step local truncation error and the total step count $n$.
- State the formal order classification of Euler's method, contrasting its local and global error orders.

**The Step-Halving Rule of First-Order Methods**
- State the step-halving rule for a first-order numerical method and explain how halving the step size $dx$ affects both the total number of steps and the terminal global error.

**Geometric Curvature and Systematic Error Bias**
- Determine how the concavity $y''(x)$ of the exact solution curve governs whether Euler's method systematically overestimates or underestimates the true solution, and explain what happens at points of inflection.

**Truncation Error versus Floating-Point Round-Off Error**
- Compare the sources and step-size scaling of discretization (truncation) error and floating-point round-off error in finite-precision computation.

**The Optimal Step Size Phenomenon**
- Explain why an optimal step size $dx_{\text{optimal}}$ exists in finite-precision arithmetic and why shrinking $dx$ indefinitely eventually degrades numerical accuracy.

### Synthesis and Comparative Perspective

**Euler's Method as the Foundational Prototype**
- Explain the slope-sampling mechanisms of the Improved Euler Method (Heun's method) and the Classical Fourth-Order Runge-Kutta Method (RK4), and state how each improves upon the global convergence order of Euler's method.

**Comparative Summary of Numerical ODE Methods**
- Compare Euler's Method, the Improved Euler Method (Heun), and the Fourth-Order Runge-Kutta Method (RK4) across their method order, local truncation error order, global truncation error order, number of slope evaluations per step, and geometric mechanism.
